# Mathematical Methods — Answers

Questions: [questions.md](questions.md). Answer numbers match question numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Functions and relations

#### Linear functions and coordinate geometry

**A1.** A. −2
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-1 -->

Explanation: Gradient = (−7 − 5)/(4 − (−2)) = −12/6 = −2. The value 2 drops the sign; ±1/2 inverts the ratio.

**A2.** D. y = −x/3 + 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-2 -->

Explanation: The perpendicular gradient is −1/3. Then y − 1 = −(1/3)(x − 6), so y = −x/3 + 3. y = 3x − 17 is the parallel line through (6, 1); y = −3x + 19 only negates the gradient rather than taking the negative reciprocal.

**A3.** D. (2, 3)
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-3 -->

Explanation: Midpoint = ((−3 + 7)/2, (8 + (−2))/2) = (2, 3).

**A4.** B. 10
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-4 -->

Explanation: d = √((7 − 1)² + (6 − (−2))²) = √(36 + 64) = √100 = 10. Forgetting the square root gives 100.

**A5.** B. −3
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-5 -->

Explanation: Gradients: −2/k and 6/9 = 2/3. Setting −2/k = 2/3 gives k = −3. Check: 2x − 3y = 5 has gradient 2/3.

**A6.** Midpoint of AB = (2, 4) [1]; gradient of AB = 4/6 = 2/3, so perpendicular gradient = −3/2 [1]; y − 4 = −(3/2)(x − 2), giving y = −3x/2 + 7, or 3x + 2y = 14 [1]. Check: (2, 4) satisfies 3(2) + 2(4) = 14.
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-6 -->

Explanation:

**A7.** From the second equation y = 5x − 7 [1]; substitute: 3x + 10x − 14 = 12, so 13x = 26, x = 2 and y = 3 [1]; the solution (2, 3) is the point of intersection of the two lines [1]. Check: 3(2) + 2(3) = 12 and 5(2) − 3 = 7.
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-7 -->

Explanation:

**A8.** (a) Gradient 2.2: the cost increases by $2.20 for each extra kilometre; vertical intercept 4.5: the fixed charge of $4.50 at the start of the trip (d = 0) [1 for both]. (b) 26.5 = 4.5 + 2.2d [1]; 2.2d = 22, so d = 10 km [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-8 -->

Explanation:

**A9.** A. 3/4
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-9 -->

Explanation: Rearranging gives y = (3/4)x − 3, so the gradient is 3/4. The value 4/3 inverts the ratio, and −3/4 loses a sign when dividing by −4.

**A10.** B. 10
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-10 -->

Explanation: Put x = 0: 2y = 20, so y = 10. The value 4 is the x-intercept.

**A11.** D. y = 2x − 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-11 -->

Explanation: Gradient = (5 − (−3))/4 = 2 and the y-intercept is −3, so y = 2x − 3. Using x/2 inverts the gradient.

**A12.** C. 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-12 -->

Explanation: Set y = 0: 3x = 12, so x = 4. The value 12 is the y-intercept.

**A13.** B. 13
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-13 -->

Explanation: d = √((2 − (−3))² + (11 − (−1))²) = √(25 + 144) = √169 = 13. √119 comes from subtracting the squares, and 169 forgets the square root.

**A14.** C. (5, 1)
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-14 -->

Explanation: B = 2M − A = (2 − (−3), 8 − 7) = (5, 1). Averaging A and M gives (−1, 5.5), which is not B.

**A15.** D. (2, 5)
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-15 -->

Explanation: Setting 2x + 1 = −x + 7 gives x = 2, then y = 5. (3, 4) lies on y = −x + 7 but not on y = 2x + 1.

**A16.** C. 3/2
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-16 -->

Explanation: The given line has gradient −2/3, so the perpendicular gradient is the negative reciprocal, 3/2. −2/3 is the original gradient and −3/2 only inverts.

**A17.** A. y = −x/2 − 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-17 -->

Explanation: Parallel lines share gradient −1/2: −3 = −2 + c gives c = −1. y = 2x − 11 is the perpendicular line through (4, −3).

**A18.** B. 5
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-18 -->

Explanation: The gradient from (2, 1) to (8, 13) is 12/6 = 2, so k = 1 + 2 × 2 = 5. Using k = 4 or 6 does not give a gradient of 2 to both points.

**A19.** D. √3
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-19 -->

Explanation: Gradient = tan 60° = √3. 1/√3 is tan 30°, and 60 confuses the angle with the gradient.

**A20.** D. Vertical, undefined gradient
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-20 -->

Explanation: Every point has x = 4, so the line is vertical and its gradient is undefined. A horizontal line has the form y = c.

**A21.** A. 13
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-21 -->

Explanation: Gradient = −12/4 = −3, so y − 7 = −3(x − 2) gives y = −3x + 13. The value 1 comes from adding −3 × 2 instead of subtracting it.

**A22.** B. 24
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-22 -->

Explanation: It is a right triangle with base 6 and height 8: area = ½ × 6 × 8 = 24. 48 forgets the ½.

**A23.** A. (3, −1)
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-23 -->

Explanation: Substituting x = 3 gives y = −6 + 5 = −1. (3, 1) has the wrong sign for y.

**A24.** C. Infinitely many solutions
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-24 -->

Explanation: The second equation rearranges to y = 3x + 2, the same line, so every point on it is a solution. Parallel but distinct lines would give none.

**A25.** A. 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-25 -->

Explanation: The given gradient is −2, so the required gradient is 1/2: (k − 1)/2 = 1/2, so k = 2. k = −3 comes from using the parallel gradient −2.

**A26.** D. y = 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-26 -->

Explanation: Both points have y = 3, so the line is horizontal with equation y = 3. x = 3 would be a vertical line.

**A27.** Gradient = (8 − (−1))/(5 − 2) = 9/3 = 3 [1]; y + 1 = 3(x − 2), so y = 3x − 7 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-27 -->

Explanation:

**A28.** (a) AB = √((5 − (−4))² + (−9 − 3)²) = √(81 + 144) [1] = √225 = 15 [1]. (b) Midpoint = ((−4 + 5)/2, (3 + (−9))/2) = (1/2, −3) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-28 -->

Explanation:

**A29.** Gradient of AB = 2/4 = 1/2 [1]; gradient of AC = 2/(−1) = −2 [1]; the product is −1, so AB ⊥ AC and the angle at A is 90° [1]. AB = √20, AC = √5, so area = ½ × √20 × √5 = ½ × 10 = 5 square units [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-29 -->

Explanation:

**A30.** Substitute y = 2x − 1: 3x + 2(2x − 1) = 12 [1]; 7x − 2 = 12, so x = 2 [1]; y = 2(2) − 1 = 3, so the intersection is (2, 3) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-30 -->

Explanation:

**A31.** (10 − k)/(4 − 1) = 3 [1]; 10 − k = 9, so k = 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-31 -->

Explanation:

**A32.** Rearrange: y = 2x − 5/2, so the gradient is 2 [1]; parallel line has gradient 2, so y + 2 = 2(x − 3) [1]; y = 2x − 8 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-32 -->

Explanation:

**A33.** (a) 40 + 8t = 15 + 13t [1], so 25 = 5t and t = 5 hours [1]. (b) C = 40 + 8(5) = $80 [1]. (c) At t = 3: X = $64, Y = $54, so Company Y is cheaper [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-33 -->

Explanation:

**A34.** Put y = 0: 3x = 30, so the x-intercept is (10, 0) [1]; put x = 0: −5y = 30, so the y-intercept is (0, −6) [1]; rearranging gives y = (3/5)x − 6, so the gradient is 3/5 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-34 -->

Explanation:

**A35.** Let the point be (a, 0): (a − 1)² + 9 = (a − 5)² + 1 [1]; a² − 2a + 10 = a² − 10a + 26, so 8a = 16 [1]; a = 2, so the point is (2, 0). Check: both distances are √10 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-35 -->

Explanation:

**A36.** Gradient of AB = (4 − (−2))/(2 − (−1)) = 6/3 = 2 [1]; gradient of BC = (8 − 4)/(4 − 2) = 2; equal gradients with the common point B, so the points are collinear [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-36 -->

Explanation:

**A37.** x: (−2 + x)/2 = 3, so x = 8 [1]; y: (4 + y)/2 = −1, so y = −6, giving Q(8, −6) [1]; PQ = √(10² + (−10)²) = √200 = 10√2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-37 -->

Explanation:

**A38.** y − 1 = −(3/4)(x − 8) [1]; multiply by 4: 4y − 4 = −3x + 24, so 3x + 4y = 28 [1]; put y = 0: x = 28/3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-lin-38 -->

Explanation:

#### Functions, relations, domain and range

**A39.** B. {(1, 2), (3, 4), (1, 5)}
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-1 -->

Explanation: A function gives exactly one output for each input. In the third relation the input 1 maps to both 2 and 5. Different inputs sharing an output (as in the first and last) is allowed.

**A40.** B. [3, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-2 -->

Explanation: We need 2x − 6 ≥ 0, so x ≥ 3. The value x = 3 gives f(3) = 0, which is defined, so the interval is closed at 3.

**A41.** C. [3, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-3 -->

Explanation: Completing the square: f(x) = (x − 2)² + 3, so the minimum value is 3 at x = 2. The range is [3, ∞).

**A42.** D. R \ {3}
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-4 -->

Explanation: The horizontal asymptote is y = 3, and 1/(x − 2) can take every non-zero value, so f never equals 3. Range is R \ {3}. R \ {2} is the domain.

**A43.** C. (−2, 5]
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-5 -->

Explanation: A strict inequality at −2 gives a round bracket and ≤ at 5 gives a square bracket: (−2, 5].

**A44.** (a) f(−2) = 2(4) + 6 = 14 [1]. (b) f(a + 1) = 2(a² + 2a + 1) − 3a − 3 = 2a² + a − 1 [1]. (c) f(a + 1) − f(a) = (2a² + a − 1) − (2a² − 3a) = 4a − 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A45.** (a) Need 9 − x² ≥ 0, so domain = [−3, 3] [1]; the output is a square root so is ≥ 0, with maximum √9 = 3 at x = 0, so range = [0, 3] [1]. (b) x² + y² = 9 is a circle; for example x = 0 gives y = 3 and y = −3 [1], so one input has two outputs and it fails the vertical line test [1]. f is only the upper semicircle.
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A46.** (a) f(−2) = 1; f(1) = 1; f(3) = 9 [1 for all three]. (b) For x < 1, x + 3 takes values in (−∞, 4) [1]; for x ≥ 1, x² takes values in [1, ∞) [1]; the union covers every real number, so range = R [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A47.** B. R \ {−4}
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-9 -->

Explanation: The denominator is zero when x + 4 = 0, so x = −4 must be excluded. Excluding 4 comes from sign confusion.

**A48.** C. [−2, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-10 -->

Explanation: √x takes every value in [0, ∞), so subtracting 2 gives [−2, ∞). The minimum −2 is reached at x = 0, so the end is included.

**A49.** D. (−∞, 8]
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-11 -->

Explanation: Since x² ≥ 0, 2x² ≥ 0 and so f(x) ≤ 8, with 8 reached at x = 0. The negative coefficient means the parabola opens downwards, so [8, ∞) is wrong.

**A50.** A. [−4, 11]
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-12 -->

Explanation: f is increasing, so the range runs from f(−1) = −4 to f(4) = 11. [−1, 4] is the domain, not the range.

**A51.** C. y = |x|
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-13 -->

Explanation: Each x gives exactly one value of |x|. The other relations give two y-values for some x (for example y² = x with x = 4 gives y = ±2) so they fail the vertical line test.

**A52.** B. [1, ∞) \ {5}
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-14 -->

Explanation: The square root needs x ≥ 1, and the denominator is zero at x = 5, which lies in [1, ∞) so must be removed. At x = 1 the numerator is 0 and f is defined, so 1 is included.

**A53.** A. f(x) = x³
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-15 -->

Explanation: x³ is strictly increasing, so each output comes from exactly one input. The others give the same output for x and −x, so a horizontal line cuts them twice.

**A54.** D. (−∞, −1) ∪ [3, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-16 -->

Explanation: The strict inequality at −1 uses a round bracket and ≥ at 3 uses a square bracket. Two separate pieces are joined with a union, not an intersection.

**A55.** B. 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-17 -->

Explanation: f(−2) = (−2)² − 3 = 4 − 3 = 1. Getting −7 comes from treating (−2)² as −4.

**A56.** A. [1, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-18 -->

Explanation: |x − 3| ≥ 0 with minimum 0 at x = 3, so f(x) ≥ 1. The value 3 is the x-coordinate of the turning point, not the minimum value.

**A57.** C. (−∞, 4)
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-19 -->

Explanation: We need 4 − x > 0, so x < 4. The value x = 4 is excluded because it makes the denominator zero, even though √0 is defined on its own.

**A58.** A. 5
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-20 -->

Explanation: f(−1) = −1 (first rule) and f(3) = 9 − 3 = 6 (second rule), so the sum is 5. Using 2x + 1 for x = 3 gives 7 and a total of 6, which is the wrong branch.

**A59.** B. [0, 9)
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-21 -->

Explanation: The minimum 0 occurs at x = 0 (included). The largest values approach 3² = 9 but x = 3 is excluded, so 9 is not reached. The value f(−2) = 4 is not the maximum.

**A60.** D. range
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-22 -->

Explanation: The range is the set of outputs (y-values) actually produced. The domain is the set of allowed inputs.

**A61.** C. many-to-one
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-23 -->

Explanation: Different inputs such as 2 and −2 give the same output 4, but each input gives only one output, so it is many-to-one and a function.

**A62.** A. (−∞, −2] ∪ [2, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-24 -->

Explanation: We need x² ≥ 4, so x ≤ −2 or x ≥ 2. [−2, 2] is where x² − 4 is negative. The endpoints are included because √0 = 0 is defined.

**A63.** D. [−4, 5]
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-25 -->

Explanation: f(x) = (x − 3)² − 4, so the minimum is −4 at x = 3. The endpoints give f(0) = 5 and f(5) = 0, so the maximum is 5 and the range is [−4, 5]. [−4, ∞) ignores the restricted domain.

**A64.** C. (−∞, 3]
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-26 -->

Explanation: √(x + 2) ≥ 0, so f(x) ≤ 3 with equality at x = −2. The reflection in the x-axis makes the range go downwards, so [3, ∞) is wrong.

**A65.** (a) x + 2 ≠ 0, so domain = R \ {−2} [1]. (b) 3/(x + 2) never equals 0, so g(x) never equals −1; range = R \ {−1} [1]. (c) Vertical asymptote x = −2 [1]; horizontal asymptote y = −1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-27 -->

Explanation:

**A66.** (−∞, −3] ∪ (2, ∞) [1]. The numbers not in the set are (−3, 2] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-28 -->

Explanation:

**A67.** (a) f(x) = (x + 1)² − 9, so the minimum is −9 and range = [−9, ∞) [1][1]. (b) The vertex x = −1 is outside [0, 3], so f is increasing on the domain [1]; f(0) = −8 and f(3) = 7, so range = [−8, 7] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-29 -->

Explanation:

**A68.** (a) When x = 5, y² = 4 so y = 2 or y = −2 [1]; one input gives two outputs, so a vertical line cuts the graph twice and it is not a function [1]. (b) Domain = [1, ∞) and range = [0, ∞) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-30 -->

Explanation:

**A69.** (a) Length = 20 − w, so A(w) = w(20 − w) = 20w − w² [1]. (b) Both side lengths must be positive, so domain = (0, 20) [1]. The maximum is A(10) = 100 and A approaches 0 at the ends, so range = (0, 100] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-31 -->

Explanation:

**A70.** (a) f(x) = 2x − 4 for x ≥ 2 and f(x) = 4 − 2x for x < 2 [1]. (b) Domain = R [1]; absolute value is non-negative and 0 is reached at x = 2, so range = [0, ∞) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-32 -->

Explanation:

**A71.** f is decreasing, so the largest value is f(−2) = 5, included [1]. The smallest value approaches f(5) = −2 but is not reached as x = 5 is excluded, so range = (−2, 5] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-33 -->

Explanation:

**A72.** (a) f(0) = 0 and f(2) = 0, so two different inputs give the same output [1]; equivalently a horizontal line such as y = 0 cuts the graph twice [1]. (b) The turning point is at x = 1, so restricting to [1, ∞) makes f one-to-one [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-34 -->

Explanation:

**A73.** D. [−6, 3]
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-35 -->

Explanation: The vertex is (2, 3), the maximum, and the endpoint values are f(0) = −1 and f(5) = −6, so the lowest value is −6. Using f(0) as the minimum overlooks that the graph is lower at x = 5.

**A74.** f(x) = (x − 1)² − 1 so the minimum is f(1) = −1 [1]. f(−2) = 8 is included in the domain and exceeds the limiting value f(3) = 3 [1]. Range [−1, 8] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-fdr-36 -->

Explanation:

#### Quadratic functions

**A75.** B. (3, −5)
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-1 -->

Explanation: Complete the square: y = (x − 3)² − 5, so the vertex is (3, −5). Check: 9 − 18 + 4 = −5.

**A76.** B. k > 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-2 -->

Explanation: Discriminant Δ = 16 − 8k. No real solutions requires Δ < 0, so 8k > 16 and k > 2.

**A77.** A. x = 7 or x = −2
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-3 -->

Explanation: x² − 5x − 14 = (x − 7)(x + 2) = 0, so x = 7 or x = −2. The second option has the signs reversed (it would give +5x).

**A78.** D. y = 2(x − 2)² − 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-4 -->

Explanation: Use y = a(x − 2)² − 3. At x = 0: 4a − 3 = 5, so a = 2. The first option also passes through (0, 5) but has its vertex at (−2, −3).

**A79.** A. 9
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-5 -->

Explanation: y = −(x − 4)² + 9, so the maximum is 9 when x = 4. (Roots 1 and 7 give axis x = 4; y(4) = −16 + 32 − 7 = 9.)

**A80.** Quadratic formula: x = (−2 ± √(4 + 48))/6 [1] = (−2 ± √52)/6 = (−2 ± 2√13)/6 = (−1 ± √13)/3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-6 -->

Explanation:

**A81.** y-intercept: (0, −5) [1]. x-intercepts: (x − 5)(x + 1) = 0, so (5, 0) and (−1, 0) [1]. Turning point: y = (x − 2)² − 9, so minimum at (2, −9) [1]. Upward-opening parabola through these points, symmetric about x = 2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-7 -->

Explanation:

**A82.** Set x² + 1 = 2x + k, so x² − 2x + (1 − k) = 0 [1]. Discriminant Δ = 4 − 4(1 − k) = 4k. (a) Tangent when Δ = 0, so k = 0 [1] (touching at (1, 2)). (b) Two intersections when Δ > 0, so k > 0 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-8 -->

Explanation:

**A83.** A. x = −5
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-9 -->

Explanation: The axis is x = −b/(2a) = −10/2 = −5. The option x = 5 forgets the negative sign.

**A84.** B. 13
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-10 -->

Explanation: Put x = 0: 2(−3)² − 5 = 18 − 5 = 13. The value 18 forgets to subtract 5.

**A85.** C. x = ½ or x = 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-11 -->

Explanation: 2x² − 7x + 3 = (2x − 1)(x − 3) = 0, so x = ½ or x = 3. The factor 2x − 1 gives x = ½, not x = 2.

**A86.** D. −56
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-12 -->

Explanation: Δ = b² − 4ac = 4 − 4(3)(5) = 4 − 60 = −56. The value 64 comes from adding 4ac instead of subtracting it.

**A87.** A. Exactly one
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-13 -->

Explanation: Δ = 36 − 36 = 0, and indeed x² − 6x + 9 = (x − 3)², so there is one repeated solution x = 3.

**A88.** B. y ≥ 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-14 -->

Explanation: Completing the square gives y = (x − 2)² + 3, so the minimum value is 3 and the range is y ≥ 3. The value 7 is only the y-intercept.

**A89.** C. y = −3(x − 4)²
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-15 -->

Explanation: Reflection and dilation give y = −3x². Translating 4 units right replaces x with (x − 4). A translation to the right is x − 4, not x + 4.

**A90.** D. 6
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-16 -->

Explanation: For ax² + bx + c = 0 the sum of the solutions is −b/a = 12/2 = 6. The product, not the sum, is c/a = 5/2.

**A91.** A. y = −2x² + 6x + 8
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-17 -->

Explanation: Use y = a(x + 1)(x − 4). At x = 0: −4a = 8, so a = −2 and y = −2x² + 6x + 8. The option with a = −1 has the correct roots but passes through (0, 4).

**A92.** B. m = 6 or m = −6
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-18 -->

Explanation: Δ = m² − 36 = 0, so m = ±6. Both signs work because m is squared.

**A93.** D. 21 m
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-19 -->

Explanation: The turning point is at t = −20/(−10) = 2, and h(2) = −20 + 40 + 1 = 21. The value 1 is the starting height.

**A94.** A. None
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-20 -->

Explanation: Setting equal: x² − 4x + 5 = 0 with Δ = 16 − 20 = −4 < 0. The minimum of the parabola is −4, which lies above the line y = −5.

**A95.** B. x = 0 or x = 5
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-21 -->

Explanation: x² − 5x = x(x − 5) = 0, so x = 0 or x = 5. Dividing both sides by x loses the solution x = 0.

**A96.** C. −2 < x < 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-22 -->

Explanation: x² − x − 6 = (x − 3)(x + 2). An upward parabola is negative between its roots, so −2 < x < 3. The first option is the solution of x² − x − 6 > 0.

**A97.** D. (3, 13)
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-23 -->

Explanation: x = −12/(2 × −2) = 3, and y = −18 + 36 − 5 = 13. The point (3, −5) wrongly uses the y-intercept as the y-coordinate.

**A98.** A. Translate 2 units left and 3 units down
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-24 -->

Explanation: Replacing x with (x + 2) shifts the graph 2 units left, and −3 shifts it 3 units down. The sign inside the bracket acts in the opposite direction to what is expected.

**A99.** B. 100 m²
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-25 -->

Explanation: With width x, length 20 − x and area A = x(20 − x), the maximum is at x = 10, giving 100 m². The maximum occurs for a square.

**A100.** C. x = −b/(2a)
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-26 -->

Explanation: The vertex lies midway between the roots, whose sum is −b/a, so the axis is x = −b/(2a). The option −b/a is the sum of the roots, not their midpoint.

**A101.** Factorise: (2x + 1)(x − 3) = 0 [1]. So x = −½ or x = 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-27 -->

Explanation:

**A102.** Complete the square: y = (x + 3)² − 9 + 2 = (x + 3)² − 7 [1]. Turning point is (−3, −7) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-28 -->

Explanation:

**A103.** Use y = a(x + 2)(x − 3) [1]. At x = 0: −6a = −12, so a = 2 [1]. y = 2(x + 2)(x − 3) = 2x² − 2x − 12 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-29 -->

Explanation:

**A104.** Δ = k² − 4(k + 3) = k² − 4k − 12 [1]. Δ = (k − 6)(k + 2), which is zero at k = 6 and k = −2 [1]. Two distinct solutions need Δ > 0, so k < −2 or k > 6 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-30 -->

Explanation:

**A105.** (a) −5t(t − 3) = 0, so the ball lands at t = 3 s [1]. (b) The maximum occurs midway at t = 1.5 s [1], where h = −5(2.25) + 22.5 = 11.25 m [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-31 -->

Explanation:

**A106.** y-intercept: (0, 3) [1]. x-intercepts: −(x − 3)(x + 1) = 0, so (3, 0) and (−1, 0) [1]. Turning point: y = −(x − 1)² + 4, so maximum at (1, 4) [1]. Downward-opening parabola through these points, symmetric about x = 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-32 -->

Explanation:

**A107.** Set equal: x² − 2x − 3 = 2x + 2, so x² − 4x − 5 = 0 [1]. (x − 5)(x + 1) = 0, so x = 5 or x = −1 [1]. Points are (5, 12) and (−1, 0) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-33 -->

Explanation:

**A108.** Let the two sides perpendicular to the wall be x m, so the third is (60 − 2x) m and A = x(60 − 2x) = −2x² + 60x [1]. Maximum at x = −60/(−4) = 15 [1]. A = 15 × 30 = 450 m² [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-34 -->

Explanation:

**A109.** (x + 2)² − 4 − 7 = 0, so (x + 2)² = 11 [1]. x = −2 ± √11 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-35 -->

Explanation:

**A110.** Reflection in the x-axis [1]. Dilation by a factor of 2 from the x-axis [1]. Translation 1 unit to the left and 5 units up [1]. y-intercept: y = −2(1) + 5 = 3, so (0, 3) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-36 -->

Explanation:

**A111.** Δ = 9 − 4(2)(4) = −23 [1]. Since Δ < 0 there are no real roots, so no x-intercepts [1]. Minimum at x = 3/4: y = 2(9/16) − 9/4 + 4 = 23/8 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-37 -->

Explanation:

**A112.** The axis passes midway between the x-intercepts 4 and −10, so x = −3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-quad-38 -->

Explanation:

### Polynomials

#### Cubic and quartic graphs

**A113.** C. The graph touches the x-axis and turns (a local turning point)
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-1 -->

Explanation: The factor (x − 1) is squared (repeated, even power), so the graph touches the x-axis and turns at x = 1. A stationary point of inflection would need a cubed factor (x − 1)³.

**A114.** C. 6
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-2 -->

Explanation: At x = 0: (1)(−2)(−3) = 6. The value −6 is a sign error.

**A115.** D. 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-3 -->

Explanation: A polynomial of degree n has at most n − 1 turning points, so a quartic has at most 3.

**A116.** C. −2
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-4 -->

Explanation: At x = 0: a(−1)(2)(−4) = 8a = −16, so a = −2.

**A117.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-5 -->

Explanation: x⁴ − 5x² + 4 = (x² − 1)(x² − 4) = 0, so x = ±1 and x = ±2: four x-intercepts.

**A118.** f(x) = x(x² − x − 6) = x(x − 3)(x + 2) [1]. x-intercepts at −2, 0 and 3; y-intercept (0, 0) [1]. Positive leading coefficient, so y → −∞ as x → −∞ and y → ∞ as x → ∞ [1]. Graph crosses the axis at each intercept (three single factors), positive between −2 and 0 and negative between 0 and 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-6 -->

Explanation:

**A119.** Form: y = a(x + 3)²(x − 2) [1]. At x = 0: a(9)(−2) = −18a = 18, so a = −1 [1]. y = −(x + 3)²(x − 2). Leading coefficient negative, so y → −∞ as x → ∞ [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-7 -->

Explanation:

**A120.** (a) x-intercepts at −3, 0, 2. At x = 0 the factor is squared so the graph touches the axis and turns (flat); crosses at −3 and 2 [1]. Leading coefficient −1 (degree 4), so y → −∞ at both ends [1]. (b) Test: f(1) = −1(−1)(4) = 4 > 0, f(−1) = −1(−3)(2) = 6 > 0, f(3) < 0, f(−4) < 0 [1]. So f(x) > 0 for −3 < x < 2, x ≠ 0, i.e. (−3, 0) ∪ (0, 2) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-8 -->

Explanation:

**A121.** B. y → −∞
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-9 -->

Explanation: For large x the term −2x³ dominates, and it is negative when x is large and positive, so y → −∞. The graph rises to ∞ only if the leading coefficient is positive.

**A122.** C. 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-10 -->

Explanation: A polynomial of degree n has at most n real roots, so a cubic has at most 3 x-intercepts. Four would require a quartic.

**A123.** B. 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-11 -->

Explanation: The ends of a cubic go in opposite directions, so the continuous graph must cross the x-axis at least once. Zero x-intercepts is possible for a quartic, not a cubic.

**A124.** A. The graph crosses the x-axis with a stationary point of inflection
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-12 -->

Explanation: The factor x is cubed (repeated an odd number of times), so the graph flattens and crosses the axis at the origin. Touching and turning needs an even power.

**A125.** C. −6
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-13 -->

Explanation: At x = 0: −2(−1)²(3) = −2 × 1 × 3 = −6. The value 6 comes from dropping the −2.

**A126.** A. 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-14 -->

Explanation: x⁴ + 4x² = x²(x² + 4), and x² + 4 = 0 has no real solutions, so the only x-intercept is at x = 0. Counting x² = 0 as two intercepts is a common error.

**A127.** B. y → ∞ as x → −∞ and as x → ∞
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-15 -->

Explanation: Even degree means both ends point the same way, and a positive leading coefficient makes that direction upward. The mixed-direction options describe odd-degree graphs.

**A128.** D. y = (x − 2)³ − 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-16 -->

Explanation: A shift of 2 to the right replaces x with x − 2, and a shift down 1 subtracts 1. A + inside the bracket would move the graph left.

**A129.** B. (−3, −4)
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-17 -->

Explanation: The basic graph y = x³ has its inflection at the origin, and this graph is shifted 3 left and 4 down, giving (−3, −4). The bracket (x + 3) moves the graph left, not right.

**A130.** C. −1 < x < 2 or x > 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-18 -->

Explanation: The leading coefficient is positive, so y > 0 for x > 4, then the sign alternates at each single root, so y > 0 again on −1 < x < 2. Testing x = 0 gives 8 > 0, which confirms this.

**A131.** B. 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-19 -->

Explanation: x⁴ − 4x³ + 4x² = x²(x² − 4x + 4) = x²(x − 2)², so the intercepts are at x = 0 and x = 2, both touch points. Counting repeated roots separately gives 4, which is wrong.

**A132.** A. y = (x − 1)³
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-20 -->

Explanation: A triple factor (x − 1)³ gives a single x-intercept at which the graph flattens and crosses. The other options each have two or three distinct x-intercepts.

**A133.** C. y = 3x⁴ + 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-21 -->

Explanation: Dilation by 3 from the x-axis multiplies y by 3 to give 3x⁴, then adding 2 gives 3x⁴ + 2. Multiplying by 3 after the translation would give 3x⁴ + 6.

**A134.** x-intercepts at x = −1 and x = 2 [1]. y-intercept: (1)²(2) = 2, so (0, 2) [1]. The factor (x + 1) is squared, so the graph touches the x-axis and turns at x = −1; it crosses at x = 2 [1]. The leading term is −x³ (negative), so y → ∞ as x → −∞ and y → −∞ as x → ∞ [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-22 -->

Explanation:

**A135.** Let u = x², so u² − 3u − 4 = 0 and (u − 4)(u + 1) = 0, giving (x² − 4)(x² + 1) = 0 [1]. x² = 4 gives x = ±2 [1]. x² = −1 has no real solutions, so the only x-intercepts are (−2, 0) and (2, 0) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-23 -->

Explanation:

**A136.** Form: y = a(x − 2)²(x + 1)(x − 4) [1]. At x = 0: a(4)(1)(−4) = −16a = 16, so a = −1 [1]. y = −(x − 2)²(x + 1)(x − 4) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-24 -->

Explanation:

**A137.** f has even degree and negative leading coefficient, so f(x) → −∞ as x → −∞ and as x → ∞ [1]. g has odd degree and positive leading coefficient, so g(x) → −∞ as x → −∞ and g(x) → ∞ as x → ∞ [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-25 -->

Explanation:

**A138.** (a) y = −(x − 3)³ + 2 [1]. (b) The inflection point moves from (0, 0) to (3, 2) [1]. (c) y-intercept: −(−3)³ + 2 = 27 + 2 = 29, so (0, 29) [1]. x-intercept: (x − 3)³ = 2, so x = 3 + ∛2 ≈ 4.26 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-26 -->

Explanation:

**A139.** x³ − 9x = x(x − 3)(x + 3), with roots −3, 0 and 3 [1]. Positive leading coefficient: the graph is below the axis for x < −3 and for 0 < x < 3, and above for −3 < x < 0 and x > 3 [1]. So x³ − 9x ≤ 0 for x ≤ −3 or 0 ≤ x ≤ 3 (roots included) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-27 -->

Explanation:

**A140.** The ends of a cubic go in opposite directions (one up, one down), so the continuous graph must cross the x-axis at least once [1]. The ends of a quartic go in the same direction, so with a positive leading coefficient the graph can stay entirely above the axis [1]. Example: y = x⁴ + 1 (any quartic whose minimum is above the axis) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-28 -->

Explanation:

**A141.** Form: y = a(x + 2)(x − 1)(x − 3) [1]. At x = 2: a(4)(1)(−1) = −4a = 8 [1]. So a = −2 and y = −2(x + 2)(x − 1)(x − 3) [1]. At x = 0: −2(2)(−1)(−3) = −12, so the y-intercept is (0, −12) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-29 -->

Explanation:

**A142.** (a) The base is (12 − 2x) by (8 − 2x) and the height is x, so V = x(12 − 2x)(8 − 2x) [1]. (b) x-intercepts at x = 0, 4 and 6 [1]. The cut must be positive and less than half the shorter side, so 0 < x < 4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-30 -->

Explanation:

**A143.** C. It crosses the x-axis with a stationary point of inflection
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-31 -->

Explanation: A repeated factor of odd power (x − 2)³ gives a cross with a flattened, stationary point of inflection shape. An even power would be needed for the graph to touch and turn.

**A144.** D. y = −x(x + 2)(x − 4)
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-32 -->

Explanation: Replacing x with −x gives (−x)(−x − 2)(−x + 4) = −x(x + 2)(x − 4). The option y = −x(x − 2)(x + 4) is the reflection in the x-axis instead.

**A145.** A. 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-33 -->

Explanation: Substituting x = 0 gives a(1)²(−3)² = 9a = 18, so a = 2. Using (−3) without squaring gives 3a = 18 and the incorrect 6.

**A146.** B. It has two x-intercepts: it touches the axis at x = −2 and crosses at x = 0
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-34 -->

Explanation: Factorising gives y = x(x + 2)², so x = 0 is a single factor (cross) and x = −2 is a double factor (touch). The tempting alternative reverses which factor is repeated.

**A147.** C. y → −∞ in both cases
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-35 -->

Explanation: The dominant term −x⁴ is negative for all large |x|, so both ends go down. The linear term 2x is too small to change the end behaviour.

**A148.** f(1) = 0 gives a + b = −7 and f(2) = 0 gives 4a + 2b = −14 [1]. Solving gives a = 0 and b = −7 [1]. f(x) = x³ − 7x + 6 = (x − 1)(x − 2)(x + 3), so the third intercept is x = −3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-36 -->

Explanation:

**A149.** y = (x² − 4)² = (x − 2)²(x + 2)² [1]. x-intercepts at x = ±2, where the graph touches the axis (repeated factors) [1]. y-intercept (0, 16), which is a local maximum [1]. Positive leading coefficient so y → ∞ as x → ±∞, giving a W-shape [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-cq-37 -->

Explanation:

#### Factor and remainder theorems

**A150.** D. −3
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-1 -->

Explanation: By the remainder theorem, remainder = P(2) = 8 − 8 + 2 − 5 = −3.

**A151.** D. 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-2 -->

Explanation: Need P(−1) = 0: −1 + k + 1 − 2 = k − 2 = 0, so k = 2.

**A152.** C. 2x − 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-3 -->

Explanation: P(1/2) = 1/4 + 1/4 − 13/2 + 6 = 0, so (2x − 1) is a factor. P(1) = −4, P(−1/2) ≠ 0 and P(3) = 30, so the others are not factors.

**A153.** B. −6
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-4 -->

Explanation: P(3) = 27 + 3a − 2 = 7, so 3a = −18 and a = −6.

**A154.** C. 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-5 -->

Explanation: Remainder = P(−1/2) = 4(−1/8) + 1/2 + 2 = −1/2 + 1/2 + 2 = 2. The values −1 and 5 are P(−1) and P(1).

**A155.** P(2) = 8 − 12 − 8 + 12 = 0, so (x − 2) is a factor [1]. Division gives P(x) = (x − 2)(x² − x − 6) [1] = (x − 2)(x − 3)(x + 2) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A156.** P(1) = 0: 1 + a + b − 6 = 0, so a + b = 5 [1]. P(−1) = 2: −1 + a − b − 6 = 2, so a − b = 9 [1]. Adding: 2a = 14, a = 7 and b = −2 [1]. Check: P(x) = x³ + 7x² − 2x − 6; P(1) = 0 and P(−1) = 2.
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A157.** Q(x) = x² + 4x + 3 [1]; remainder R = P(2) = 8 + 8 − 10 + 1 = 7 [1]. P(x) = (x − 2)(x² + 4x + 3) + 7 [1]. Check: (x − 2)(x² + 4x + 3) = x³ + 2x² − 5x − 6, and adding 7 gives P(x).
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-8 -->

Explanation:

**A158.** A. 10
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-9 -->

Explanation: Remainder = P(−2) = −8 + 12 + 2 + 4 = 10. The value 22 is P(2), which would be the remainder for (x − 2).

**A159.** B. 7
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-10 -->

Explanation: Need P(3) = 0: 27 − 45 + 3k − 3 = 3k − 21 = 0, so k = 7. Getting −7 comes from a sign slip when collecting terms.

**A160.** C. P(a) = 0
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-11 -->

Explanation: Substituting x = a, the value that makes (x − a) zero, must give P(a) = 0. P(−a) = 0 would instead make (x + a) a factor.

**A161.** D. −7
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-12 -->

Explanation: Remainder = P(1) = 2 − 3 + 1 − 7 = −7. The value −13 is P(−1), which applies to a divisor (x + 1).

**A162.** C. x − 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-13 -->

Explanation: P(1) = 1 + 1 − 4 + 2 = 0, so (x − 1) is a factor. P(−1), P(2) and P(−2) are all 6, so the other options are not factors.

**A163.** B. −5
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-14 -->

Explanation: Remainder = P(−1) = 1 − 3 − 2 − 1 = −5. The value −1 is P(1), the remainder for (x − 1).

**A164.** D. 14/3
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-15 -->

Explanation: Remainder = P(2/3) = 8/9 − 8/9 + 2/3 + 4 = 14/3. The value 14/9 is P(−2/3), and 22 is P(2).

**A165.** B. −5
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-16 -->

Explanation: P(−2) = −8 − 2k − 4 = −2, so −2k = 10 and k = −5. Using P(2) instead would wrongly give k = −3.

**A166.** B. x² + x − 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-17 -->

Explanation: Division gives x² + x − 2 with remainder 1, since (x − 3)(x² + x − 2) = x³ − 2x² − 5x + 6. The remainder equals P(3) = 1, which is not part of the quotient.

**A167.** C. (x − 2) is a factor of P(x)
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-18 -->

Explanation: P(2) = 0 means (x − 2) is a factor by the factor theorem. (x + 2) would need P(−2) = 0, which is not given.

**A168.** A. −3
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-19 -->

Explanation: P(2) = 8 + 4a − 2 + 6 = 12 + 4a = 0, so a = −3. A value of 3 comes from losing the negative sign.

**A169.** B. 5/2
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-20 -->

Explanation: Remainder = P(1/2) = 1/4 + 5/4 − 2 + 3 = 5/2. The value 6 is P(−1/2) and also P(1), which are for different divisors.

**A170.** D. 0
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-21 -->

Explanation: Remainder = P(−1) = (−1)²¹ + 1 = −1 + 1 = 0, so (x + 1) is a factor. The value 2 is P(1), the remainder for (x − 1).

**A171.** C. (x − 1)(x − 2)(x − 3)
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-22 -->

Explanation: Each zero a gives a factor (x − a), so the factors are (x − 1), (x − 2) and (x − 3). Factors of the form (x + a) correspond to zeros at −a, not a.

**A172.** A. a = 2, b = −1
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-23 -->

Explanation: P(1) = 0 gives a + b = 1 and P(−2) = 0 gives 2a − b = 5, so a = 2 and b = −1. The pair a = 3, b = −2 satisfies only the first equation.

**A173.** A. A constant
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-24 -->

Explanation: The remainder has smaller degree than the divisor (degree 1), so it is a constant equal to P(a). Degree three describes the quotient, not the remainder.

**A174.** D. −2
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-25 -->

Explanation: P(−1) = −2 − 1 + 5 + k = 2 + k = 0, so k = −2. The value 4 comes from using P(1) = k − 4 = 0 instead.

**A175.** D. (x − a)
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-26 -->

Explanation: P(x) − R has value P(a) − R = 0 at x = a, so (x − a) is a factor. R is a value of the polynomial, not a zero.

**A176.** By the remainder theorem the remainder is P(−1) [1]. P(−1) = −2 − 1 − 3 − 4 = −10 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-27 -->

Explanation:

**A177.** P(−3) = −27 + 18 + 15 − 6 = 0, so (x + 3) is a factor [1]. Division gives P(x) = (x + 3)(x² − x − 2) [1] = (x + 3)(x − 2)(x + 1) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-28 -->

Explanation:

**A178.** P(3) = 27 + 9k − 21 + 6 = 12 + 9k = −6 [1], so k = −2 [1]. Then P(x) = x³ − 2x² − 7x + 6 and P(−1) = −1 − 2 + 7 + 6 = 10, so the remainder is 10 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-29 -->

Explanation:

**A179.** Let P(x) = x³ − 3x² − 6x + 8. P(1) = 1 − 3 − 6 + 8 = 0, so (x − 1) is a factor [1]. Division gives P(x) = (x − 1)(x² − 2x − 8) [1] = (x − 1)(x − 4)(x + 2) [1]. So x = 1, x = 4 or x = −2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-30 -->

Explanation:

**A180.** P(1) = 1 + 1 − 7 − 1 + 6 = 0 [1]. P(−1) = 1 − 1 − 7 + 1 + 6 = 0 [1]. So (x − 1)(x + 1) = x² − 1 is a factor, and dividing gives P(x) = (x² − 1)(x² + x − 6) [1] = (x − 1)(x + 1)(x − 2)(x + 3) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-31 -->

Explanation:

**A181.** The remainder is P(1/2) since 2x − 1 = 0 when x = 1/2 [1]. P(1/2) = 6/8 − 1/4 + 2 − 1 = 3/2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-32 -->

Explanation:

**A182.** P(−1/2) = −1/4 + 7/4 − k/2 − 3 = 0 [1], so −3/2 − k/2 = 0 and k = −3 [1]. Division gives P(x) = (2x + 1)(x² + 3x − 3), so the quotient is x² + 3x − 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-33 -->

Explanation:

**A183.** P(−2) = −8 + 16 + 2 − 6 = 4 [1]. Since P(−2) ≠ 0, (x + 2) is not a factor of P(x) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-34 -->

Explanation:

**A184.** V(−3) = −27 + 27 + 12 − 12 = 0, so (x + 3) is a factor [1]. Division gives V(x) = (x + 3)(x² − 4) [1] = (x + 3)(x − 2)(x + 2), so the dimensions are (x + 3), (x − 2) and (x + 2) cm [1]. When x = 5: 8 × 3 × 7 = 168 cm³ [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-35 -->

Explanation:

**A185.** Let P(x) = 2x³ − 3x² − 11x + 6. P(3) = 54 − 27 − 33 + 6 = 0, so (x − 3) is a factor [1]. Division gives P(x) = (x − 3)(2x² + 3x − 2) = (x − 3)(2x − 1)(x + 2) [1]. So x = 3, x = 1/2 or x = −2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-36 -->

Explanation:

**A186.** P(1) = 1 − 5 + 7 − 3 = 0, so (x − 1) is a factor [1]. Division gives P(x) = (x − 1)(x² − 4x + 3) [1] = (x − 1)²(x − 3), so (x − 1) is the repeated factor [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-37 -->

Explanation:

**A187.** P(x) = (x − 3)(x² + 2x − 1) + 4 [1] = x³ − x² − 7x + 3 + 4, which gives P(x) = x³ − x² − 7x + 7 [1]. P(3) = 27 − 9 − 21 + 7 = 4, as required [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-38 -->

Explanation:

**A188.** D. 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-frt-39 -->

Explanation: By the remainder theorem P(−1) = −2 + k + 1 + 4 = k + 3 = 7, so k = 4. Evaluating P(1) instead gives the incorrect equation k + 5 = 7.

#### Solving polynomial equations

**A189.** D. x = 0, x = 2 or x = −2
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-1 -->

Explanation: x(x² − 4) = x(x − 2)(x + 2) = 0, so x = 0, 2 or −2. Dividing both sides by x would lose the solution x = 0.

**A190.** D. x = ±2 or x = ±3
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-2 -->

Explanation: Let u = x²: u² − 13u + 36 = (u − 4)(u − 9) = 0, so x² = 4 or x² = 9, giving x = ±2 or ±3. The first option stops at u.

**A191.** C. 3/2
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-3 -->

Explanation: (x + 2)(2x² − 7x + 3) = (x + 2)(2x − 1)(x − 3), so solutions are −2, 1/2 and 3. Sum = 3/2 (also −b/a = 3/2).

**A192.** B. 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-4 -->

Explanation: x(x² − 6x + 9) = x(x − 3)² = 0, so x = 0 or x = 3. The repeated root counts once, so there are 2 distinct solutions.

**A193.** C. 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-5 -->

Explanation: x³ + 8 = (x + 2)(x² − 2x + 4). The quadratic has discriminant 4 − 16 < 0, so the only real solution is x = −2.

**A194.** Try x = 2: 8 − 12 − 20 + 24 = 0, so (x − 2) is a factor [1]. Dividing: (x − 2)(x² − x − 12) [1] = (x − 2)(x − 4)(x + 3), so x = 2, 4 or −3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-6 -->

Explanation:

**A195.** (a) Let u = x²: u² − 10u + 9 = (u − 1)(u − 9) = 0, so x² = 1 or 9, x = ±1 or ±3 [2]. (b) u² + u − 6 = (u + 3)(u − 2) = 0; x² = −3 has no real solution, x² = 2 gives x = ±√2 [2].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-7 -->

Explanation:

**A196.** x(x + 2)(x + 5) = 120, so x³ + 7x² + 10x − 120 = 0 [1]. Try x = 3: 27 + 63 + 30 − 120 = 0, so (x − 3) is a factor [1]. Dividing: (x − 3)(x² + 10x + 40) = 0; the quadratic has discriminant 100 − 160 < 0 so no further real roots [1]. x = 3, so the dimensions are 3 cm, 5 cm, 8 cm (3 × 5 × 8 = 120) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-8 -->

Explanation:

**A197.** A. x = 1, x = −4 or x = 3/2
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-9 -->

Explanation: Each factor is set to zero: x − 1 = 0, x + 4 = 0 and 2x − 3 = 0, giving x = 1, −4 and 3/2. Forgetting to divide by 2 in the last factor gives x = 3, which is wrong.

**A198.** B. x = 0 or x = ±3√3
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-10 -->

Explanation: x(x² − 27) = 0, so x = 0 or x² = 27, giving x = ±3√3. Dividing through by x would lose x = 0, and x² = 27 does not give x = ±3.

**A199.** C. 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-11 -->

Explanation: Substituting x = 2: 8 − 8 + 2k − 6 = 0, so 2k = 6 and k = 3. A sign error when moving the 6 across gives k = −3.

**A200.** A. −3
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-12 -->

Explanation: Dividing out (x − 1)(x − 2) = x² − 3x + 2 leaves (x + 3), so the third solution is x = −3. The solutions sum to 0 (there is no x² term), which also gives 1 + 2 + (−3) = 0.

**A201.** B. x = 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-13 -->

Explanation: 2x³ = 16 gives x³ = 8, so x = 2. A cube has only one real cube root, so x = −2 is not a solution; x = 8 forgets to take the cube root.

**A202.** C. 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-14 -->

Explanation: x⁴ − 16 = (x² − 4)(x² + 4). The factor x² − 4 gives x = ±2, but x² + 4 = 0 has no real solution, so there are 2 real solutions, not 4.

**A203.** D. 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-15 -->

Explanation: The solutions are x = 0, x = 2 (repeated) and x = −1. The repeated factor is counted once, so there are 3 distinct solutions, not 4.

**A204.** A. 2/3
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-16 -->

Explanation: A rational solution p/q must have p dividing 3 and q dividing 2, so the candidates are ±1, ±3, ±1/2 and ±3/2. The value 2/3 has numerator 2, which does not divide 3.

**A205.** B. x = 2 or x = −1
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-17 -->

Explanation: Let u = x³: (u − 8)(u + 1) = 0, so x³ = 8 or x³ = −1, giving x = 2 or x = −1. Stopping at u gives 8 and −1, and cube roots have only one real value each, so there are no ± pairs.

**A206.** C. −6
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-18 -->

Explanation: For a cubic ax³ + bx² + cx + d = 0 the product of the solutions is −d/a = −6. Using +6 forgets the negative sign in the formula.

**A207.** D. x = 0, x = 1 or x = −3
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-19 -->

Explanation: x³ + 2x² − 3x = x(x² + 2x − 3) = x(x − 1)(x + 3) = 0, so x = 0, 1 or −3. Dividing both sides by x would lose x = 0.

**A208.** B. x = ±3
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-20 -->

Explanation: Let u = x²: (u − 9)(u + 4) = 0, so x² = 9 or x² = −4. Only x² = 9 gives real solutions, x = ±3.

**A209.** C. x = 0, x = 1/2 or x = −2
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-21 -->

Explanation: x(2x² + 3x − 2) = x(2x − 1)(x + 2) = 0, so x = 0, 1/2 or −2. The second option omits x = 0, and the others have sign errors in the quadratic factors.

**A210.** D. x(x − 2)(x + 3) = 0
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-22 -->

Explanation: A solution x = a comes from the factor (x − a), so the factors are x, (x − 2) and (x + 3). Writing (x + 2) or (x − 3) reverses the sign of a solution.

**A211.** A. 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-23 -->

Explanation: x(x² + 4) = 0. The factor x gives x = 0, while x² + 4 = 0 has no real solutions, so there is only 1 real solution.

**A212.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-24 -->

Explanation: x(x² − 2x − 8) = x(x − 4)(x + 2) = 0, so x = 0, 4 or −2 and the largest is 4. Reading the factor (x + 2) as x = 2 is a sign error.

**A213.** C. x = 1 or x = −2
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-25 -->

Explanation: Rearranging, x³ + 3x² − 4 = 0, and x = 1 works, giving (x − 1)(x² + 4x + 4) = (x − 1)(x + 2)². So x = 1 or x = −2 (a repeated solution).

**A214.** x(x² − 9) = x(x − 3)(x + 3) = 0 [1]. x = 0, x = 3 or x = −3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-26 -->

Explanation:

**A215.** Try x = 2: 8 + 8 − 10 − 6 = 0, so (x − 2) is a factor [1]. Dividing gives (x − 2)(x² + 4x + 3) [1] = (x − 2)(x + 1)(x + 3), so x = 2, −1 or −3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-27 -->

Explanation:

**A216.** Group: x²(2x − 1) − 4(2x − 1) [1] = (2x − 1)(x² − 4) = (2x − 1)(x − 2)(x + 2) [1]. So x = 1/2, x = 2 or x = −2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-28 -->

Explanation:

**A217.** Let u = x²: 2u² − 11u + 5 = 0 [1]. (2u − 1)(u − 5) = 0, so u = 1/2 or u = 5 [1]. x² = 1/2 gives x = ±√2/2 [1]. x² = 5 gives x = ±√5 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-29 -->

Explanation:

**A218.** Substitute x = −1: −1 + k + 4 − 6 = 0 [1], so k = 3 [1]. Dividing x³ + 3x² − 4x − 6 by (x + 1) gives (x + 1)(x² + 2x − 6) [1]. The quadratic gives x = −1 ± √7, so the solutions are x = −1, −1 + √7 and −1 − √7 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-30 -->

Explanation:

**A219.** The box has base (12 − 2x) by (12 − 2x) and height x, so x(12 − 2x)² = 128, giving 4x³ − 48x² + 144x − 128 = 0 and x³ − 12x² + 36x − 32 = 0 [1]. Try x = 2: 8 − 48 + 72 − 32 = 0, so (x − 2) is a factor [1]. Dividing: (x − 2)(x² − 10x + 16) = (x − 2)(x − 2)(x − 8), so x = 2 or x = 8 [1]. Since 12 − 2x > 0 requires x < 6, x = 8 is rejected and x = 2 cm [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-31 -->

Explanation:

**A220.** Let u = x² − 3x: u² − 2u − 8 = 0 [1]. (u − 4)(u + 2) = 0, so u = 4 or u = −2 [1]. x² − 3x − 4 = 0 gives (x − 4)(x + 1) = 0, so x = 4 or x = −1 [1]. x² − 3x + 2 = 0 gives (x − 1)(x − 2) = 0, so x = 1 or x = 2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-32 -->

Explanation:

**A221.** Try x = 1: 1 − 7 + 14 − 8 = 0, so (x − 1) is a factor [1]. Dividing gives (x − 1)(x² − 6x + 8) [1] = (x − 1)(x − 2)(x − 4), so x = 1, 2 or 4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-33 -->

Explanation:

**A222.** Dividing by x² assumes x ≠ 0, so the solution x = 0 is lost [1]. Correctly, x³ − 4x² = x²(x − 4) = 0, so x = 0 or x = 4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-34 -->

Explanation:

**A223.** A. x = 1 and x = −1
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-35 -->

Explanation: The factor x² + 4 is always positive, so only x² − 1 = 0 gives real solutions, x = ±1. The solutions ±2 would only occur if the factor were x² − 4.

**A224.** B. 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-36 -->

Explanation: Factorising gives x(x − 5)(x + 3) = 0 with solutions 0, 5 and −3, which sum to 2. The value −2 comes from mis-signing the solutions (using −5 and 3).

**A225.** Test x = 3: 27 + 36 − 33 − 30 = 0, so (x − 3) is a factor [1]. x³ + 4x² − 11x − 30 = (x − 3)(x² + 7x + 10) = (x − 3)(x + 2)(x + 5) [1]. Solutions x = 3, x = −2, x = −5 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-37 -->

Explanation:

**A226.** x²(x + 2) = 45, so x³ + 2x² − 45 = 0 [1]. 27 + 18 − 45 = 0, so x = 3 is a solution [1]. x³ + 2x² − 45 = (x − 3)(x² + 5x + 15) and the discriminant 25 − 60 < 0, so no other real solutions [1]. The base has side 3 cm [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-spe-38 -->

Explanation:

### Transformations and other functions

#### Power functions and hyperbolas

**A227.** A. (0, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-1 -->

Explanation: x² > 0 for x ≠ 0, so 1/x² > 0 and takes every positive value. It never equals 0 (asymptote), so the range is (0, ∞).

**A228.** B. x = 3, y = 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-2 -->

Explanation: The vertical asymptote occurs where the denominator is zero (x = 3), and the horizontal asymptote is the vertical translation y = 1.

**A229.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-3 -->

Explanation: Set 6/(x + 2) = 1, so x + 2 = 6 and x = 4. The value 2 is the y-intercept (3 − 1).

**A230.** D. y = x⁻⁴
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-4 -->

Explanation: x⁻⁴ gives f(−x) = f(x), so it is symmetric about the y-axis. The other three satisfy f(−x) = −f(x) (odd).

**A231.** C. x^(1/2)
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-5 -->

Explanation: For 0 < x < 1, a smaller index gives a larger value. For x = 0.25: x^(1/2) = 0.5, x^(3/2) = 0.125, x² = 0.0625, x³ ≈ 0.016.

**A232.** Asymptotes: x = 1 and y = 2 [1]. y-intercept: 3/(−1) + 2 = −1, so (0, −1) [1]. x-intercept: 3/(x − 1) = −2, so x − 1 = −3/2 and x = −1/2, so (−1/2, 0) [1]. Domain R \ {1}, range R \ {2} [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A233.** 4/x = x + 3, so 4 = x² + 3x (x ≠ 0) and x² + 3x − 4 = 0 [1]. (x + 4)(x − 1) = 0, so x = 1 or x = −4 [1]. Points: (1, 4) and (−4, −1) [1]. Check: −4 + 3 = −1 = 4/(−4).
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A234.** (1, 2): a(1)^n = 2, so a = 2 [1]. (2, 16): 2(2^n) = 16, so 2^n = 8 and n = 3 [1]. y = 2x³; f(−x) = −2x³ = −f(x), so it is an odd function [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A235.** A. R \ {−4}
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-9 -->

Explanation: The denominator is zero when x = −4, so x = −4 is excluded. The value −5 is the vertical translation, which affects the range, not the domain.

**A236.** B. R \ {−2}
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-10 -->

Explanation: The horizontal asymptote is y = −2, and 3/x takes every non-zero value, so y takes every value except −2. Excluding 2 would come from misreading the sign of the translation.

**A237.** D. Second and fourth
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-11 -->

Explanation: When a < 0, positive x gives negative y (fourth quadrant) and negative x gives positive y (second quadrant). First and third quadrants is the case for a > 0.

**A238.** D. y = x^(2/3)
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-12 -->

Explanation: x^(2/3) = (∛x)² is defined for all real x and satisfies f(−x) = f(x). Although x⁻² is even, it is undefined at x = 0, so its domain is not R.

**A239.** C. [0, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-13 -->

Explanation: x^(1/2) = √x requires x ≥ 0, and x = 0 gives a valid output of 0. The interval (0, ∞) wrongly excludes x = 0.

**A240.** B. y = −4/(x − 2) − 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-14 -->

Explanation: Write y = a/(x − 2) − 1 and substitute (0, 1): 1 = −a/2 − 1, so a = −4. The positive version gives y = −3 at x = 0.

**A241.** A. 0
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-15 -->

Explanation: Setting 1/x = −x gives x² = −1, which has no real solution. Students who forget the sign and solve x² = 1 would wrongly find two points.

**A242.** C. y = 3/x
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-16 -->

Explanation: A dilation by factor 3 from the y-axis replaces x with x/3, giving y = 1/(x/3) = 3/x. The rule 1/(3x) corresponds to a dilation by factor 1/3 from the y-axis.

**A243.** B. y → −∞
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-17 -->

Explanation: For small negative x, 1/x is a large negative number, so y → −∞. The behaviour y → ∞ occurs as x approaches 0 from the right.

**A244.** C. 1/4
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-18 -->

Explanation: 8^(−2/3) = 1/(8^(1/3))² = 1/2² = 1/4. A negative index gives a reciprocal, not a negative value.

**A245.** D. x = 8 or x = −8
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-19 -->

Explanation: x^(2/3) = (∛x)² = 4 gives ∛x = ±2, so x = ±8. Giving only x = 8 misses the negative solution, which also works since (−8)^(2/3) = 4.

**A246.** A. (0, 0) and (1, 1)
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-20 -->

Explanation: Solving x^(1/2) = x^(1/3) gives x^(1/3)(x^(1/6) − 1) = 0, so x = 0 or x = 1. The point (−1, −1) is not on y = x^(1/2), which is undefined for negative x.

**A247.** B. It is its own inverse
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-21 -->

Explanation: Swapping x and y in y = 1/x gives x = 1/y, so y = 1/x; the graph is symmetric about y = x. It is one-to-one, so an inverse exists, and it is not −1/x.

**A248.** A. y = −2/(x − 1) + 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-22 -->

Explanation: Since 3x − 5 = 3(x − 1) − 2, the expression equals 3 − 2/(x − 1). The positive 2/(x − 1) version results from a sign error.

**A249.** A. 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-23 -->

Explanation: y = −8/(−2) = 4. A negative divided by a negative is positive, so −4 is incorrect.

**A250.** D. (3, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-24 -->

Explanation: 1/(x − 2)² is always positive and never zero, so y > 3 and the range is (3, ∞). The interval [3, ∞) is wrong because y = 3 is only approached, never reached.

**A251.** B. y = −12/x
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-25 -->

Explanation: Substituting gives 4 = a/(−3), so a = −12. Using a = 12 ignores that x is negative.

**A252.** Asymptotes: x = −1 and y = 4 [1]. Domain: R \ {−1} [1]. Range: R \ {4} [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-26 -->

Explanation:

**A253.** Form: y = a/(x − 1) + 3 [1]. Substitute (3, 5): 5 = a/2 + 3 [1]. So a = 4 and y = 4/(x − 1) + 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-27 -->

Explanation:

**A254.** f(−x) = (−x)⁻³ = −x⁻³ = −f(x), so the function is odd [1]. Domain: R \ {0} [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-28 -->

Explanation:

**A255.** 2x + 5 = 2(x + 1) + 3, so y = 3/(x + 1) + 2 [1]. Asymptotes: x = −1 and y = 2 [1]. y-intercept: when x = 0, y = 5, so (0, 5) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-29 -->

Explanation:

**A256.** 16^(3/4) = (⁴√16)³ = 2³ = 8 [1]. 125^(−2/3) = 1/(∛125)² = 1/5² = 1/25 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-30 -->

Explanation:

**A257.** Dilation by a factor of 2 from the x-axis (y = 2/x) [1]. Reflection in the x-axis (y = −2/x) [1]. Translation 3 units to the left and 1 unit up [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-31 -->

Explanation:

**A258.** 6 = k/4, so k = 24 [1]. With 8 pumps, T = 24/8 = 3 hours [1]. 24/n = 2, so n = 12 pumps [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-32 -->

Explanation:

**A259.** Raise both sides of x^(3/2) = 27 to the power 2/3 [1]. x = 27^(2/3) = 9 [1]. x^(−1/3) = 1/2 gives x^(1/3) = 2 [1]. So x = 8 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-33 -->

Explanation:

**A260.** x⁻² = x⁻⁴ gives x⁴ = x², so x²(x² − 1) = 0 and x = ±1 (x ≠ 0) [1]. Points: (1, 1) and (−1, 1) [1]. For |x| > 1, x⁻² > x⁻⁴ (e.g. x = 2: 1/4 > 1/16), so y = x⁻² is higher [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-34 -->

Explanation:

**A261.** Swap x and y: x = 1/(y − 2) + 3 [1]. Rearrange: y − 2 = 1/(x − 3), so f⁻¹(x) = 1/(x − 3) + 2 [1]. Domain R \ {3}, range R \ {2} [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-35 -->

Explanation:

**A262.** D. −1
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-36 -->

Explanation: At x = 0, y = 4/(−2) + 1 = −1. Mistakenly using x + 2 in the denominator would give the incorrect 3.

**A263.** A. R
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-37 -->

Explanation: The cube root function has domain R and range R, and scaling by −3 and shifting up by 2 keeps the range as all real numbers. The restriction y ≤ 2 would apply to a square root, not a cube root.

**A264.** Asymptotes x = 3 and y = 2 [1]. Domain R \ {3} [1]. Range (−∞, 2) [1]. Solving 1/(x − 3)² = 2 gives (x − 3)² = 1/2, so x = 3 ± √2/2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-38 -->

Explanation:

**A265.** 2 = k/9 so k = 18 [1]. y = 18/36 = 1/2 when x = −6 [1]. f(−x) = 18/(−x)² = f(x), so the function is even and symmetrical about the y-axis [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-pwr-39 -->

Explanation:

#### Transformations of graphs

**A266.** C. y = −(x − 3)² + 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-1 -->

Explanation: Reflection in the x-axis gives y = −x². Moving 3 right replaces x with x − 3, and 2 up adds 2: y = −(x − 3)² + 2.

**A267.** C. (1, 7)
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-2 -->

Explanation: x + 1 = 2 gives x = 1 (a shift of 1 to the left, not right). The y-value becomes 2(5) − 3 = 7. The image point is (1, 7).

**A268.** D. Dilation by factor 1/3 from the y-axis
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-3 -->

Explanation: Replacing x with 3x compresses the graph horizontally by a factor of 1/3, that is a dilation of factor 1/3 from the y-axis (parallel to the x-axis).

**A269.** C. y = −3/x + 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-4 -->

Explanation: Dilation: y = 3/x. Reflection in the y-axis replaces x with −x: y = −3/x. Translation up 1: y = −3/x + 1.

**A270.** A. (−∞, 1]
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-5 -->

Explanation: √(4 − x) ≥ 0, so −√(4 − x) ≤ 0 and y ≤ 1. The range is (−∞, 1]. (−∞, 4] is the domain.

**A271.** Dilation by factor 2 from the x-axis, reflection in the x-axis, translation 1 unit left and 5 units up [1]. Vertex (−1, 5) [1]. y-intercept: −2(1) + 5 = 3, so (0, 3) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-6 -->

Explanation:

**A272.** Each point (x, y) maps to (x + 1, −y + 2) [1]. (−3, 4) → (−2, −2) [1]; (0, 0) → (1, 2); (2, −1) → (3, 3) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-7 -->

Explanation:

**A273.** (a) Dilation gives y = 2(x³ − x) [1]; reflection in the y-axis replaces x with −x: y = 2(−x³ + x) = −2x³ + 2x [1]. (b) (x, y) → (−x, 2y), so (2, 6) → (−2, 12) [1]. Check: −2(−8) + 2(−2) = 16 − 4 = 12 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-8 -->

Explanation:

**A274.** B. y = (x + 5)²
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-9 -->

Explanation: Moving left by 5 replaces x with x + 5, giving y = (x + 5)². y = (x − 5)² would be a shift to the right.

**A275.** C. y = −f(x)
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-10 -->

Explanation: Reflection in the x-axis changes the sign of every y-value, so y = −f(x). y = f(−x) is the reflection in the y-axis.

**A276.** A. (18, −2)
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-11 -->

Explanation: x/3 = 6 gives x = 18, so the graph is stretched horizontally by factor 3 and the y-value is unchanged. (2, −2) would come from f(3x).

**A277.** B. [−3, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-12 -->

Explanation: We need x + 3 ≥ 0, so x ≥ −3. The −2 shifts the range, not the domain.

**A278.** C. (−∞, 4]
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-13 -->

Explanation: The parabola opens downwards (negative coefficient) with vertex (−2, 4), so y ≤ 4. [4, ∞) would be the range if it opened upwards.

**A279.** D. x = 2, y = 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-14 -->

Explanation: The graph of y = 1/x is translated 2 right and 3 up, so the asymptotes x = 0 and y = 0 move to x = 2 and y = 3. x = −2 would result from a shift to the left.

**A280.** A. y = −2x + 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-15 -->

Explanation: Replace x with −x: y = −2x + 1. y = −2x − 1 is the reflection in the x-axis.

**A281.** B. x = −1 and x = 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-16 -->

Explanation: f(2x) = 0 when 2x = −2 or 4, so x = −1 or 2. The graph is compressed horizontally by factor 1/2; x = −4 and 8 would come from f(x/2).

**A282.** C. y = −x² − 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-17 -->

Explanation: Translating up gives y = x² + 4. Reflecting in the x-axis negates the whole expression: y = −(x² + 4) = −x² − 4. Reflecting first would give −x² + 4.

**A283.** D. y = x² − 2x
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-18 -->

Explanation: Replace x with x − 2: (x − 2)² + 2(x − 2) = x² − 4x + 4 + 2x − 4 = x² − 2x. Using x + 2 would give x² + 6x + 8.

**A284.** A. x = ±6
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-19 -->

Explanation: The image is y = (x/3)² − 4. Setting it to 0 gives x² = 36, so x = ±6. Each x-intercept is multiplied by 3.

**A285.** B. 5
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-20 -->

Explanation: At x = 0: 2f(−1) + 3 = 2(1) + 3 = 5. Using f(0) = 0 would incorrectly give 3.

**A286.** C. [2, 8]
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-21 -->

Explanation: We need −1 ≤ x − 3 ≤ 5, so 2 ≤ x ≤ 8. The graph shifts 3 units right, so the domain shifts right as well.

**A287.** D. [−5, 3]
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-22 -->

Explanation: −f has range [−6, 2], and adding 1 gives [−5, 3]. The reflection reverses the order of the endpoints, so [−1, 7] is wrong.

**A288.** A. 3 units right and 2 units up
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-23 -->

Explanation: Completing the square gives y = (x − 3)² + 2. This is a translation of 3 right and 2 up; 11 is not the vertical shift.

**A289.** B. y = √(2 − x)
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-24 -->

Explanation: Reflection gives y = √(−x). Replacing x with x − 2 gives y = √(−(x − 2)) = √(2 − x). √(−x − 2) would come from translating first.

**A290.** C. y = 2(x − 1)² − 2
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-25 -->

Explanation: Write y = a(x − 1)² − 2. At x = 3: 4a − 2 = 6, so a = 2. The vertex (1, −2) gives (x − 1), not (x + 1).

**A291.** A. y = −x³ + x²
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-26 -->

Explanation: Replace x with −x: (−x)³ + (−x)² = −x³ + x². Only the odd-power term changes sign.

**A292.** y = (x − 2)² − 3 [1]. Translation 2 units right and 3 units down, so the vertex is (2, −3) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-27 -->

Explanation:

**A293.** Equation y = 2/(x + 1) − 3 [1]. Asymptotes x = −1 and y = −3 [1]. y-intercept 2 − 3 = −1, so (0, −1); x-intercept 2/(x + 1) = 3 gives x = −1/3, so (−1/3, 0) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-28 -->

Explanation:

**A294.** x/2 = 4 gives x = 8 [1]. y = 3(−6) + 1 = −17, so the image point is (8, −17) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-29 -->

Explanation:

**A295.** Translation: y = (x + 2)²; dilation: y = 3(x + 2)²; reflection: y = −3(x + 2)² [1]. Vertex (−2, 0) [1]. y-intercept: −3(2)² = −12, so (0, −12) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-30 -->

Explanation:

**A296.** g(x) = −√(x/4) + 3 [1]. Domain [0, ∞) [1]. Range (−∞, 3] [1]. x-intercept: √(x/4) = 3, x/4 = 9, so x = 36 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-31 -->

Explanation:

**A297.** Reflection in the x-axis, translation 3 units left and 6 units up [1]. Vertex (−3, 6) [1]. (x + 3)² = 6 gives x = −3 ± √6 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-32 -->

Explanation:

**A298.** Replace x with x − 4 and subtract 1: y = 3(x − 4) − 2 − 1 [1]. y = 3x − 15 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-33 -->

Explanation:

**A299.** y = a(x + 1)² + 3 [1]. At x = 1: 4a + 3 = −5, so a = −2 and y = −2(x + 1)² + 3 [1]. Dilation by factor 2 from the x-axis, reflection in the x-axis, translation 1 unit left and 3 units up [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-34 -->

Explanation:

**A300.** (a) y = 4(x + 1)² − 1 [1]. (b) Dilation by factor 4 from the x-axis, translation 1 unit left and 1 unit down [1]. (c) 4(x + 1)² = 1 gives (x + 1)² = 1/4 [1], so x = −1/2 and x = −3/2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-35 -->

Explanation:

**A301.** Each point (x, y) maps to (x − 1, 2y). x-intercepts (−2, 0) and (4, 0) [1]. Turning point (1, 18) [1]. It is still a maximum since the dilation factor is positive [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-36 -->

Explanation:

**A302.** B. (5, −1)
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-37 -->

Explanation: Solving 2x − 6 = 4 gives x = 5, and the y-value is unchanged. Computing (4 − 6)/2 or 2(4) − 6 applies the transformation in the wrong direction.

**A303.** C. (−1, −4)
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-38 -->

Explanation: The x-value shifts 2 units left to −1, and the y-value becomes −3 − 1 = −4. Shifting right instead of left would give the incorrect x-value 3.

**A304.** Dilation from the y-axis replaces x with x/2, giving y = 4/x² [1]. Reflection and translation give y = −4/(x − 1)² + 4 [1]. Asymptotes x = 1 and y = 4 [1]. Range (−∞, 4) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-trf-39 -->

Explanation:

#### Inverse functions

**A305.** D. (x + 6)/3
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-1 -->

Explanation: Swap and solve: x = 3y − 6, so y = (x + 6)/3. The first option adds in the wrong order (undoing the operations out of sequence).

**A306.** A. [2, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-2 -->

Explanation: The range of f⁻¹ equals the domain of f, which is [2, ∞). The set [1, ∞) is the range of f (and domain of f⁻¹).

**A307.** A. y = x³ + 1
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-3 -->

Explanation: An inverse function exists only if f is one-to-one. y = x³ + 1 passes the horizontal line test; the other three are even, so each output is hit twice.

**A308.** C. (3, 3)
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-4 -->

Explanation: The graphs of f and f⁻¹ are reflections in y = x, so they meet where f(x) = x: 2x − 3 = x gives x = 3, point (3, 3).

**A309.** B. x = 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-5 -->

Explanation: f has horizontal asymptote y = 3. Reflecting in y = x swaps x and y, so f⁻¹(x) = 1/(x − 3) + 2 has vertical asymptote x = 3.

**A310.** Domain of f: x + 3 ≥ 0, so [−3, ∞); range [−1, ∞) [1]. Swap: x = √(y + 3) − 1, so √(y + 3) = x + 1 and y = (x + 1)² − 3 [1]. f⁻¹(x) = (x + 1)² − 3 [1], with domain equal to the range of f, [−1, ∞) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-6 -->

Explanation:

**A311.** (a) f(x) = (x − 2)² − 3 has its turning point at x = 2, so the largest such domain is [2, ∞) [1]. (b) x = (y − 2)² − 3, so y − 2 = √(x + 3) (positive root since y ≥ 2) [1]; f⁻¹(x) = 2 + √(x + 3) [1]; domain [−3, ∞) [1]. Check: f(5) = 6 and f⁻¹(6) = 2 + 3 = 5.
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-7 -->

Explanation:

**A312.** f(f(x)) = ((x + 3)/(x − 1) + 3) / ((x + 3)/(x − 1) − 1) [1]. Numerator: (x + 3 + 3x − 3)/(x − 1) = 4x/(x − 1); denominator: (x + 3 − x + 1)/(x − 1) = 4/(x − 1) [1]. So f(f(x)) = 4x/4 = x, hence f = f⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-8 -->

Explanation:

**A313.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-9 -->

Explanation: An inverse reverses the mapping, so f⁻¹(9) = 4. The value 1/4 confuses an inverse function with a reciprocal.

**A314.** C. one-to-one
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-10 -->

Explanation: Each output must come from exactly one input, which is the one-to-one (horizontal line) test. Continuity is not enough: y = x² is continuous but has no inverse function over R.

**A315.** D. ∛(x + 8)
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-11 -->

Explanation: Swap and solve: x = y³ − 8, so y³ = x + 8 and y = ∛(x + 8). The option ∛(x − 8) takes the cube root without first adding 8.

**A316.** B. (5, 2)
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-12 -->

Explanation: Reflecting in y = x swaps the coordinates, so (2, 5) becomes (5, 2). Taking reciprocals of the coordinates is a common confusion between inverse and reciprocal.

**A317.** C. f⁻¹(x) = −√x
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-13 -->

Explanation: The range of f⁻¹ is the domain of f, (−∞, 0], so only the negative root is allowed: f⁻¹(x) = −√x. The positive root would give outputs that are not in (−∞, 0].

**A318.** D. (−∞, −1]
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-14 -->

Explanation: The turning point is at x = −1, so f is one-to-one on (−∞, −1]. The value −4 is the y-coordinate of the turning point, not its x-coordinate.

**A319.** A. [2, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-15 -->

Explanation: The domain of f⁻¹ equals the range of f. Since √(x − 1) ≥ 0, the range of f is [2, ∞). The set [1, ∞) is the domain of f.

**A320.** B. log₃(x)
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-16 -->

Explanation: Swapping gives x = 3ʸ, which by definition means y = log₃(x). Raising x to the power 3 is a different operation, and 1/3ˣ is the reflection in the y-axis, not in y = x.

**A321.** D. f⁻¹(x) = 4 − x
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-17 -->

Explanation: Swapping gives x = 4 − y, so y = 4 − x. The function is its own inverse, since f(f(x)) = 4 − (4 − x) = x.

**A322.** A. y = −2
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-18 -->

Explanation: Solving x = (y − 1)/(y + 2) gives f⁻¹(x) = (−2x − 1)/(x − 1), which has horizontal asymptote y = −2. The vertical asymptote of f (x = −2) becomes the horizontal asymptote of f⁻¹.

**A323.** B. f⁻¹(x) = −3 + √(x + 9)
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-19 -->

Explanation: Completing the square gives f(x) = (x + 3)² − 9, so x = (y + 3)² − 9 and y = −3 ± √(x + 9). Since y ≥ −3, the positive root is used.

**A324.** C. 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-20 -->

Explanation: f⁻¹(x) = (x − 1)/3, so f⁻¹(10) = 9/3 = 3. The value 31 is f(10), which applies f instead of f⁻¹.

**A325.** D. f(x) = x + 4 and g(x) = x − 4
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-21 -->

Explanation: Inverses satisfy f(g(x)) = g(f(x)) = x. Here (x − 4) + 4 = x and (x + 4) − 4 = x. In the pair 3x and −3x, the composite is −9x, not x.

**A326.** A. [1, 9]
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-22 -->

Explanation: The domain of f⁻¹ is the range of f, so it is [1, 9]. The set [−2, 5] is the range of f⁻¹.

**A327.** B. 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-23 -->

Explanation: Since x³ is increasing, the graphs meet where x³ = x, giving x = −1, 0 and 1, so three points. Many students find only x = 1 or x = 0 and miss the others.

**A328.** C. f⁻¹(x) = x², x ≥ 0
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-24 -->

Explanation: Swapping gives x = √y, so y = x², and the domain of f⁻¹ is the range of f, which is x ≥ 0. Leaving the domain as R would make f⁻¹ not match the reflection of f.

**A329.** Swap and solve: x = 5 − 2y, so y = (5 − x)/2, giving f⁻¹(x) = (5 − x)/2 [1]. Domain of f⁻¹ is R [1]. Range of f⁻¹ is R [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-25 -->

Explanation:

**A330.** Swap: x = 2/(y + 1) − 3, so x + 3 = 2/(y + 1) [1]. Then y + 1 = 2/(x + 3), so f⁻¹(x) = 2/(x + 3) − 1 [1]. Domain of f⁻¹ = range of f = R \ {−3} [1]. Range of f⁻¹ = domain of f = R \ {−1} [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-26 -->

Explanation:

**A331.** g(h(x)) = 3 × (x + 7)/3 − 7 = x + 7 − 7 = x [1]. h(g(x)) = (3x − 7 + 7)/3 = x, so h = g⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-27 -->

Explanation:

**A332.** Swap: x = (y + 1)² − 5, so (y + 1)² = x + 5 [1]. Since y ≤ −1, y + 1 = −√(x + 5), so f⁻¹(x) = −1 − √(x + 5) [1]. Domain of f⁻¹ = range of f = [−5, ∞) [1]. Range of f⁻¹ = domain of f = (−∞, −1] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-28 -->

Explanation:

**A333.** Swap: x = √(2y + 3), so x² = 2y + 3 and f⁻¹(x) = (x² − 3)/2 [1]. Domain of f⁻¹ = range of f = [0, ∞) [1]. Intersection lies on y = x: √(2x + 3) = x, so 2x + 3 = x², i.e. (x − 3)(x + 1) = 0 [1]. x = −1 is rejected since √1 ≠ −1, so the point is (3, 3) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-29 -->

Explanation:

**A334.** Swap: x = (3y + 1)/(y − 2), so xy − 2x = 3y + 1 [1]. Then y(x − 3) = 2x + 1, so f⁻¹(x) = (2x + 1)/(x − 3) [1]. Domain of f⁻¹ = range of f = R \ {3} [1]. Range of f⁻¹ = domain of f = R \ {2} [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-30 -->

Explanation:

**A335.** A horizontal line such as y = 0 cuts the graph at two points [1], so f is not one-to-one, e.g. f(−2) = f(2) = 0 [1]. Restricting the domain to x ≥ 0 (or x ≤ 0) makes f one-to-one, so an inverse exists [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-31 -->

Explanation:

**A336.** Swap: x = 2ʸ + 1, so 2ʸ = x − 1 and f⁻¹(x) = log₂(x − 1) [1]. Domain of f⁻¹ = range of f = (1, ∞) [1]. Range of f⁻¹ = domain of f = R [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-32 -->

Explanation:

**A337.** Swap: x = (y − 1)³ + 2, so (y − 1)³ = x − 2 [1]. f⁻¹(x) = 1 + ∛(x − 2) [1]. f⁻¹(10) = 1 + ∛8 = 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-33 -->

Explanation:

**A338.** For x ≤ 3, |x − 3| = 3 − x, so f(x) = 4 − x [1]. Swap: x = 4 − y, so f⁻¹(x) = 4 − x [1]. Domain of f⁻¹ = range of f = [1, ∞) [1]. Range of f⁻¹ = domain of f = (−∞, 3] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-34 -->

Explanation:

**A339.** Make C the subject: F − 32 = 1.8C [1], so C = (F − 32)/1.8 [1]. When F = 86, C = 54/1.8 = 30°C [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-35 -->

Explanation:

**A340.** Swap: x = −√(4 − y), so x² = 4 − y [1]. f⁻¹(x) = 4 − x² [1]. Domain of f⁻¹ = range of f = (−∞, 0] [1]. Range of f⁻¹ = domain of f = (−∞, 4] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-36 -->

Explanation:

**A341.** D. f⁻¹(x) = (3x − 1)/2
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-37 -->

Explanation: Swapping gives x = (2y + 1)/3, so y = (3x − 1)/2. Taking the reciprocal is not the same as finding an inverse function.

**A342.** A. 3
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-38 -->

Explanation: Solving x² + 2 = 11 gives x² = 9, and the domain restriction means x = 3 only. The value −3 is not in the domain of f.

**A343.** f(x) = (x + 2)² − 4 so f⁻¹(x) = √(x + 4) − 2 [1]. Domain of f⁻¹ is [−4, ∞) (the range of f) [1]. Solve f(x) = x: x² + 3x = 0 so x = 0 or x = −3 [1]. x = −3 is outside the domain, so the intersection is (0, 0) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a1-inv-39 -->

Explanation:

### Exponential and logarithmic functions

#### Index laws

**A344.** D. 2x⁵/y³
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-1 -->

Explanation: (2x³y⁻²)³ = 8x⁹y⁻⁶. Dividing by 4x⁴y⁻³ gives 2x⁵y⁻³ = 2x⁵/y³. The y index is −6 − (−3) = −3, not −9 or +3.

**A345.** A. 1/9
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-2 -->

Explanation: 27^(−2/3) = 1/(27^(1/3))² = 1/3² = 1/9. A negative index gives a reciprocal, not a negative number.

**A346.** A. 2^(3x+3)
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-3 -->

Explanation: 8 = 2³, so 8^(x+1) = 2^(3(x+1)) = 2^(3x+3). The index 3 multiplies the whole exponent (x + 1), not just x.

**A347.** A. 2√3
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-4 -->

Explanation: √48 = 4√3 and √12 = 2√3, so the difference is 2√3. Surds cannot be subtracted as √(48 − 12) = √36.

**A348.** A. 3(√5 + 1)/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-5 -->

Explanation: Multiply top and bottom by (√5 + 1): 6(√5 + 1)/(5 − 1) = 6(√5 + 1)/4 = 3(√5 + 1)/2.

**A349.** Write both sides with base 3: 3^(2x) = 3^(3(x − 1)) = 3^(3x − 3); equate indices: 2x = 3x − 3; x = 3 (check: 9³ = 729 = 27²).
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-6 -->

Explanation:

**A350.** (a^(1/2) b⁻¹)⁴ = a²b⁻⁴; (a^(3/2) b²)⁻² = a⁻³b⁻⁴; product = a^(2 − 3) b^(−4 − 4) = a⁻¹b⁻⁸ = 1/(a b⁸).
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-7 -->

Explanation:

**A351.** Common denominator: [(2 − √3) + (2 + √3)] / [(2 + √3)(2 − √3)]; numerator = 4; denominator = 4 − 3 = 1; so the sum = 4/1 = 4.
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-8 -->

Explanation:

**A352.** B. a¹²
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-9 -->

Explanation: Raising a power to a power multiplies the indices: 3 × 4 = 12. Adding the indices gives a⁷, which is the rule for multiplying.

**A353.** C. 6/5
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-10 -->

Explanation: 5⁰ = 1 and 5⁻¹ = 1/5, so the sum is 6/5. Treating 5⁰ as 0 would give 1/5.

**A354.** D. 8
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-11 -->

Explanation: 16^(3/4) = (16^(1/4))³ = 2³ = 8. Multiplying 16 by 3/4 to get 12 is not an index law.

**A355.** A. 1/x
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-12 -->

Explanation: The index is 5 − 2 − 4 = −1, so the result is x⁻¹ = 1/x. Forgetting to subtract the divisor's index gives x³.

**A356.** B. 2^(2x+1)
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-13 -->

Explanation: Multiplying powers with the same base adds indices: x + (x + 1) = 2x + 1. The base stays 2; it is not multiplied to 4.

**A357.** C. 5√3
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-14 -->

Explanation: √75 = √(25 × 3) = 5√3. The perfect square 25 comes out as its square root 5, not 25.

**A358.** D. 24√3
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-15 -->

Explanation: 3√2 × 4√6 = 12√12 = 12 × 2√3 = 24√3. Adding the coefficients and radicands is incorrect for multiplication.

**A359.** A. −3/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-16 -->

Explanation: Writing both sides in base 2: 2^(2x) = 2⁻³, so 2x = −3 and x = −3/2. Dividing by the wrong index gives −2/3.

**A360.** B. 3/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-17 -->

Explanation: 25 = 5², so 2x − 1 = 2 and x = 3/2. Setting 2x = 2 forgets the −1.

**A361.** C. 9/4
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-18 -->

Explanation: A negative index inverts the fraction: (2/3)⁻² = (3/2)² = 9/4. It does not make the value negative.

**A362.** D. 25
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-19 -->

Explanation: Adding the indices gives 1/2 + 3/2 = 2, so the product is 5² = 25. Multiplying the indices would give 5^(3/4).

**A363.** A. x^(2/3)
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-20 -->

Explanation: The cube root gives denominator 3 and the square gives numerator 2, so x^(2/3). The reciprocal 3/2 reverses the roles.

**A364.** C. 7
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-21 -->

Explanation: The difference of two squares gives 3² − (√2)² = 9 − 2 = 7. Adding 9 and 2 gives 11, which ignores the negative sign.

**A365.** D. 20
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-22 -->

Explanation: 2^(x+2) = 2ˣ × 2² = 5 × 4 = 20. Adding 2 to 5 treats an index as a term.

**A366.** B. 2a²b²
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-23 -->

Explanation: 6 ÷ 3 = 2, a⁴⁻² = a², and b⁻¹⁻⁽⁻³⁾ = b². Adding the b indices instead gives b⁻⁴.

**A367.** C. x + 2 + 1/x
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-24 -->

Explanation: Expanding gives x + 2·x^(1/2)·x^(−1/2) + x⁻¹ = x + 2 + 1/x. Dropping the middle term is the common error of squaring each part only.

**A368.** A. 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-25 -->

Explanation: 2⁻³ = 1/8 and 4² = 16, so the product is 2. Reading 2⁻³ as −8 gives −128 instead.

**A369.** (3x²y⁻¹)² = 9x⁴y⁻² [1]; (x⁻³y)³ = x⁻⁹y³ [1]; product = 9x⁻⁵y = 9y/x⁵ [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-26 -->

Explanation:

**A370.** 27^(2/3) = (∛27)² = 9 [1]; 16^(−1/2) = 1/4, so the result is 9 − 1/4 = 35/4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-27 -->

Explanation:

**A371.** Let u = 2ˣ so u² − 5u + 4 = 0 [1]; (u − 1)(u − 4) = 0 so u = 1 or u = 4 [1]; 2ˣ = 1 gives x = 0 [1]; 2ˣ = 4 gives x = 2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-28 -->

Explanation:

**A372.** (√2)⁵ = 2^(5/2) [1]; 2^(5/2) ÷ 2^(−1/2) = 2^(5/2 + 1/2) = 2³ = 8 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-29 -->

Explanation:

**A373.** Multiply top and bottom by (3 + √5) [1]; denominator = 9 − 5 = 4 [1]; 10(3 + √5)/4 = (15 + 5√5)/2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-30 -->

Explanation:

**A374.** √18 = 3√2, √50 = 5√2, √8 = 2√2 [1]; 3√2 + 5√2 − 2√2 = 6√2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-31 -->

Explanation:

**A375.** (√7 + 2)² = 11 + 4√7 and (√7 − 2)² = 11 − 4√7 [1]; difference = 8√7 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-32 -->

Explanation:

**A376.** 4ˣ = (2ˣ)² = a² [1]; 2^(x+1) = 2 × 2ˣ = 2a, so the expression is a² + 2a [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-33 -->

Explanation:

**A377.** 1/81 = 3⁻⁴ [1]; x + 1 = −4 so x = −5 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-34 -->

Explanation:

**A378.** (8a³)^(2/3) = 8^(2/3) a² = 4a² [1]; (a^(1/2))² = a [1]; 4a² ÷ a = 4a [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-35 -->

Explanation:

**A379.** Multiply top and bottom by (2 + √3) [1]; denominator = 4 − 3 = 1 [1]; numerator (2 + √3)² = 7 + 4√3, so a = 7, b = 4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-36 -->

Explanation:

**A380.** B. 3^(−x − 2)
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-37 -->

Explanation: 9^(x − 1) = 3^(2x − 2) and 27^x = 3^(3x), so dividing gives 3^(2x − 2 − 3x) = 3^(−x − 2). Adding the indices would suit multiplication, not division.

**A381.** C. 2√6/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-38 -->

Explanation: Multiplying top and bottom by √6 gives 4√6/6 = 2√6/3. Forgetting to divide by the new denominator 6 gives the incorrect 4√6.

**A382.** Multiply top and bottom by (√5 + √2) [1]. Numerator (√5 + √2)² = 5 + 2√10 + 2 = 7 + 2√10 [1]. Denominator 5 − 2 = 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-idx-39 -->

Explanation:

#### Exponential graphs

**A383.** B. horizontal asymptote y = −4 and y-intercept (0, −1)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-1 -->

Explanation: As x → −∞, 2^x → 0 so f → −4: asymptote y = −4. f(0) = 3 × 1 − 4 = −1.

**A384.** A. y = 2^(x − 3) + 1
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-2 -->

Explanation: Translating right by 3 replaces x by (x − 3); translating up by 1 adds 1. So y = 2^(x − 3) + 1.

**A385.** D. It is strictly decreasing with horizontal asymptote y = 0.
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-3 -->

Explanation: Since 0 < 1/2 < 1 the function is decreasing; f(0) = 1; f(x) → 0 as x → ∞, so y = 0 is the asymptote.

**A386.** A. a = 2, b = 3
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-4 -->

Explanation: Dividing: ab³/(ab) = b² = 54/6 = 9, so b = 3 (b > 0). Then 3a = 6 gives a = 2.

**A387.** A. (−∞, 5)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-5 -->

Explanation: 2^x > 0 for all x, so −2^x < 0 and f(x) < 5. f takes every value below 5, so the range is (−∞, 5).

**A388.** (a) y = −8; (b) f(0) = 2 − 8 = −6, so (0, −6); (c) 2^(x + 1) = 8 = 2³, so x + 1 = 3 and x = 2, giving (2, 0); (d) range (−8, ∞).
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-6 -->

Explanation:

**A389.** Translate 1 unit right, reflect in the x-axis, translate 3 units up; asymptote y = 3; y-intercept: −2⁻¹ + 3 = 5/2, i.e. (0, 2.5).
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-7 -->

Explanation:

**A390.** Asymptote gives c = −2; at x = 0: a + c = 1 so a = 3; rule y = 3 × 2^x − 2; at x = 2: y = 3 × 4 − 2 = 10.
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-8 -->

Explanation:

**A391.** A. (−1, 1/5)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-9 -->

Explanation: 5⁻¹ = 1/5, so (−1, 1/5) lies on the graph. The y-value at x = −1 is positive, so (−1, −5) is wrong.

**A392.** B. y = 5
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-10 -->

Explanation: Horizontal translation does not move a horizontal asymptote; the 2^(x − 3) part tends to 0, so y → 5. The 3 only shifts the graph right.

**A393.** C. y = 3^(−x)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-11 -->

Explanation: Reflection in the y-axis replaces x by −x, giving y = 3^(−x). Reflection in the x-axis would give y = −3^x.

**A394.** D. (−3, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-12 -->

Explanation: 4^x > 0 for all x, so f(x) > −3, and every value above −3 is reached. The range is (−3, ∞).

**A395.** A. 0
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-13 -->

Explanation: 3^x > 0, so 3^x + 2 > 2 for all x and the graph never meets the x-axis. Solving 3^x = −2 has no real solution.

**A396.** B. (0, 4)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-14 -->

Explanation: At x = 0, y = 5 − 2⁰ = 5 − 1 = 4. Forgetting that 2⁰ = 1 gives the tempting (0, 5).

**A397.** C. f(x) = 2^(−x)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-15 -->

Explanation: 2^(−x) = (1/2)^x has base between 0 and 1, so it decreases. A base greater than 1, as in (3/2)^x, gives an increasing function.

**A398.** D. a = 5, b = 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-16 -->

Explanation: At x = 0, a = 5. Then 5b² = 20 gives b² = 4, so b = 2. Taking b = 4 confuses b² with b.

**A399.** A. y = 4^x
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-17 -->

Explanation: 2^(2x) = (2²)^x = 4^x. The rule 2 × 2^x is 2^(x + 1), which is a different function.

**A400.** B. y = −3
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-18 -->
