Explanation: As x → ∞, 2^(−x) → 0, so f(x) → −3. The graph approaches the line y = −3, not the x-axis.

**A401.** C. 2 units to the left
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-19 -->

Explanation: 4 × 2^x = 2² × 2^x = 2^(x + 2), which is a translation 2 units to the left. Translating right would give 2^(x − 2).

**A402.** D. (0, 1)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-20 -->

Explanation: 2^x = 2^(−x) only when x = −x, so x = 0, and y = 2⁰ = 1. The graphs are reflections of each other in the y-axis.

**A403.** A. 9f(x)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-21 -->

Explanation: 3^(x + 2) = 3^x × 3² = 9 × 3^x = 9f(x). Adding 2 to the index multiplies the output; it does not add to it.

**A404.** B. (−2, 9)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-22 -->

Explanation: (1/3)^(−2) = 3² = 9, so (−2, 9) lies on the graph. A negative index inverts the base, it does not make the output negative.

**A405.** C. y = 3 × 2^x − 1
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-23 -->

Explanation: Asymptote y = −1 needs the form a × 2^x − 1, and f(0) = a − 1 = 2 gives a = 3. The rule 2^x − 1 has y-intercept (0, 0).

**A406.** D. [2, ∞)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-24 -->

Explanation: f is increasing and f(0) = 1 + 1 = 2, which is included. So the range is [2, ∞); the value 1 is only approached when x → −∞, which is excluded.

**A407.** A. Its graph passes through (0, 1).
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-25 -->

Explanation: b⁰ = 1 for any base, so (0, 1) is always on the graph. It is only increasing when b > 1, and the asymptote is horizontal (y = 0).

**A408.** B. 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-26 -->

Explanation: 2³ + k = 10 gives 8 + k = 10, so k = 2. Subtracting 3 instead of 2³ gives the tempting k = 7.

**A409.** C. y = −2^x + 3
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-27 -->

Explanation: Reflection in the x-axis negates the whole output: y = −(2^x − 3) = −2^x + 3. Negating only the 2^x term is incomplete.

**A410.** Horizontal asymptote y = 2 [1]; y-intercept: 4 × 1 + 2 = 6, so (0, 6) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-28 -->

Explanation:

**A411.** Set y = 0: 5 × 2^x = 20, so 2^x = 4 [1]; 2^x = 2², so x = 2 and the intercept is (2, 0) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-29 -->

Explanation:

**A412.** Substitute: 9a = 18 [1]; a = 2 [1]; y = 2 × 3⁻¹ = 2/3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-30 -->

Explanation:

**A413.** b³ = 64, so b = 4 [1]; b > 1 so the function is increasing [1]; y = 4⁻² = 1/16 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-31 -->

Explanation:

**A414.** They share the y-intercept (0, 1) (or the horizontal asymptote y = 0) [1]; (1/2)^x = 2^(−x), so one is the reflection of the other in the y-axis [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-32 -->

Explanation:

**A415.** (a) y = 2 [1]; (b) f(0) = 3⁻¹ + 2 = 7/3, so (0, 7/3) [1]; (c) 3^(x − 1) > 0 so f(x) > 2 for all x and never equals 0 [1]; (d) range (2, ∞) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-33 -->

Explanation:

**A416.** Translate 1 unit to the left [1]; dilate by factor 2 from the x-axis [1]; translate 5 units down [1]; asymptote y = −5 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-34 -->

Explanation:

**A417.** a + c = 4 and 8a + c = 25 [1]; subtracting: 7a = 21, so a = 3 and c = 1 [1]; rule y = 3 × 2^x + 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-35 -->

Explanation:

**A418.** Equate: 2^x + 1 = 3 × 2^x − 7, so 2 × 2^x = 8 [1]; 2^x = 4, so x = 2 [1]; y = 4 + 1 = 5, so the point is (2, 5) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-36 -->

Explanation:

**A419.** P(0) = 200 [1]; P(2) = 200 × 2.25 = 450 [1]; horizontal asymptote P = 0 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-37 -->

Explanation:

**A420.** (a) f(0) = 6 − 1 = 5, so (0, 5) [1]; (b) as x → ∞, 2^(−x) → 0, so y = 6 [1]; (c) 2^(−x) > 0 so f(x) < 6; range (−∞, 6) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-38 -->

Explanation:

**A421.** Equation y = 3^(−x) − 4 [1]; asymptote y = −4 [1]; y-intercept: 1 − 4 = −3, so (0, −3) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expg-39 -->

Explanation:

#### Logarithms and log laws

**A422.** D. 5
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-1 -->

Explanation: log₂(40/5 × 4) = log₂ 32 = 5, since 2⁵ = 32.

**A423.** C. p + 2q
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-2 -->

Explanation: 18 = 2 × 3², so log₁₀ 18 = log₁₀ 2 + 2 log₁₀ 3 = p + 2q.

**A424.** B. 1/25
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-3 -->

Explanation: log₅ x = −2 means x = 5⁻² = 1/25. The log of a positive number can be negative, but x itself must be positive.

**A425.** B. log_a 6
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-4 -->

Explanation: 2 log_a 3 = log_a 9. Then log_a(9 × 4 / 6) = log_a 6.

**A426.** A. log₂(x − 1)
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-5 -->

Explanation: Swap x and y: x = 2^y + 1, so 2^y = x − 1 and y = log₂(x − 1).

**A427.** Combine: log₂[x(x − 2)] = 3 so x(x − 2) = 8; x² − 2x − 8 = 0, (x − 4)(x + 2) = 0; x = 4 or −2; reject x = −2 as log₂ x is undefined; x = 4.
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-6 -->

Explanation:

**A428.** (a) log₄ 32 = log₂ 32 / log₂ 4 = 5/2; (b) log₅ 9 = log₃ 9 / log₃ 5, so the product = log₃ 9 = 2.
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-7 -->

Explanation:

**A429.** Need x − 3 > 0, so domain x > 3 (i.e. (3, ∞)); vertical asymptote x = 3; f(x) = 0: log₂(x − 3) = −1, x − 3 = 1/2, x = 7/2.
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-8 -->

Explanation:

**A430.** A. 4
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-9 -->

Explanation: 3⁴ = 81, so log₃ 81 = 4. Answering 27 confuses the logarithm with the power 3³.

**A431.** B. −3
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-10 -->

Explanation: 0.001 = 10⁻³, so log₁₀ 0.001 = −3. The answer is negative because the number is less than 1.

**A432.** C. 4³ = 64
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-11 -->

Explanation: log_a b = c means a^c = b, so log₄ 64 = 3 gives 4³ = 64. Writing 3⁴ swaps the base and the logarithm.

**A433.** D. 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-12 -->

Explanation: log₆ 4 + log₆ 9 = log₆ 36 = 2, since 6² = 36. The logs of the numbers are added by multiplying the numbers, not by adding them.

**A434.** A. 5
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-13 -->

Explanation: log₂ 96 − log₂ 3 = log₂ 32 = 5. Subtracting logs means dividing the numbers, not subtracting them.

**A435.** B. 6
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-14 -->

Explanation: 3 log₂ 4 = 3 × 2 = 6, since 2² = 4. Multiplying 3 by 4 to get 12 forgets that log₂ 4 = 2.

**A436.** C. 1
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-15 -->

Explanation: log₇ 1 = 0 and log₇ 7 = 1, so the sum is 1. Adding the arguments to get 8 is a common error.

**A437.** A. x = log₂ 20
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-16 -->

Explanation: 2^x = 20 means x = log₂ 20. Dividing 20 by 2 does not undo an exponent.

**A438.** B. 7
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-17 -->

Explanation: The functions 5^x and log₅ x are inverses, so 5^(log₅ 7) = 7.

**A439.** C. 3/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-18 -->

Explanation: log₉ 27 = log₃ 27 / log₃ 9 = 3/2. Inverting the fraction to get 2/3 is the common slip.

**A440.** D. x > −4
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-19 -->

Explanation: The argument must be positive, so x + 4 > 0 and x > −4. The value x = −4 is excluded because log₂ 0 is undefined.

**A441.** A. x = 1
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-20 -->

Explanation: x(x + 9) = 10 gives x² + 9x − 10 = 0, so x = 1 or x = −10. Since x must be positive, x = −10 is rejected.

**A442.** B. 3^x
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-21 -->

Explanation: Swapping x and y gives x = log₃ y, so y = 3^x. The reciprocal of a log is not its inverse.

**A443.** C. 1 − p
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-22 -->

Explanation: 5 = 10 ÷ 2, so log₁₀ 5 = log₁₀ 10 − log₁₀ 2 = 1 − p. Adding p would be log₁₀ 20.

**A444.** D. log₂(x/y)
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-23 -->

Explanation: The quotient law gives log₂ x − log₂ y = log₂(x/y). The quotient of the two logs is a different quantity.

**A445.** A. x = (log₃ 7)/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-24 -->

Explanation: 3^(2x) = 7 gives 2x = log₃ 7, so x = (log₃ 7)/2. The 2 divides the logarithm; it does not multiply it.

**A446.** B. x = 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-25 -->

Explanation: The argument x − 2 is zero at x = 2, so the asymptote is x = 2. The +3 shifts the graph vertically and does not move the asymptote.

**A447.** C. 7
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-26 -->

Explanation: log_a(x²y) = 2 log_a x + log_a y = 4 + 3 = 7. Doing 2 × 3 = 6 mistreats the product.

**A448.** log₂(1/8) = log₂ 2⁻³ = −3. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-27 -->

Explanation:

**A449.** 2 log₃ 5 = log₃ 25, so the first two terms cancel to 0. [1] Remaining term log₃ 9 = 2. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-28 -->

Explanation:

**A450.** 3x − 1 = 5² = 25. [1] 3x = 26, so x = 26/3 (valid as 3x − 1 > 0). [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-29 -->

Explanation:

**A451.** Take logs of both sides: x = log₁₀ 50 / log₁₀ 3 (or equivalent). [1] x = 1.69897 / 0.47712 [1] x ≈ 3.56. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-30 -->

Explanation:

**A452.** Combine: log₂((x + 6)/x) = 2. [1] (x + 6)/x = 4, so x + 6 = 4x. [1] x = 2 (valid, as x > 0 and x + 6 > 0). [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-31 -->

Explanation:

**A453.** (a) 12 = 2² × 3, so log_a 12 = 2(0.35) + 0.55. [1] = 1.25. [1] (b) log_a(2/3) = 0.35 − 0.55 = −0.2. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-32 -->

Explanation:

**A454.** Swap x and y: x = 2 log₅ y − 1. [1] log₅ y = (x + 1)/2. [1] f⁻¹(x) = 5^((x + 1)/2). [1] Domain R (all reals), range (0, ∞). [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-33 -->

Explanation:

**A455.** log_a(1/b) = log_a 1 − log_a b by the quotient law. [1] Since log_a 1 = 0, this equals −log_a b. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-34 -->

Explanation:

**A456.** 500 × 1.5^t = 4000, so 1.5^t = 8. [1] t = log₁₀ 8 / log₁₀ 1.5 = 0.90309 / 0.17609. [1] t ≈ 5.13 hours. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-35 -->

Explanation:

**A457.** (a) log₂(x²/y) = 2 log₂ x − log₂ y = 6 − 5. [1] = 1. [1] (b) log₂ √(xy) = ½(3 + 5) = 4. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-36 -->

Explanation:

**A458.** Take logs: (x − 1) log 5 = x log 3. [1] x(log 5 − log 3) = log 5. [1] x = log 5 / log(5/3) = 0.69897 / 0.22185. [1] x ≈ 3.15. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-37 -->

Explanation:

**A459.** D. 2m + 1
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-38 -->

Explanation: log_a(25a) = log_a 25 + log_a a = 2m + 1. The term log_a a equals 1 and cannot be dropped.

**A460.** log₃[(x + 2)(x − 4)] = 3 so (x + 2)(x − 4) = 27 [1]. x² − 2x − 35 = 0 so x = 7 or x = −5 [1]. x = −5 is rejected because x − 4 must be positive, so x = 7 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-log-39 -->

Explanation:

#### Exponential equations and modelling

**A461.** B. x = 1 and x = 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-1 -->

Explanation: Let u = 2^x: u² − 6u + 8 = 0, so (u − 2)(u − 4) = 0 and u = 2 or 4. Hence x = 1 or x = 2 (both valid as u > 0).

**A462.** A. x = log₅ 12
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-2 -->

Explanation: Taking log base 5 of both sides: x = log₅ 12 (= log 12 / log 5 ≈ 1.544).

**A463.** A. starts at $500 and grows by 4% each year
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-3 -->

Explanation: At t = 0, A = 500. Each year multiplies by 1.04, a 4% increase of the current amount (not a fixed $4).

**A464.** A. 60 days
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-4 -->

Explanation: 12.5% = 1/8 = (1/2)³, so t/20 = 3 and t = 60 days (three half-lives of 20 days).

**A465.** A. $2676.45
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-5 -->

Explanation: 2000 × 1.06⁵ = 2000 × 1.33823 ≈ $2676.45. $2600 results from simple interest (30%).

**A466.** (a) N(0) = 200; (b) N(4) = 200 × 3² = 1800; (c) 3^(t/2) = 5400/200 = 27 = 3³, so t/2 = 3 and t = 6 hours.
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-6 -->

Explanation:

**A467.** (a) V = 30 000 × 0.85³ = 30 000 × 0.614125 = $18 423.75; (b) solve 0.85^t = 0.5: t = log 0.5 / log 0.85 ≈ 4.27 years (value is below half after about 4.27 years).
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-7 -->

Explanation:

**A468.** Let u = 3^x: u² − 4u − 45 = 0, (u − 9)(u + 5) = 0; u = 9 or u = −5; 3^x > 0 so reject −5; 3^x = 9 gives x = 2.
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-8 -->

Explanation:

**A469.** A. x = 5
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-9 -->

Explanation: 32 = 2⁵, so x = 5. Dividing 32 by 2 gives 16, but that treats the equation as linear.

**A470.** B. x = 3
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-10 -->

Explanation: 81 = 3⁴, so x + 1 = 4 and x = 3. Setting x = 4 forgets to subtract the 1 from the index.

**A471.** C. x = 3/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-11 -->

Explanation: Write 3^(2x) = 3³, so 2x = 3 and x = 3/2. The value x = 3 ignores that 9 = 3².

**A472.** D. x = 0 and x = 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-12 -->

Explanation: Let u = 2^x: u² − 5u + 4 = 0, so u = 1 or 4, giving x = 0 or x = 2. The values u = 1 and 4 are not themselves the solutions for x.

**A473.** A. x = log₂(20/3)
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-13 -->

Explanation: Divide by 3 first: 2^x = 20/3, so x = log₂(20/3) ≈ 2.74. Treating 3 × 2 as 6 is wrong because only the 2 is raised to the power x.

**A474.** B. 926
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-14 -->

Explanation: 800 × 1.05³ = 800 × 1.157625 ≈ 926. Adding 5% of 800 three times (920) is simple, not compound, growth.

**A475.** C. falls by 10% of its value at the start of that year
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-15 -->

Explanation: The multiplier 0.9 means 90% of the previous value is kept, so a 10% fall each year, which is a changing dollar amount.

**A476.** D. 12 hours
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-16 -->

Explanation: 1/16 = (1/2)⁴, so four half-lives are needed: 4 × 3 = 12 hours. Multiplying 16 by 3 confuses the number of half-lives with the fraction.

**A477.** A. x = log_e 7
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-17 -->

Explanation: Taking natural logarithms of both sides gives x = log_e 7 ≈ 1.946. The value e^7 is what x would be if the equation were log_e x = 7.

**A478.** B. 0
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-18 -->

Explanation: 2^x is positive for every real x, so it can never equal −4. Solving 2^x = 4 and negating would be an error.

**A479.** C. x = −3/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-19 -->

Explanation: Write 2^(2x) = 2⁻³, so 2x = −3 and x = −3/2. Positive 3/2 would give 4^x = 8.

**A480.** D. 566
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-20 -->

Explanation: P = 100 × 2^2.5 ≈ 100 × 5.657 ≈ 566. Using 100 × 2.5 × 2 treats the growth as linear.

**A481.** B. x = 2.54
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-21 -->

Explanation: x = log₁₀ 350 ≈ 2.544 (between 2 and 3 since 100 < 350 < 1000). The value 5.86 is log_e 350, the wrong base.

**A482.** C. $6719.58
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-22 -->

Explanation: 5000 × 1.03¹⁰ ≈ 5000 × 1.343916 ≈ $6719.58. The $6500 figure comes from simple interest of $150 per year.

**A483.** D. 10.24 years
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-23 -->

Explanation: Solve 1.07^t = 2: t = log 2 / log 1.07 ≈ 10.24 years. The 14.29 years result is 100/7, which is the simple-interest doubling time.

**A484.** A. a = 3, b = 4
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-24 -->

Explanation: At x = 0, a = 3. Then 3b² = 48, so b² = 16 and b = 4 (b > 0). The value 16 is b², not b.

**A485.** B. x = 0 and x = log_e 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-25 -->

Explanation: Let u = e^x: (u − 1)(u − 2) = 0, so e^x = 1 or 2, giving x = 0 or x = log_e 2. The values u = 1, 2 must still be converted back to x.

**A486.** C. 147.2
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-26 -->

Explanation: N = 400e⁻¹ ≈ 400 × 0.3679 ≈ 147.2. The quantity decays continuously but never reaches 0.

**A487.** Take logs: (x + 1) log 2 = x log 3 [1]; x(log 3 − log 2) = log 2, so x = log 2 / (log 3 − log 2) [1]; x ≈ 1.71 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-27 -->

Explanation:

**A488.** (a) C = 80 × 0.8³ = 80 × 0.512 [1] = 40.96 mg/L [1]; (b) solve 0.8^t = 10/80 = 1/8 [1]; t = log(1/8) / log 0.8 ≈ 9.32 hours [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-28 -->

Explanation:

**A489.** Let u = 5^x: u² − 6u + 5 = 0 [1]; (u − 1)(u − 5) = 0, so u = 1 or u = 5 (both positive) [1]; 5^x = 1 gives x = 0 and 5^x = 5 gives x = 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-29 -->

Explanation:

**A490.** (a) y-intercept gives a = 5 [1]; 5b³ = 40, so b³ = 8 and b = 2 [1]; (b) y = 5 × 2⁵ = 160 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-30 -->

Explanation:

**A491.** Solve 12 000 × 1.06^t = 20 000 [1]; 1.06^t = 5/3 [1]; t = log(5/3) / log 1.06 ≈ 8.8 years [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-31 -->

Explanation:

**A492.** (a) m = 240 × (1/2)^2.5 [1] ≈ 42.4 g [1]; (b) 15/240 = 1/16 = (1/2)⁴ [1]; t/8 = 4, so t = 32 days [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-32 -->

Explanation:

**A493.** e^(3x) = 5, so 3x = log_e 5 and x = (log_e 5)/3 [1]; x ≈ 0.536 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-33 -->

Explanation:

**A494.** (a) 3000 × 1.06² = $3370.80 [1]; (b) 24 months: 3000 × 1.005²⁴ [1] ≈ $3381.48, which is about $10.68 more than annual compounding [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-34 -->

Explanation:

**A495.** Let u = 3^x: 3u² − 28u + 9 = 0 [1]; (3u − 1)(u − 9) = 0 [1]; u = 1/3 or u = 9, both positive [1]; so x = −1 or x = 2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-35 -->

Explanation:

**A496.** (a) e^(3k) = 2 [1], so k = (log_e 2)/3 ≈ 0.231 [1]; (b) 9 hours is three doublings, so the factor is 2³ = 8 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-36 -->

Explanation:

**A497.** A. 20 500
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-37 -->

Explanation: P = 15 000 × 1.04⁸ ≈ 15 000 × 1.3686 ≈ 20 500. Adding 4% of 15 000 for each year (simple growth) would give about 19 800, ignoring compounding.

**A498.** At t = 0, T = 20 + 70 = 90 °C [1]. 70e^(−0.1t) = 20 so e^(−0.1t) = 2/7 [1]. t = 10 log_e(3.5) ≈ 12.5 minutes [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-expm-38 -->

Explanation:

### Circular functions

#### Unit circle and exact values

**A499.** D. −√3/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-1 -->

Explanation: 5π/6 is in quadrant 2 with reference angle π/6, so cos is negative: −cos(π/6) = −√3/2.

**A500.** C. 140°
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-2 -->

Explanation: Multiply by 180/π: 7π/9 × 180/π = 7 × 20 = 140°.

**A501.** C. √3/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-3 -->

Explanation: 7π/6 is in quadrant 3, where tan is positive, with reference angle π/6; tan(π/6) = 1/√3 = √3/3.

**A502.** A. −3/4
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-4 -->

Explanation: In quadrant 2 cos θ < 0: cos θ = −√(1 − 9/25) = −4/5. So tan θ = (3/5)/(−4/5) = −3/4.

**A503.** A. 4π cm
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-5 -->

Explanation: Arc length = rθ = 6 × 2π/3 = 4π cm.

**A504.** 11π/6 is in quadrant 4 with reference angle π/6; sin = −1/2; cos = √3/2; tan = (−1/2)/(√3/2) = −1/√3 = −√3/3.
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A505.** Quadrant 3 so sin θ < 0; sin²θ = 1 − 25/169 = 144/169, sin θ = −12/13; tan θ = sin θ / cos θ = (−12/13)/(−5/13) = 12/5.
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A506.** sin(6π/5) = sin(π + π/5) = −sin(π/5) = −a; sin(4π/5) = sin(π − π/5) = a; cos(3π/10) = cos(π/2 − π/5) = sin(π/5) = a.
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A507.** A. √3/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-9 -->

Explanation: On the unit circle the point at π/3 is (1/2, √3/2), so sin(π/3) = √3/2; 1/2 is sin(π/6).

**A508.** B. 5π/6
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-10 -->

Explanation: Multiply by π/180: 150π/180 = 5π/6; 2π/3 would be 120°.

**A509.** C. −1
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-11 -->

Explanation: The point at angle 3π/2 is (0, −1), so sin = −1; +1 is sin(π/2).

**A510.** D. undefined
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-12 -->

Explanation: At π/2 the point is (0, 1), so tan = sin/cos = 1/0, which is undefined; it is not 0, which is the value of cos(π/2).

**A511.** A. −√2/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-13 -->

Explanation: Negative angles go clockwise into quadrant 4 where sin is negative; the reference angle is π/4 so sin(−π/4) = −√2/2.

**A512.** B. −1/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-14 -->

Explanation: 4π/3 is in quadrant 3 with reference angle π/3, so cos is negative: −cos(π/3) = −1/2; −√3/2 mixes up cos and sin.

**A513.** C. Quadrant 3
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-15 -->

Explanation: cos < 0 in quadrants 2 and 3; tan > 0 in quadrants 1 and 3. Only quadrant 3 satisfies both.

**A514.** D. 5π/4
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-16 -->

Explanation: 225π/180 = 5π/4; 3π/4 is 135°.

**A515.** A. −1
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-17 -->

Explanation: 3π/4 is in quadrant 2 with reference angle π/4; tan(π/4) = 1, and tan is negative in quadrant 2, so −1.

**A516.** B. −5/13
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-18 -->

Explanation: sin²θ = 1 − 144/169 = 25/169; θ is in quadrant 4 so sin θ is negative: −5/13.

**A517.** C. √3/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-19 -->

Explanation: 2π/3 is in quadrant 2 with reference angle π/3; sin is positive there, so √3/2.

**A518.** B. −1/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-20 -->

Explanation: −5π/6 is in quadrant 3 with reference angle π/6, so sin is negative: −sin(π/6) = −1/2.

**A519.** C. 7π/6 and 11π/6
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-21 -->

Explanation: sin is negative in quadrants 3 and 4; with reference angle π/6 the solutions are π + π/6 = 7π/6 and 2π − π/6 = 11π/6.

**A520.** D. √2/4
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-22 -->

Explanation: (√2/2)(1/2) = √2/4.

**A521.** A. 57.3°
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-23 -->

Explanation: π radians = 180°, so 1 radian = 180/π ≈ 57.3°.

**A522.** B. 2 radians
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-24 -->

Explanation: From l = rθ, θ = 10/5 = 2 radians; multiplying instead of dividing gives 50.

**A523.** C. −a
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-25 -->

Explanation: Adding π rotates the point to the opposite side of the circle, negating the x-coordinate, so cos(π + θ) = −a.

**A524.** 210° × π/180 = 7π/6 [1]. 5π/12 × 180/π = 75° [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-26 -->

Explanation:

**A525.** cos is negative in quadrants 2 and 3, reference angle π/4 [1]. θ = 3π/4 and θ = 5π/4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A526.** Quadrant 2: sin > 0, cos < 0; the hypotenuse is √(25 + 144) = 13, so sin θ = 5/13 [1]; cos θ = −12/13 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-28 -->

Explanation:

**A527.** Arc length = rθ = 8 × 3π/4 = 6π cm [1]. Area = ½r²θ = ½ × 64 × 3π/4 = 24π cm² [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-29 -->

Explanation:

**A528.** sin(π/6) = 1/2, cos(π/3) = 1/2, tan(π/4) = 1 [1]. (1/2)(1/2) + 1 = 5/4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-30 -->

Explanation:

**A529.** sin θ = √3/2 [1]. θ = π/3 and θ = 2π/3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-31 -->

Explanation:

**A530.** x² + y² = 1 so y² = 1 − 9/25 = 16/25 [1]. Quadrant 4 so y = −4/5 and sin θ = −4/5 [1]. tan θ = y/x = (−4/5)/(3/5) = −4/3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-32 -->

Explanation:

**A531.** θ = l/r = 6π/9 = 2π/3 radians [1]. In degrees 2π/3 × 180/π = 120° [1]. Area = ½r²θ = ½ × 81 × 2π/3 = 27π cm² [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-33 -->

Explanation:

**A532.** cos(5π/3): quadrant 4, reference angle π/3, cos positive so 1/2 [1]. sin(5π/6): quadrant 2, reference angle π/6, so 1/2 [1]. tan(5π/4): quadrant 3, reference angle π/4, tan positive so 1 [1]. Total = 1/2 + 1/2 − 1 = 0 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-34 -->

Explanation:

**A533.** B. −√2/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-35 -->

Explanation: 5π/4 lies in quadrant 3 with reference angle π/4, where sine is negative, so sin(5π/4) = −sin(π/4) = −√2/2. Choosing √2/2 ignores the sign.

**A534.** D. −1/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-36 -->

Explanation: Cosine is even, so cos(−2π/3) = cos(2π/3), which has reference angle π/3 in quadrant 2 where cosine is negative: −1/2. Using √3/2 confuses the sine and cosine values for π/3.

**A535.** −7π/6 is coterminal with 5π/6 (add 2π), which is in quadrant 2 with reference angle π/6. [1] sin(−7π/6) = 1/2 and cos(−7π/6) = −√3/2. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-37 -->

Explanation:

**A536.** Area = ½r²θ gives 54π = 72θ, so θ = 3π/4. [1] Arc length = rθ = 12 × 3π/4 = 9π cm. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-uc-38 -->

Explanation:

#### Graphs of sine and cosine

**A537.** A. 3 and π
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-1 -->

Explanation: Amplitude = |3| = 3 (the +1 only shifts the graph up). Period = 2π/2 = π.

**A538.** B. [2, 6]
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-2 -->

Explanation: cos x ∈ [−1, 1], so −2cos x ∈ [−2, 2] and adding 4 gives [2, 6].

**A539.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-3 -->

Explanation: Period = 2π ÷ (π/2) = 4.

**A540.** B. y = 3 sin(πx/4) + 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-4 -->

Explanation: Amplitude (5 − (−1))/2 = 3 and mean level (5 + (−1))/2 = 2. Period 8 = 2π/n gives n = π/4.

**A541.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-5 -->

Explanation: cos(2x) = 0 when 2x = π/2, 3π/2, 5π/2, 7π/2 (2x ∈ [0, 4π]), so x = π/4, 3π/4, 5π/4, 7π/4: four intercepts.

**A542.** Write as 2 sin(3(x − π/3)) + 1; amplitude 2; period 2π/3; range [−1, 3]; translated π/3 to the right; y-intercept: 2 sin(−π) + 1 = 1.
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A543.** (0, −1), (π/4, 0), (π/2, 1), (3π/4, 0), (π, −1); one full period (period π), starting at a minimum, rising to a maximum at π/2 then returning; amplitude 1.
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A544.** Period 12 so b = 2π/12 = π/6; a = (6 − 2)/2 = 2; c = 4; h = 2cos(πt/6) + 4; h ≥ 5 means cos(πt/6) ≥ 1/2, so πt/6 ∈ [0, π/3] or [5π/3, 2π]; t ∈ [0, 2] and [10, 12].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A545.** A. 6π
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-9 -->

Explanation: Period = 2π ÷ (1/3) = 6π. 2π/3 comes from multiplying by 1/3 instead of dividing.

**A546.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-10 -->

Explanation: Amplitude is the absolute value of the coefficient, so it is 4; the negative sign only reflects the graph in the x-axis.

**A547.** D. π/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-11 -->

Explanation: sin(2x) = 0 when 2x = π, so x = π/2. Using x = π forgets that the period has been halved.

**A548.** A. [−5, 1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-12 -->

Explanation: 3 sin x ∈ [−3, 3], and subtracting 2 gives [−5, 1]. The range [−3, 3] ignores the vertical translation.

**A549.** B. 8
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-13 -->

Explanation: The maximum is amplitude plus mean level: 5 + 3 = 8. The 2 inside the bracket changes the period, not the maximum.

**A550.** C. π/4 units to the right
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-14 -->

Explanation: Replacing x with (x − π/4) moves the graph π/4 units in the positive x direction, to the right. Left is the common mistake.

**A551.** D. 5π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-15 -->

Explanation: cos(x + π/3) = 1 when x + π/3 = 2π, so x = 5π/3. Setting x + π/3 = 0 gives x = −π/3, which is negative.

**A552.** A. π/2 units to the left
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-16 -->

Explanation: cos(x) = sin(x + π/2), which is a translation of π/2 to the left. Translating right gives −cos(x).

**A553.** B. 1/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-17 -->

Explanation: Period = 2π ÷ 4π = 1/2. The amplitude 2 does not affect the period.

**A554.** C. 3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-18 -->

Explanation: The period is 2π/3, so 2π ÷ (2π/3) = 3 cycles fit in the interval. Six comes from wrongly multiplying the period by 2 instead of dividing.

**A555.** D. factor 1/2 from the y-axis
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-19 -->

Explanation: Replacing x with 2x compresses the graph horizontally, halving the period, so it is a dilation of factor 1/2 from the y-axis. A factor of 2 would stretch it.

**A556.** A. y = 2cos(2x)
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-20 -->

Explanation: Period π gives n = 2π/π = 2, and a cosine graph starts at its maximum. y = 2cos(πx) has period 2, and sin(2x) would start at 0.

**A557.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-21 -->

Explanation: In each period of 2π the line y = 0.3 cuts the sine curve twice, so there are 4 solutions in two periods. Two solutions counts only one period.

**A558.** A. sin(−x) = −sin(x), so y = sin(x) has rotational symmetry about the origin
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-22 -->

Explanation: sin is an odd function, so sin(−x) = −sin(x) and its graph has point symmetry about the origin. cos is even, so cos(−x) = cos(x) and its graph is symmetric about the y-axis.

**A559.** B. π
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-23 -->

Explanation: sin(x/2) = 1 when x/2 = π/2, so x = π. The period is 4π, so 4π is where it returns to zero after a full cycle, not the maximum.

**A560.** C. y = sin(πx)
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-24 -->

Explanation: Period = 2π/π = 2 for y = sin(πx). sin(2πx) has period 1, sin(2x) has period π and sin(x/2) has period 4π.

**A561.** Period = 2π ÷ (1/2) = 4π, so one full cycle is drawn [1]; amplitude 3 gives maximum 3 and minimum −3 [1]; key points (0, 0), (π, 3), (2π, 0), (3π, −3), (4π, 0) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-25 -->

Explanation:

**A562.** Amplitude a = (7 − 1)/2 = 3 [1]; c = (7 + 1)/2 = 4 [1]; b = 2π/4π = 1/2, so y = 3cos(x/2) + 4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-26 -->

Explanation:

**A563.** Period = 2π ÷ (π/12) = 24 hours [1]; minimum 15 − 6 = 9 °C at t = 0 and t = 24 (midnight) [1]; maximum 15 + 6 = 21 °C at t = 12 (noon) [1]; at t = 6, cos(π/2) = 0 so T = 15 °C [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A564.** Dilation of factor 2 from the x-axis [1] and reflection in the x-axis [1]; dilation of factor 3 from the y-axis [1]; period 6π and range [−2, 2] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-28 -->

Explanation:

**A565.** y = cos(2(x + π/6)) − 2, so h = −π/6, k = −2 [1]; translated π/6 units to the left and 2 units down [1]; period 2π/2 = π [1]; range [−3, −1] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-29 -->

Explanation:

**A566.** a = 3 [1]; b = 2π ÷ (2π/3) = 3, so y = 3 sin(3x) [1]; y = 3 sin(π/4) = 3√2/2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-30 -->

Explanation:

**A567.** Maximum when x − π/3 = 0: (π/3, 2) [1]; minimum when x − π/3 = π: (4π/3, −2) [1]; y-intercept: 2cos(−π/3) = 1, so (0, 1) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-31 -->

Explanation:

**A568.** sin(2x) ∈ [−1, 1], so −3 sin(2x) ∈ [−3, 3] and the range is [1, 7] [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-32 -->

Explanation:

**A569.** A. 3 and 8
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-33 -->

Explanation: The amplitude is |−3| = 3 and the period is 2π ÷ (π/4) = 8. An amplitude cannot be negative, and 4 or π/4 results from misusing the formula for the period.

**A570.** C. [−3, 5]
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-34 -->

Explanation: sin(x) lies in [−1, 1], so −4 sin(x) lies in [−4, 4] and adding 1 gives [−3, 5]. The range [−4, 4] forgets the vertical translation.

**A571.** D. y = 3 sin(2x) + 1
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-35 -->

Explanation: The centre is (4 + (−2))/2 = 1, the amplitude is 3 and b = 2π/π = 2, giving 3 sin(2x) + 1. Using 6 gives the full height of the graph rather than the amplitude.

**A572.** Period = 2π ÷ (π/20) = 40 seconds. [1] Maximum height = 12 + 10 = 22 m. [1] Minimum height = 12 − 10 = 2 m. [1] H(10) = 12 − 10cos(π/2) = 12 m. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-36 -->

Explanation:

**A573.** Period π gives b = 2π/π = 2. [1] Substituting, a sin(π/3) = 2, so a(√3/2) = 2. [1] a = 4/√3 = 4√3/3. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-sc-37 -->

Explanation:

#### The tangent function

**A574.** D. π/2
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-1 -->

Explanation: The period of tan(nx) is π/n, so for n = 2 it is π/2.

**A575.** D. x = ±π
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-2 -->

Explanation: Asymptotes occur when x/2 = π/2 + kπ, i.e. x = π + 2kπ. In (−2π, 2π) these are x = ±π.

**A576.** B. x = π/4 + kπ
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-3 -->

Explanation: tan(x − π/4) = 0 when x − π/4 = kπ, so x = π/4 + kπ.

**A577.** C. 3
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-4 -->

Explanation: Asymptotes at 3x = π/2 + kπ, i.e. x = π/6, π/2, 5π/6 inside (0, π): three.

**A578.** B. π/3 and 4π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-5 -->

Explanation: tan x = √3 gives reference angle π/3, and tan is positive in quadrants 1 and 3: x = π/3 and π + π/3 = 4π/3.

**A579.** Period π/2; asymptotes where 2x − π/2 = π/2 + kπ, i.e. x = π/2 + kπ/2, so x = 0, π/2, π; x-intercepts where 2x − π/2 = kπ, i.e. x = π/4 + kπ/2, so x = π/4 and 3π/4.
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-6 -->

Explanation:

**A580.** 2x ∈ [0, 2π]; tan = 1 at π/4 and π/4 + π = 5π/4; so 2x = π/4 or 5π/4; x = π/8 or 5π/8.
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-7 -->

Explanation:

**A581.** tan x = sin x / cos x, where the point on the unit circle is (cos x, sin x); at x = π/2 the point is (0, 1) so cos x = 0 and the quotient is undefined; as x approaches π/2 from below cos x → 0⁺ and tan x → +∞, from above cos x → 0⁻ and tan x → −∞, so the graph has a vertical asymptote.
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-8 -->

Explanation:

**A582.** A. 3π
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-9 -->

Explanation: The period of tan(nx) is π/n, so for n = 1/3 it is π ÷ (1/3) = 3π. Choosing π/3 comes from multiplying instead of dividing.

**A583.** C. all real numbers
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-10 -->

Explanation: On each branch tan x takes every real value, from −∞ to +∞. The restriction −1 ≤ y ≤ 1 belongs to sine and cosine, not tangent.

**A584.** B. −1/√3
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-11 -->

Explanation: The reference angle is π/6 with tan = 1/√3, and tan is negative in the second quadrant. Giving +1/√3 ignores the quadrant sign.

**A585.** D. −√3
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-12 -->

Explanation: tan is an odd function, so tan(−π/3) = −tan(π/3) = −√3. The value √3 forgets the negative sign.

**A586.** B. It is an odd function, with rotational symmetry about the origin
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-13 -->

Explanation: tan(−x) = sin(−x)/cos(−x) = −sin x/cos x = −tan x, so tan is odd. Cosine is the even function, which is where the first option comes from.

**A587.** A. x = π/6 + kπ
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-14 -->

Explanation: Set x + π/3 = π/2 + kπ, giving x = π/6 + kπ. The translation moves the asymptotes π/3 to the left, not to π/3 + kπ.

**A588.** C. period π and the same asymptotes, but steeper branches
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-15 -->

Explanation: A factor of 3 in front dilates the graph from the x-axis only, so the period and asymptotes are unchanged while the branches become steeper. Only a change inside the argument alters the period.

**A589.** D. −3/4
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-16 -->

Explanation: In the second quadrant cos x = −4/5, so tan x = (3/5) ÷ (−4/5) = −3/4. The value 3/4 ignores the negative cosine.

**A590.** B. 1
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-17 -->

Explanation: The period of tan(nx) is π/n, so with n = π it is π/π = 1. Stating π ignores the coefficient of x.

**A591.** A. decreasing and passes through the origin
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-18 -->

Explanation: The negative sign reflects y = tan x in the x-axis, so the increasing branch becomes decreasing, still through (0, 0). The asymptotes remain at x = ±π/2.

**A592.** D. 3
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-19 -->

Explanation: tan(2x) = 0 when 2x = 0, π, 2π, so x = 0, π/2, π, which is three intercepts. Counting only the interior points gives 1, and forgetting the endpoint x = π gives 2.

**A593.** C. π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-20 -->

Explanation: Only the coefficient of x changes the period: π/3. The factor 2 and the shift +1 affect the dilation from the x-axis and the vertical position, not the period.

**A594.** B. y = tan(3x)
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-21 -->

Explanation: The asymptotes are π/3 apart, so the period is π/3 and n = π ÷ (π/3) = 3. Check: 3x = π/2 gives x = π/6. The equation y = tan(6x) would have period π/6.

**A595.** D. undefined
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-22 -->

Explanation: At x = 3π/2, cos x = 0, so tan x = sin x / cos x is undefined; this is a vertical asymptote. The value 0 would need sin x = 0, but sin(3π/2) = −1.

**A596.** A. all real x except x = π/2 + kπ, k ∈ Z
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-23 -->

Explanation: tan x is undefined wherever cos x = 0, which is at every odd multiple of π/2. Excluding only x = kπ confuses the asymptotes with the x-intercepts.

**A597.** C. π/6 and −5π/6
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-24 -->

Explanation: The principal solution is π/6, and subtracting the period π gives π/6 − π = −5π/6. The value 5π/6 has tan = −1/√3, so it is not a solution.

**A598.** B. 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-25 -->

Explanation: At x = 0, y = tan(π/4) + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2. Giving 1 forgets the vertical translation.

**A599.** Period = π/4 [1]. Asymptotes where 4x = π/2 + kπ, so x = π/8 + kπ/4 [1]. In the interval these are x = π/8 and x = 3π/8 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-26 -->

Explanation:

**A600.** Reference angle is π/3, and tan is negative in quadrants 2 and 4 [1]. x = π − π/3 = 2π/3 and x = 2π − π/3 = 5π/3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-27 -->

Explanation:

**A601.** Let u = x − π/6, so −π/6 ≤ u ≤ 11π/6 [1]. tan u = 1 gives u = π/4 or u = 5π/4 [1]. x = u + π/6, so x = 5π/12 or x = 17π/12 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-28 -->

Explanation:

**A602.** tan(−x) = sin(−x) / cos(−x) [1] = −sin x / cos x = −tan x, so tan is an odd function [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-29 -->

Explanation:

**A603.** Period π [1]. Asymptotes where x + π/4 = π/2 + kπ, so x = π/4 + kπ: x = −3π/4 and x = π/4 [1]. x-intercepts where x + π/4 = kπ, so x = −π/4 + kπ: x = −π/4 and x = 3π/4 [1]. y-intercept f(0) = tan(π/4) = 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-30 -->

Explanation:

**A604.** Period π/b = π/2, so b = 2 [1]. Substitute: 3 = a tan(2 × π/8) = a tan(π/4) = a [1]. So a = 3 and b = 2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-31 -->

Explanation:

**A605.** In the third quadrant cos x is negative, so cos x = −√(1 − 25/169) = −12/13 [1]. tan x = sin x / cos x = (−5/13) ÷ (−12/13) = 5/12 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-32 -->

Explanation:

**A606.** tan x = sin x / cos x, where (cos x, sin x) is the point on the unit circle at angle x [1]. Adding π moves to the diametrically opposite point (−cos x, −sin x), so sine and cosine both change sign [1]. The ratio is unchanged, so tan(x + π) = tan x, and π is the smallest such shift [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-33 -->

Explanation:

**A607.** Let u = 3x, so 0 ≤ u ≤ 3π [1]. tan u = −1 gives u = 3π/4, 7π/4, 11π/4 [1]. x = u/3, so x = π/4, 7π/12, 11π/12 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-34 -->

Explanation:

**A608.** tan x = −1/√3 [1]. Reference angle π/6, with tan negative in quadrants 2 and 4 [1]. x = 5π/6 and x = −π/6 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-35 -->

Explanation:

**A609.** Dilation by factor 2 from the x-axis [1]. Dilation by factor 3 from the y-axis [1]. Translation 1 unit down [1]. Period = π ÷ (1/3) = 3π [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-36 -->

Explanation:

**A610.** A. 2
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-37 -->

Explanation: tan(3π/4) = −1, so y = −2 × (−1) = 2. Giving −2 ignores the negative sign of tan(3π/4).

**A611.** The calculator gives the reference solution x = tan⁻¹(2) ≈ 1.11. [1] Tangent has period π, so the second solution is x = 1.11 + π ≈ 4.25. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-tan-38 -->

Explanation:

#### Solving trigonometric equations

**A612.** B. π/3 and 2π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-1 -->

Explanation: sin x = √3/2 gives reference angle π/3; sin is positive in quadrants 1 and 2: x = π/3 and π − π/3 = 2π/3.

**A613.** C. π/3 and 2π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-2 -->

Explanation: 2x ∈ [0, 2π]; cos θ = −1/2 at θ = 2π/3 and 4π/3. Halving: x = π/3 and 2π/3.

**A614.** C. 6
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-3 -->

Explanation: sin(3x) = 1/2 with 3x ∈ [0, 6π]. Each 2π of 3x gives 2 solutions, so 3 × 2 = 6.

**A615.** C. 3π/4 and 7π/4
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-4 -->

Explanation: Reference angle π/4; tan is negative in quadrants 2 and 4: x = π − π/4 = 3π/4 and 2π − π/4 = 7π/4.

**A616.** B. {π/3, π/2, 3π/2, 5π/3}
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-5 -->

Explanation: Factorise: cos x (2cos x − 1) = 0. cos x = 0 gives π/2, 3π/2; cos x = 1/2 gives π/3, 5π/3. Dividing by cos x would lose the first pair.

**A617.** Factorise: (2 sin x − 1)(sin x + 1) = 0; sin x = 1/2 gives x = π/6, 5π/6; sin x = −1 gives x = 3π/2; solutions x = π/6, 5π/6, 3π/2.
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-6 -->

Explanation:

**A618.** cos(2x − π/3) = 1/√2 = √2/2; let θ = 2x − π/3 ∈ [−π/3, 5π/3]; θ = −π/4 or π/4 (7π/4 is outside the interval); x = (θ + π/3)/2, so x = π/24 or x = 7π/24.
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-7 -->

Explanation:

**A619.** T = 23: −6cos(πt/12) = 3, cos(πt/12) = −1/2; πt/12 = 2π/3 or 4π/3 so t = 8 or t = 16; T > 23 when cos is less than −1/2, i.e. 8 < t < 16: for 8 hours.
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-8 -->

Explanation:

**A620.** A. π/6 and 5π/6
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-9 -->

Explanation: The reference angle is π/6 and sine is positive in quadrants 1 and 2, giving π/6 and π − π/6 = 5π/6. 7π/6 is in quadrant 3, where sine is negative.

**A621.** D. π/4 and 7π/4
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-10 -->

Explanation: Cosine is positive in quadrants 1 and 4, so x = π/4 and 2π − π/4 = 7π/4. 3π/4 is tempting but cosine is negative there.

**A622.** A. −π and π
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-11 -->

Explanation: cos x = −1 occurs at odd multiples of π, so x = −π and π in this interval. −2π, 0 and 2π are where cos x = 1.

**A623.** D. π/6 and π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-12 -->

Explanation: 2x ∈ [0, 2π] and sin θ = √3/2 at θ = π/3 and 2π/3. Halving gives x = π/6 and π/3; forgetting to halve gives π/3 and 2π/3.

**A624.** C. π/8 and 5π/8
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-13 -->

Explanation: 2x ∈ [0, 2π] and tan θ = 1 at θ = π/4 and 5π/4, so x = π/8 and 5π/8. 9π/8 lies outside the domain.

**A625.** B. 2π/3 and 10π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-14 -->

Explanation: x/2 ∈ [0, 2π] and cos θ = 1/2 at θ = π/3 and 5π/3. Doubling gives x = 2π/3 and 10π/3.

**A626.** A. π/4, 3π/4, 5π/4, 7π/4
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-15 -->

Explanation: sin²x = 1/2 gives sin x = ±√2/2. The positive value gives π/4 and 3π/4 and the negative value gives 5π/4 and 7π/4, so taking only the positive root loses two solutions.

**A627.** D. {0, π/2, π, 2π}
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-16 -->

Explanation: Rearranging gives sin x (sin x − 1) = 0, so sin x = 0 (x = 0, π, 2π) or sin x = 1 (x = π/2). Dividing by sin x would lose the sin x = 0 solutions.

**A628.** A. π/3, π, 5π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-17 -->

Explanation: Factorising gives (2cos x − 1)(cos x + 1) = 0. cos x = 1/2 gives π/3 and 5π/3, and cos x = −1 gives π; leaving out the second factor misses x = π.

**A629.** C. 3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-18 -->

Explanation: The period of tan x is π and tan x takes every real value exactly once in each period, so 3π gives 3 solutions. Using the period 2π of sine and cosine would give 2 or 6.

**A630.** A. 4π/3 and 5π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-19 -->

Explanation: The reference angle is π/3 and sine is negative in quadrants 3 and 4, giving π + π/3 = 4π/3 and 2π − π/3 = 5π/3. 7π/6 and 11π/6 have reference angle π/6.

**A631.** C. ±π/3
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-20 -->

Explanation: Cosine is positive in quadrants 1 and 4, which for this interval are x = π/3 and x = −π/3. 5π/3 lies outside the interval, and cos is symmetric about the y-axis so both ±π/3 work.

**A632.** B. −π/6 and −5π/6
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-21 -->

Explanation: sin x = −1/2 has a reference angle of π/6 and sine is negative in quadrants 3 and 4, which are x = −5π/6 and −π/6 in this interval. 7π/6 is outside the domain.

**A633.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-22 -->

Explanation: 2x ∈ [0, 4π] covers two periods of cosine, and each period gives 2 solutions of cos θ = 0.3, so there are 4. Counting only one period gives 2.

**A634.** D. π/3 and π
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-23 -->

Explanation: With θ = x − π/6 ∈ [−π/6, 11π/6], sin θ = 1/2 gives θ = π/6 or 5π/6, so x = π/3 or π. Quoting θ itself, or forgetting to add π/6 back, gives the wrong values.

**A635.** B. Solutions where sin x = 0 are lost
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-24 -->

Explanation: Dividing by sin x discards the solutions where sin x = 0 (such as x = 0 and π). The correct approach is to factorise: sin x (2cos x − 1) = 0.

**A636.** C. 0
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-25 -->

Explanation: The range of sin x is [−1, 1], and −3/2 lies outside it, so there are no solutions. Using a reference angle without checking the range would wrongly suggest 2 solutions.

**A637.** cos x = −1/2 [1]; cosine is negative in quadrants 2 and 3 with reference angle π/3, so x = 2π/3 and 4π/3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-26 -->

Explanation:

**A638.** sin(2x) = −√2/2 with 2x ∈ [0, 2π] [1]; 2x = 5π/4 or 7π/4 [1]; x = 5π/8 or 7π/8 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-27 -->

Explanation:

**A639.** Factorise: (2cos x − 1)(cos x − 1) = 0 [1]; cos x = 1/2 gives x = π/3 [1] and x = 5π/3 [1]; cos x = 1 gives x = 0 and x = 2π [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-28 -->

Explanation:

**A640.** Divide by cos x (cos x = 0 is not a solution since it would force sin x = 0, which is impossible) to give tan x = √3 [1]; x = π/3 [1]; x = π + π/3 = 4π/3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-29 -->

Explanation:

**A641.** Use cos²x = 1 − sin²x: 2 − 2sin²x + sin x = 1, so 2sin²x − sin x − 1 = 0 [1]; factorise: (2sin x + 1)(sin x − 1) = 0 [1]; sin x = −1/2 gives x = 7π/6 and 11π/6 [1]; sin x = 1 gives x = π/2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-30 -->

Explanation:

**A642.** sin(x + π/4) = √3/2 [1]; let θ = x + π/4 ∈ [−3π/4, 5π/4], so θ = π/3 or 2π/3 [1]; x = θ − π/4 = π/12 or 5π/12 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-31 -->

Explanation:

**A643.** h = 2.75: 1.5 sin(πt/6) = 0.75, so sin(πt/6) = 1/2 [1]; πt/6 ∈ [0, 2π] so πt/6 = π/6 or 5π/6 [1]; t = 1 or t = 5 [1]; h > 2.75 when 1 < t < 5, which is 4 hours [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-32 -->

Explanation:

**A644.** The equation is tan x = 5/3, and tan x has period π and takes each real value once per period [1]; 4π/π = 4 periods, so there are 4 solutions [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-33 -->

Explanation:

**A645.** 3x ∈ [0, 3π] [1]; 3x = 5π/6, 7π/6 or 17π/6 [1]; x = 5π/18, 7π/18 or 17π/18 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-34 -->

Explanation:

**A646.** C. x = −π/6, π/6
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-35 -->

Explanation: cos(x) = √3/2 has reference angle π/6, and cosine is positive in quadrants 1 and 4, giving x = ±π/6 on this domain. The values π/6 and 5π/6 would be solutions of sine instead.

**A647.** C. 5
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-36 -->

Explanation: sin(2x) = 0 when 2x = 0, π, 2π, 3π, 4π, so x = 0, π/2, π, 3π/2, 2π gives 5 solutions. Counting only 0, π and 2π would be solving sin(x) = 0.

**A648.** Use sin²x = 1 − cos²x to get 2cos²x + 3cos x − 2 = 0. [1] Factorise: (2cos x − 1)(cos x + 2) = 0. [1] cos x = −2 has no solution, so cos x = 1/2. [1] x = π/3 or 5π/3. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-37 -->

Explanation:

**A649.** sin(x/2) = −1/2 with 0 ≤ x/2 ≤ 2π. [1] x/2 = 7π/6 or 11π/6. [1] x = 7π/3 or 11π/3. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a2-sol-38 -->

Explanation:

### Probability and counting

#### Probability rules and Venn diagrams

**A650.** B. 0.7
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-1 -->

Explanation: Addition rule: P(A ∪ B) = 0.5 + 0.4 − 0.2 = 0.7.

**A651.** C. 0.65
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-2 -->

Explanation: Independence: P(A ∩ B) = 0.3 × 0.5 = 0.15. P(A ∪ B) = 0.3 + 0.5 − 0.15 = 0.65.

**A652.** A. 0.40
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-3 -->

Explanation: Mutually exclusive, so P(A ∪ B) = 0.35 + 0.25 = 0.60. A′ ∩ B′ is the complement of A ∪ B: 1 − 0.60 = 0.40.

**A653.** C. 0.3
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-4 -->

Explanation: P(A | B) = P(A ∩ B)/P(B) = 0.12/0.4 = 0.3.

**A654.** D. 25/35
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-5 -->

Explanation: Not art: 60 − 25 = 35 students. Music but not art: 35 − 10 = 25. So P = 25/35 = 5/7.

**A655.** (a) Union = 22 + 18 − 8 = 32, so 8 own neither; (b) cat only = 18 − 8 = 10, P = 10/40 = 1/4; (c) P(dog | cat) = 8/18 = 4/9; (d) P(dog) × P(cat) = 22/40 × 18/40 = 0.2475 but P(dog ∩ cat) = 8/40 = 0.2; not equal, so not independent.
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-6 -->

Explanation:

**A656.** (a) P(A ∩ B) = 0.4 + 0.25 − 0.55 = 0.10; (b) P(A) × P(B) = 0.4 × 0.25 = 0.10 = P(A ∩ B), so independent; (c) independent events have independent complements, so P(A | B′) = P(A) = 0.4.
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-7 -->

Explanation:

**A657.** (a) P(late) = 0.3 × 0.6 + 0.7 × 0.1 = 0.18 + 0.07 = 0.25; (b) P(rain | late) = P(rain ∩ late)/P(late) = 0.18/0.25 = 0.72.
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-8 -->

Explanation:

**A658.** A. 0.3
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-9 -->

Explanation: Rearranging the addition rule: P(A ∩ B) = 0.5 + 0.6 − 0.8 = 0.3. Using 0.5 + 0.6 = 1.1 forgets to subtract the union.

**A659.** B. A and B are independent
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-10 -->

Explanation: P(A) × P(B) = 0.7 × 0.4 = 0.28 = P(A ∩ B), so A and B are independent. They cannot be mutually exclusive because P(A ∩ B) is not 0.

**A660.** C. 0.3
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-11 -->

Explanation: P(A ∩ B) = P(A | B) × P(B) = 0.5 × 0.6 = 0.3. Dividing 0.5 by 0.6 gives 0.83, which reverses the conditional probability formula.

**A661.** A. 0.35
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-12 -->

Explanation: A is split into the part in B and the part outside B, so P(A ∩ B′) = 0.55 − 0.2 = 0.35. 0.75 adds the two values instead of subtracting.

**A662.** C. 0.7
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-13 -->

Explanation: Let P(B) = b. Then 0.85 = 0.5 + b − 0.5b, so 0.5b = 0.35 and b = 0.7. The value 0.35 is 0.85 − 0.5, which ignores the overlap.

**A663.** A. 15
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-14 -->

Explanation: 40 people drink at least one, so both = 30 + 25 − 40 = 15. Answering 5 comes from 30 + 25 − 50, forgetting the 10 who drink neither.

**A664.** D. 0.85
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-15 -->

Explanation: By De Morgan's law, A′ ∪ B′ = (A ∩ B)′, so its probability is 1 − 0.15 = 0.85. 0.15 is P(A ∩ B) itself.

**A665.** C. 4/9
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-16 -->

Explanation: The sample space is the 45 instrument players, of whom 20 play sport, so P = 20/45 = 4/9. 1/3 divides by 60, the number who play sport.

**A666.** D. A and B are not independent, since P(A ∩ B) = 0 but P(A) × P(B) = 0.12
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-17 -->

Explanation: Independence needs P(A ∩ B) = P(A) × P(B). Here P(A ∩ B) = 0 while P(A) × P(B) = 0.12, so they are not independent; mutually exclusive events with non-zero probabilities are never independent.

**A667.** A. 0.3
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-18 -->

Explanation: P(A ∪ B) = 0.45 + 0.35 − 0.1 = 0.7, so P(A′ ∩ B′) = 1 − 0.7 = 0.3. 0.7 is the probability that at least one occurs.

**A668.** C. 0.4
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-19 -->

Explanation: A′ and B′ are also independent, so P(A′ ∩ B′) = 0.8 × 0.5 = 0.4. 0.6 is P(A ∪ B), and 0.3 wrongly treats the events as mutually exclusive.

**A669.** D. P(A | B) = P(A)
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-20 -->

Explanation: Independence means knowing B occurred does not change the probability of A, so P(A | B) = P(A). P(A ∩ B) = 0 describes mutually exclusive events instead.

**A670.** B. 0.35
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-21 -->

Explanation: Bread only = 48 − 20 = 28, so P = 28/80 = 0.35. 0.60 is the probability of buying bread at all, including those who also buy milk.

**A671.** B. 0.5
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-22 -->

Explanation: Not bread: 80 − 48 = 32 shoppers. Milk but not bread: 36 − 20 = 16. So P = 16/32 = 0.5. 0.2 divides by 80 instead of the restricted sample space.

**A672.** D. 0.7
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-23 -->

Explanation: P(A ∩ B) = 0.5 × 0.6 = 0.3, so P(A ∪ B) = 0.4 + 0.6 − 0.3 = 0.7. 1.0 forgets to subtract the intersection.

**A673.** B. A′ ∪ B
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-24 -->

Explanation: By De Morgan's law, the complement of an intersection is the union of the complements: (A ∩ B′)′ = A′ ∪ (B′)′ = A′ ∪ B. A′ ∩ B would be the complement of A ∪ B′.

**A674.** D. 0.6
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-25 -->

Explanation: P(A) = 0.36 + 0.24 = 0.60, so P(B | A) = 0.36/0.60 = 0.6. 0.4 is P(B′ | A), the complementary conditional probability.

**A675.** B. 0.032
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-26 -->

Explanation: P(defective) = 0.6 × 0.02 + 0.4 × 0.05 = 0.012 + 0.020 = 0.032. Averaging 2% and 5% to get 0.035 ignores that the machines produce different proportions.

**A676.** C. 0.50
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-27 -->

Explanation: P(A ∩ B) = 0.3 + 0.5 − 0.65 = 0.15, so P(B | A) = 0.15/0.3 = 0.5. This equals P(B), showing the events are independent; 0.15 is only the intersection.

**A677.** (a) P(A ∪ B) = 0.5 + 0.3 − 0.12 = 0.68 [1]; (b) P(A) × P(B) = 0.15 ≠ 0.12 = P(A ∩ B), so not independent [1]; (c) P(A | B) = 0.12/0.3 = 0.4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-28 -->

Explanation:

**A678.** (a) P(A ∪ B) = 0.2 + 0.45 = 0.65 [1]; (b) P(A′ ∩ B′) = 1 − 0.65 = 0.35 [1]; (c) P(A | B) = P(A ∩ B)/P(B) = 0/0.45 = 0 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-29 -->

Explanation:

**A679.** (a) Union = 70 + 50 − 30 = 90, so 120 − 90 = 30 play neither [1]; (b) netball only = 70 − 30 = 40 and tennis only = 50 − 30 = 20 [1]; (c) P(tennis | netball) = 30/70 = 3/7 [1]; (d) not tennis = 120 − 50 = 70, of whom 40 play netball, so 40/70 = 4/7 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-30 -->

Explanation:

**A680.** (a) P(A ∩ B) = 0.6 × 0.25 = 0.15 [1]; (b) P(A ∪ B) = 0.6 + 0.25 − 0.15 = 0.70 [1]; (c) A′ and B are independent, so P(A′ ∩ B) = 0.4 × 0.25 = 0.10 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-31 -->

Explanation:

**A681.** (a) P(A ∩ B) = 0.5 × 0.4 = 0.20 [1]; (b) P(B) = 0.20 + 0.5 × 0.2 = 0.30 [1]; (c) P(A | B) = 0.20/0.30 = 2/3 [1]; (d) not independent, since P(B | A) = 0.4 ≠ 0.3 = P(B) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-32 -->

Explanation:

**A682.** P(A ∩ B) = P(A) − P(A ∩ B′) = 0.8 − 0.32 = 0.48 [1]; P(A) × P(B) = 0.8 × 0.6 = 0.48 = P(A ∩ B), so A and B are independent [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-33 -->

Explanation:

**A683.** (a) P(choir ∩ drama) = 0.4 × 0.75 = 0.30 [1]; (b) P(drama) = 0.30 + 0.6 × 0.25 = 0.45 [1]; (c) P(choir | drama) = 0.30/0.45 = 2/3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-34 -->

Explanation:

**A684.** P(A ∩ B) = 0.4 + 0.5 − 0.7 = 0.2 [1]; A′ ∪ B′ = (A ∩ B)′, so P(A′ ∪ B′) = 1 − 0.2 = 0.8 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-35 -->

Explanation:

**A685.** (a) Union = 28 + 20 − 9 = 39, so 50 − 39 = 11 study neither [1]; (b) French only = 19 and Japanese only = 11, so P = 30/50 = 3/5 [1]; (c) P(French | Japanese) = 9/20 [1]; (d) P(F) × P(J) = 0.56 × 0.4 = 0.224 but P(F ∩ J) = 9/50 = 0.18; not equal, so not independent [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-36 -->

Explanation:

**A686.** Mutually exclusive events have P(A ∩ B) = 0 [1]; independence requires P(A ∩ B) = P(A) × P(B), which is non-zero when both probabilities are non-zero, so the two conditions contradict [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-37 -->

Explanation:

**A687.** (a) P(B) = P(A ∩ B)/P(A | B) = 0.2/0.5 = 0.4 [1]; (b) P(A) = P(A ∩ B)/P(B | A) = 0.2/0.4 = 0.5 [1]; (c) P(A ∪ B) = 0.5 + 0.4 − 0.2 = 0.7 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a2-prob-38 -->

Explanation:

#### Conditional probability and independence

**A688.** B. A and B are independent
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-1 -->

Explanation: Pr(A) × Pr(B) = 0.4 × 0.5 = 0.2 = Pr(A ∩ B), so A and B are independent. They are not mutually exclusive (Pr(A ∩ B) ≠ 0). Pr(B | A) = 0.2/0.4 = 0.5, not 0.4, and Pr(A ∪ B) = 0.7.

**A689.** C. 0.40
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-2 -->

Explanation: Pr(B) = 0.12 + 0.18 = 0.30. Pr(A | B) = Pr(A ∩ B)/Pr(B) = 0.12/0.30 = 0.40. 0.30 is Pr(B), not the conditional probability.

**A690.** D. 3/7
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-3 -->

Explanation: After removing one red counter, 7 counters remain and 3 are blue, so the probability is 3/7. 3/8 would apply only with replacement.

**A691.** A. 0.30
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-4 -->

Explanation: Pr(B) = Pr(A)Pr(B | A) + Pr(A′)Pr(B | A′) = 0.5 × 0.4 + 0.5 × 0.2 = 0.20 + 0.10 = 0.30.

**A692.** B. 0.86
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-5 -->

Explanation: Independence gives Pr(A ∩ B) = 0.3 × 0.8 = 0.24. Then Pr(A ∪ B) = 0.3 + 0.8 − 0.24 = 0.86. Simply adding the probabilities (1.10) double counts the overlap.

**A693.** (a) Pr(C | Y) = 36/60 = 0.6 (1 mark); (b) Pr(C) = 90/150 = 0.6 (1 mark); Pr(C | Y) = Pr(C), so C and Y are independent (1 mark). Equivalent check: Pr(C ∩ Y) = 36/150 = 0.24 = 0.6 × 0.4.
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A694.** Let Pr(B) = p. Independence: Pr(A ∩ B) = 0.4p (1 mark); Pr(A ∪ B) = 0.4 + p − 0.4p = 0.7 (1 mark); 0.6p = 0.3 so Pr(B) = 0.5 (1 mark). Check: 0.4 + 0.5 − 0.2 = 0.7.
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A695.** (a) Pr(D) = 0.6 × 0.03 + 0.4 × 0.05 = 0.018 + 0.020 = 0.038 (2 marks); (b) Pr(X | D) = 0.018/0.038 (1 mark) = 9/19 ≈ 0.474 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A696.** A. Pr(A ∩ B)/Pr(B)
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-9 -->

Explanation: Conditional probability restricts the sample space to B, so we divide the overlap by Pr(B). Dividing by Pr(A) gives Pr(B | A) instead.

**A697.** B. 0.30
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-10 -->

Explanation: For independent events Pr(A ∩ B) = Pr(A) × Pr(B) = 0.6 × 0.5 = 0.30. Adding the probabilities (1.10) is not valid for an intersection.

**A698.** C. 0
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-11 -->

Explanation: Mutually exclusive events cannot both occur, so Pr(A ∩ B) = 0 and Pr(A | B) = 0/0.4 = 0. The value 0.12 would be correct only if A and B were independent.

**A699.** D. 0.30
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-12 -->

Explanation: Rearranging the conditional probability formula gives Pr(A ∩ B) = Pr(A | B) × Pr(B) = 0.5 × 0.6 = 0.30. Dividing instead (0.5/0.6 ≈ 0.83) is a common error.

**A700.** C. 2/3
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-13 -->

Explanation: The even outcomes are 2, 4 and 6, and two of these (4 and 6) exceed 3, so the probability is 2/3. Using all six outcomes as the sample space would give 1/3, which ignores the condition.

**A701.** B. 1/3
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-14 -->

Explanation: The outcomes with at least one head are HH, HT and TH, and only HH has two heads, so the probability is 1/3. The value 1/4 ignores the given information.

**A702.** A. 0.6
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-15 -->

Explanation: Pr(A ∩ B) = 0.6 + 0.5 − 0.8 = 0.3, so Pr(A | B) = 0.3/0.5 = 0.6. The value 0.3 is the intersection, not the conditional probability.

**A703.** D. A′ and B are independent
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-16 -->

Explanation: If knowing A does not change the probability of B, then knowing A′ does not either, so A′ and B are independent. Independent events with non-zero probabilities always overlap, so Pr(A ∩ B) = 0 is false.

**A704.** A. 0.18
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-17 -->

Explanation: Pr(both) = Pr(Monday) × Pr(Tuesday | Monday) = 0.3 × 0.6 = 0.18. Adding the probabilities (0.90) is not the rule for 'and'.

**A705.** B. 5/18
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-18 -->

Explanation: The condition restricts attention to the 18 tennis players, 5 of whom play netball, giving 5/18. Using 12 as the denominator gives Pr(tennis | netball) instead.

**A706.** C. 1/17
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-19 -->

Explanation: Pr(both hearts) = 13/52 × 12/51 = 156/2652 = 1/17. Using 1/4 × 1/4 = 1/16 wrongly treats the draws as independent.

**A707.** D. A and B are independent
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-20 -->

Explanation: If knowing that B occurred does not change the probability of A, then A and B are independent by definition. Mutually exclusive events would give Pr(A | B) = 0.

**A708.** B. 1/3
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-21 -->

Explanation: Pr(A ∩ B) = 0.2 × 0.5 = 0.10 and Pr(B) = 0.10 + 0.8 × 0.25 = 0.30, so Pr(A | B) = 0.10/0.30 = 1/3. The value 0.5 is Pr(B | A), the reverse conditional.

**A709.** A. 0.30
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-22 -->

Explanation: A′ and B′ are also independent, so Pr(A′ ∩ B′) = 0.5 × 0.6 = 0.30. Equivalently Pr(A ∪ B) = 0.5 + 0.4 − 0.2 = 0.7 and 1 − 0.7 = 0.30.

**A710.** C. A and B are not independent because Pr(B | A) = 0.3 ≠ Pr(B)
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-23 -->

Explanation: Pr(B | A) = 0.15/0.5 = 0.3, which differs from Pr(B) = 0.4, so the events are not independent. They are not mutually exclusive since Pr(A ∩ B) ≠ 0, and Pr(A | B) = 0.15/0.4 = 0.375.

**A711.** D. 0.36
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-24 -->

Explanation: With replacement the draws are independent, so Pr(both blue) = 0.6 × 0.6 = 0.36. The value 1/3 (6/10 × 5/9) is the answer if the counters are not replaced.

**A712.** A. 0.65
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-25 -->

Explanation: Conditional probabilities given the same event B sum to 1 over A and A′, so Pr(A′ | B) = 1 − 0.35 = 0.65.

**A713.** B. 0.14
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-26 -->

Explanation: Pr(positive) = 0.05 × 0.9 + 0.95 × 0.1 = 0.045 + 0.095 = 0.14. The value 0.045 counts only those who have the condition.

**A714.** C. 0.4
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-27 -->

Explanation: A and B′ are independent, so Pr(A | B′) = Pr(A) = 0.4. The value 0.28 is Pr(A ∩ B′), not the conditional probability.

**A715.** (a) Pr(GG) = 6/10 × 5/9 = 1/3 [1] (b) Pr(GY) = 6/10 × 4/9 = 4/15 and Pr(YG) = 4/10 × 6/9 = 4/15 [1] Pr(different) = 8/15 [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-28 -->

Explanation:

**A716.** Pr(A ∩ B) = Pr(A) × Pr(B | A) = 0.7 × 0.2 = 0.14 [1] Pr(A | B) = Pr(A ∩ B)/Pr(B) = 0.14/0.3 [1] = 7/15 ≈ 0.467 [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-29 -->

Explanation:

**A717.** (a) Pr(positive) = 0.02 × 0.95 + 0.98 × 0.04 [1] = 0.019 + 0.0392 = 0.0582 [1] (b) Pr(condition | positive) = 0.019/0.0582 [1] ≈ 0.326 [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-30 -->

Explanation:

**A718.** Pr(A ∩ B) = Pr(A) − Pr(A ∩ B′) = 0.5 − 0.2 = 0.3 [1] Independence: 0.3 = 0.5 × Pr(B) [1] Pr(B) = 0.6 [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-31 -->

Explanation:

**A719.** Pr(A′ ∩ B) = Pr(B) − Pr(A ∩ B) [1] = Pr(B) − Pr(A)Pr(B) by independence [1] = Pr(B)(1 − Pr(A)) = Pr(A′)Pr(B), so A′ and B are independent [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-32 -->

Explanation:

**A720.** (a) 30/70 = 3/7 [1] (b) 30/45 = 2/3 [1] (c) Pr(Physics) = 45/120 = 3/8 [1] Pr(Physics | girl) = 3/7 ≠ 3/8, so the events are not independent [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-33 -->

Explanation:

**A721.** (a) Pr(A ∩ B) = 0.6 + 0.5 − 0.9 = 0.2 [1] (b) Pr(A | B) = 0.2/0.5 = 0.4 [1] Pr(A | B) ≠ Pr(A) = 0.6 (or 0.6 × 0.5 = 0.3 ≠ 0.2), so A and B are not independent [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-34 -->

Explanation:

**A722.** Pr(sum is 8) = 5/36, from (2,6), (3,5), (4,4), (5,3), (6,2) [1] Pr(sum is 8 and both even) = 3/36, from (2,6), (4,4), (6,2) [1] Pr(both even | sum 8) = 3/36 ÷ 5/36 = 3/5 [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-35 -->

Explanation:

**A723.** Pr(A ∩ B′) = 0.3 × 0.4 = 0.12 [1] Pr(A′ ∩ B) = 0.7 × 0.6 = 0.42 [1] Pr(exactly one) = 0.12 + 0.42 = 0.54 [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-36 -->

Explanation:

**A724.** (a) 0.4 = 0.5 × Pr(B | A) + 0.5 × 0.2 [1] so Pr(B | A) = 0.6 [1] (b) Pr(A | B) = 0.5 × 0.6/0.4 = 0.75 [1] (c) Pr(B | A) = 0.6 ≠ Pr(B) = 0.4, so A and B are not independent [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-37 -->

Explanation:

**A725.** B. 0.4
<!-- id: methods12a3-cp-38 -->

Explanation: Pr(A | B′) = Pr(A ∩ B′) ÷ Pr(B′) = 0.2 ÷ 0.5 = 0.4. Using 0.1, the product, is multiplying instead of dividing.

#### Permutations and combinations

**A726.** C. 60
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-1 -->

Explanation: There are 6 letters with A repeated 3 times and N repeated 2 times: 6!/(3! × 2!) = 720/12 = 60. 720 ignores the repeats.

**A727.** D. 56
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-2 -->

Explanation: Order does not matter, so ⁸C₃ = 8!/(3! × 5!) = 56. 336 = ⁸P₃ counts ordered selections.

**A728.** D. 840
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-3 -->

Explanation: Order matters and no book is reused: ⁷P₄ = 7 × 6 × 5 × 4 = 840. 35 is ⁷C₄ and 2401 would be 7⁴.

**A729.** B. 9000
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-4 -->

Explanation: First digit: 9 choices; each of the other three digits: 10 choices. 9 × 10 × 10 × 10 = 9000. 4536 would be the answer if repeats were not allowed.

**A730.** A. 10
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-5 -->

Explanation: n(n − 1)/2 = 45 gives n(n − 1) = 90, so n = 10 (⁹C₂ = 36 and ¹¹C₂ = 55).

**A731.** (a) Treat Alice and Ben as one block: 4! arrangements of 4 objects × 2! orders within the block = 48 (2 marks); (b) total 5! = 120, so 120 − 48 = 72 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A732.** Cases: 2 girls, 2 boys = ⁵C₂ × ⁶C₂ = 10 × 15 = 150; 3 girls, 1 boy = ⁵C₃ × ⁶C₁ = 10 × 6 = 60; 4 girls = ⁵C₄ = 5 (2 marks for correct cases). Total = 150 + 60 + 5 = 215 (1 mark). Check: ¹¹C₄ = 330, minus 0 girls (15) and 1 girl (100) = 215.
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A733.** The last digit must be even: 3 choices (2, 4 or 6) (1 mark); the other three places are filled from the remaining 5 digits in order: 5 × 4 × 3 = 60 (1 mark); total 3 × 60 = 180 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A734.** C. 42
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-9 -->

Explanation: 7!/5! = 7 × 6 = 42, since the 5! cancels. 35 would come from 7 × 5.

**A735.** B. 45
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-10 -->

Explanation: ¹⁰C₈ = ¹⁰C₂ = 10 × 9/2 = 45. 90 is ¹⁰P₂, which counts ordered selections.

**A736.** A. 24
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-11 -->

Explanation: All four letters are different, so there are 4! = 24 arrangements. 16 would be 4² and wrongly allows letters to be reused.

**A737.** D. 336
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-12 -->

Explanation: Order matters, so ⁸P₃ = 8 × 7 × 6 = 336. 56 is ⁸C₃, which ignores who finishes in which place.

**A738.** C. 20
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-13 -->

Explanation: ⁵P₂ = 5 × 4 = 20. 10 is ⁵C₂, which divides by 2! because it ignores order.

**A739.** C. 30
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-14 -->

Explanation: There are 5 letters with T repeated twice and O repeated twice: 5!/(2! × 2!) = 120/4 = 30. 60 only divides out one of the repeated pairs.

**A740.** A. 8
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-15 -->

Explanation: n(n − 1) = 56 gives n = 8, since 8 × 7 = 56. n = 7 gives only 42.

**A741.** D. 24
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-16 -->

Explanation: By the multiplication principle, 4 × 3 × 2 = 24. Adding the numbers (4 + 3 + 2 = 9) is a common mistake.

**A742.** C. 7
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-17 -->

Explanation: Using ⁿCᵣ = ⁿC₍ₙ₋ᵣ₎, ⁹C₂ = ⁹C₇. A value of 6 does not work, since ⁹C₆ = 84 but ⁹C₂ = 36.

**A743.** B. 66
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-18 -->

Explanation: Each handshake is an unordered pair, so ¹²C₂ = 66. 132 is ¹²P₂, which counts each handshake twice.

**A744.** A. 125
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-19 -->

Explanation: Each of the three positions has 5 choices, so 5³ = 125. 60 is the answer if repeats were not allowed.

**A745.** D. 72
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-20 -->

Explanation: Either a boy or a girl can start (2 ways), then the boys can be ordered 3! ways and the girls 3! ways: 2 × 6 × 6 = 72. 36 forgets that the pattern can start with either sex.

**A746.** C. 9
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-21 -->

Explanation: Choose any 2 of the 6 vertices: ⁶C₂ = 15, then subtract the 6 sides to leave 9 diagonals. 15 counts the sides as well.

**A747.** B. 60
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-22 -->

Explanation: Choose the men and women separately: ⁵C₃ × ⁴C₂ = 10 × 6 = 60. 126 is ⁹C₅, which ignores the restriction on numbers of men and women.

**A748.** C. 3024
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-23 -->

Explanation: Order matters and digits are not reused: ⁹P₄ = 9 × 8 × 7 × 6 = 3024. 6561 = 9⁴ allows repeats, and 126 is ⁹C₄.

**A749.** D. 34 650
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-24 -->

Explanation: There are 11 letters with I repeated 4 times, S 4 times and P twice: 11!/(4! × 4! × 2!) = 39 916 800/1152 = 34 650.

**A750.** B. 56
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-25 -->

Explanation: Sam is already in, so choose the other 3 from the remaining 8: ⁸C₃ = 56. 126 is the number of committees with no condition on Sam.

**A751.** A. 36
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-26 -->

Explanation: The last digit is odd (1, 3 or 5): 3 choices. The first two digits are then filled in 4 × 3 ways, giving 3 × 12 = 36. 60 counts all 3-digit numbers, odd or even.

**A752.** ⁷C₃ = 7!/(3! × 4!) = (7 × 6 × 5)/6 = 35 [1]; ⁷P₃ = 7 × 6 × 5 = 210 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A753.** The first digit must be 3, 4 or 5, so there are 3 choices [1]; each of the other three digits has 5 choices, so 3 × 5 × 5 × 5 = 375 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-28 -->

Explanation:

**A754.** n(n − 1) = 12, so n² − n − 12 = 0, giving (n − 4)(n + 3) = 0 [1]; n must be positive, so n = 4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-29 -->

Explanation:

**A755.** (a) ¹²C₅ = 792 [1]; (b) ⁵C₃ × ⁷C₂ = 10 × 21 = 210 [1] [1]; (c) subtract the all-women committees: 792 − ⁵C₅ = 792 − 1 = 791 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-30 -->

Explanation:

**A756.** ⁸C₃ = 56 [1]; ⁸C₄ = 70, so the sum is 126 [1]; ⁹C₄ = (9 × 8 × 7 × 6)/24 = 126, so the two are equal [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-31 -->

Explanation:

**A757.** Runner A can take any one of the 3 medals: 3 ways [1]; the other two medals go to 2 of the remaining 7 runners in order: ⁷P₂ = 42 ways [1]; total 3 × 42 = 126 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-32 -->

Explanation:

**A758.** (a) 9 × 9 × 8 × 7 = 4536 [1]; (b) ends in 0: 9 × 8 × 7 = 504 [1]; ends in 5: first digit has 8 choices (not 0 or 5), then 8 × 7 for the middle digits, giving 8 × 8 × 7 = 448 [1]; total 504 + 448 = 952 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-33 -->

Explanation:

**A759.** Choosing 2 toppings: ⁶C₂ = 15, and choosing 3 toppings: ⁶C₃ = 20 [1]; the cases are exclusive, so total 15 + 20 = 35 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-34 -->

Explanation:

**A760.** Treat each couple as a block; the 3 blocks can be arranged in 3! = 6 ways [1]; within each block the two people can swap, giving 2³ = 8 ways [1]; total 6 × 8 = 48 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-35 -->

Explanation:

**A761.** Choose the 2 hearts from 13: ¹³C₂ = 78 [1]; choose the other 3 cards from the 39 non-hearts: ³⁹C₃ = 9139 [1]; total 78 × 9139 = 712 842 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-36 -->

Explanation:

**A762.** C. 180
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-37 -->

Explanation: There are 6 letters with T repeated twice and E repeated twice, so 6!/(2! × 2!) = 720/4 = 180. Dividing by only one factorial of 2 gives 360.

**A763.** Chen can take any of the 5 middle positions. [1] The other 6 people can be arranged in 6! = 720 ways, so the total is 5 × 720 = 3600. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-pc-38 -->

Explanation:

### Introductory calculus

#### Rates of change

**A764.** D. 5
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-1 -->

Explanation: (f(4) − f(1))/(4 − 1) = (16 − 1)/3 = 5. 15 is the change in f without dividing by the change in x.

**A765.** A. 8 m s⁻¹
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-2 -->

Explanation: s(2) = 12 + 4 = 16 and s(0) = 0, so the average velocity is 16/2 = 8 m s⁻¹. 16 is the displacement; 14 would be the instantaneous velocity at t = 2.

**A766.** B. litres per minute
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-3 -->

Explanation: Average rate of change = change in V divided by change in t, so the units are litres per minute.

**A767.** D. −3 L min⁻¹
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-4 -->

Explanation: Gradient = (20 − 50)/(10 − 0) = −3 L min⁻¹. The negative sign shows the volume is decreasing.

**A768.** D. 2 + h
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-5 -->

Explanation: ((1 + h)² − 1)/h = (2h + h²)/h = 2 + h. As h → 0 this approaches 2, the gradient of the tangent.

**A769.** (a) f(3) = 27 − 6 = 21 and f(1) = 1 − 2 = −1 (1 mark); average rate = (21 − (−1))/(3 − 1) = 22/2 = 11 (1 mark); (b) it is the gradient of the secant (chord) joining (1, −1) and (3, 21) (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A770.** (a) P(3) = 860, P(0) = 500, so (860 − 500)/3 = 120 bacteria per hour (1 mark); (b) P(4) = 1140, so (1140 − 860)/1 = 280 bacteria per hour (1 mark); (c) the growth rate is increasing over time, as expected for a quadratic with positive coefficient (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A771.** 0 to 5 min: (60 − 90)/5 = −6 °C per minute (1 mark); 5 to 10 min: (45 − 60)/5 = −3 °C per minute (1 mark); the tea is cooling in both intervals (negative rates) but the rate of cooling slows down as it approaches room temperature (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A772.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-9 -->

Explanation: f(5) = 10 and f(2) = −2, so the average rate is (10 − (−2))/(5 − 2) = 12/3 = 4. 12 is only the change in f, not divided by the change in x.

**A773.** C. 60 km h⁻¹
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-10 -->

Explanation: Average speed = 150 ÷ 2.5 = 60 km h⁻¹. 75 comes from dividing by 2 instead of 2.5, and 375 from multiplying.

**A774.** A. 6
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-11 -->

Explanation: ((3 + h)² − 9)/h = 6 + h, which approaches 6 as h → 0. 9 is the value f(3), not the rate of change.

**A775.** B. the limit of the average rate of change over [a, a + h] as h approaches 0
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-12 -->

Explanation: Shrinking the interval so that h → 0 turns the secant gradient into the tangent gradient. A fixed interval always gives an average rate, not an instantaneous one.

**A776.** C. 10 m s⁻¹
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-13 -->

Explanation: s′(t) = 2t + 4, so s′(3) = 10 m s⁻¹. 7 m s⁻¹ is the average velocity from t = 0 to t = 3 (21/3), and 21 is the displacement at t = 3.

**A777.** D. 12
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-14 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 3x², so at x = 2 the gradient is 3 × 4 = 12. 8 is the y-value at x = 2, not the gradient.

**A778.** C. 4
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-15 -->

Explanation: (f(2) − f(0))/(2 − 0) = (8 − 0)/2 = 4. 8 is the change in f without dividing by the change in x.

**A779.** D. −2
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-16 -->

Explanation: f(10) = −15 and f(1) = 3, so the average rate is −18/9 = −2, the gradient of the line. −18 is the change in f only.

**A780.** A. decreasing
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-17 -->

Explanation: A negative instantaneous rate of change means the quantity is decreasing. The rate being negative does not mean the height itself is zero or negative.

**A781.** B. 3 + 3h + h²
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-18 -->

Explanation: ((1 + h)³ − 1)/h = (3h + 3h² + h³)/h = 3 + 3h + h². The value 3 is only the limit as h → 0, not the secant gradient for a fixed h.

**A782.** C. 27
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-19 -->

Explanation: f(6) − f(2) = 5 × (6 − 2) = 20, so f(6) = 27. 32 comes from using 5 × 6 + 2, and 12 from adding 5 to f(2).

**A783.** D. −20 L min⁻¹
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-20 -->

Explanation: V is linear with gradient −20, so the volume falls by 20 L every minute. 20 L min⁻¹ ignores the sign and so misses that the tank is draining.

**A784.** A. −12 m s⁻¹
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-21 -->

Explanation: s′(t) = 3t² − 12t, so s′(2) = 12 − 24 = −12 m s⁻¹, meaning it moves in the negative direction. −16 is the displacement s(2), not the velocity.

**A785.** B. zero
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-22 -->

Explanation: Average velocity is total displacement divided by time, and the displacement is zero because the ball returns to its start. Average speed, using distance, would not be zero.

**A786.** C. equals m
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-23 -->

Explanation: (m·b + c − m·a − c)/(b − a) = m for every interval, since the graph has constant gradient. c is the y-intercept and has no effect on the rate.

**A787.** D. −1/4
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-24 -->

Explanation: f(4) = 1/4 and f(1) = 1, so the rate is (1/4 − 1)/(4 − 1) = (−3/4)/3 = −1/4. 1/4 loses the negative sign, which shows f is decreasing.

**A788.** f(5) = 25 + 15 = 40 and f(1) = 1 + 3 = 4 [1]; average rate = (40 − 4)/(5 − 1) = 36/4 = 9 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-25 -->

Explanation:

**A789.** (a) s(2 + h) − s(2) = (4 + 4h + h²) − 4 = 4h + h² [1]; gradient = (4h + h²)/h = 4 + h [1]; (b) as h → 0, 4 + h → 4, so the instantaneous velocity is 4 m s⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-26 -->

Explanation:

**A790.** (a) 50/2 = 25 km h⁻¹ and (b) (140 − 50)/3 = 30 km h⁻¹ [1]; (c) 140/5 = 28 km h⁻¹ [1]; the mean of 25 and 30 is 27.5, which differs because the two intervals have different lengths (2 h and 3 h), so the average must be weighted by time [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A791.** (a) f(3) = −3 and f(1) = −3, so the average rate is 0/2 = 0 [1]; (b) f(3 + h) − f(3) = (9 + 6h + h² − 12 − 4h) − (−3) = 2h + h² [1]; dividing by h gives 2 + h, which approaches 2 as h → 0, so the instantaneous rate is 2 [1]; (c) f′(x) = 2x − 4 = 0 gives x = 2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-28 -->

Explanation:

**A792.** Change in population = 15 600 − 12 000 = 3600 over 10 years [1]; average rate = 3600/10 = 360 people per year [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-29 -->

Explanation:

**A793.** (a) s(3) = 51 and s(1) = 1 [1]; average velocity = (51 − 1)/(3 − 1) = 25 m s⁻¹ [1]; (b) s′(t) = 6t² − 1, so s′(2) = 23 m s⁻¹ [1]; (c) the velocity is not constant, so the average over an interval need not equal the instantaneous value at its midpoint [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-30 -->

Explanation:

**A794.** The average rate is the change in position divided by the change in time over an interval, shown as the gradient of a secant (chord) [1]; the instantaneous rate is the rate at a single moment, like a speedometer reading, found as the limit as the interval shrinks to zero and shown as the gradient of the tangent [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-31 -->

Explanation:

**A795.** (a) C(20) = 2100 and C(10) = 900 [1]; average rate = 1200/10 = $120 per item [1]; (b) C′(10) = 8 × 10 = $80 per item [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-32 -->

Explanation:

**A796.** (30 − 12)/(8 − 2) = 18/6 = 3 cm per week [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-33 -->

Explanation:

**A797.** f(4) = 16 − 24 + 1 = −7 and f(0) = 1 [1]; average rate = (−7 − 1)/4 = −2 [1]; f′(x) = 2x − 6 [1]; setting 2x − 6 = −2 gives x = 2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-34 -->

Explanation:

**A798.** D. 7
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-35 -->

Explanation: f(1) = 1 and f(3) = 15, so the average rate of change is (15 − 1)/(3 − 1) = 7. Giving 14 forgets to divide by the interval width.

**A799.** f(1) = 1 and f(1 + h) = 3(1 + h)² − 2(1 + h) = 1 + 4h + 3h². [1] Secant gradient = (4h + 3h²)/h = 4 + 3h. [1] As h → 0, the instantaneous rate of change at x = 1 is 4. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-36 -->

Explanation:

**A800.** (a) h(0) = 0 and h(2) = 60, so the average velocity is 60/2 = 30 m/s. [1] (b) h(4) = 80, so the average velocity is (80 − 60)/2 = 10 m/s. [1] (c) The average velocity is smaller later, so the rocket is slowing down. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-rc-37 -->

Explanation:

#### Differentiation from first principles

**A801.** C. lim (h→0) [f(x + h) − f(x)]/h
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-1 -->

Explanation: The derivative is the limit of the gradient of the secant, [f(x + h) − f(x)]/h, as h → 0. Option B has the numerator reversed, giving −f′(x).

**A802.** B. 2x + h
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-2 -->

Explanation: ((x + h)² − x²)/h = (2xh + h²)/h = 2x + h. Letting h → 0 then gives f′(x) = 2x.

**A803.** A. 3x² + 3xh + h²
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-3 -->

Explanation: (x + h)³ = x³ + 3x²h + 3xh² + h³, so the numerator is 3x²h + 3xh² + h³ and dividing by h gives 3x² + 3xh + h².

**A804.** C. 4
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-4 -->

Explanation: ((2 + h)² − 4)/h = (4h + h²)/h = 4 + h, which tends to 4 as h → 0.

**A805.** B. f(x) = |x|
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-5 -->

Explanation: The graph of |x| has a sharp corner at the origin: the gradient is −1 from the left and +1 from the right, so the limit defining f′(0) does not exist. The other functions are smooth polynomials.

**A806.** [f(x + h) − f(x)]/h = [(x + h)² + 3(x + h) − x² − 3x]/h (1 mark) = (2xh + h² + 3h)/h = 2x + 3 + h (1 mark); as h → 0, f′(x) = 2x + 3 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A807.** f(3) = 18 − 3 = 15 (1 mark); f(3 + h) = 2(9 + 6h + h²) − 3 − h = 15 + 11h + 2h² (1 mark); [f(3 + h) − f(3)]/h = 11 + 2h → 11 as h → 0, so f′(3) = 11 (1 mark). Check: f′(x) = 4x − 1 gives 11.
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A808.** f(x + h) − f(x) = (x + h)³ − (x + h) − x³ + x = 3x²h + 3xh² + h³ − h (1 mark); dividing by h: 3x² + 3xh + h² − 1 (1 mark); as h → 0 this tends to 3x² − 1 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A809.** B. 1
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-9 -->

Explanation: (x + h − x)/h = h/h = 1, so f′(x) = 1 for every x. It does not depend on x because the graph is a straight line of gradient 1.

**A810.** C. 0
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-10 -->

Explanation: f(x + h) − f(x) = 5 − 5 = 0, so the quotient is 0/h = 0 for every h ≠ 0, and the limit is 0. It is not undefined, because h is never actually zero in the quotient.

**A811.** D. 6x + 3h
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-11 -->

Explanation: 3(x + h)² − 3x² = 6xh + 3h², and dividing by h gives 6x + 3h. Forgetting to multiply the h term by 3 gives 6x + h, which is wrong.

**A812.** C. the horizontal distance between the two points on the secant
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-12 -->

Explanation: h is the horizontal step from x to x + h. The vertical change is f(x + h) − f(x), which is the numerator of the quotient.

**A813.** D. −2
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-13 -->

Explanation: ((−1 + h)² − 1)/h = (−2h + h²)/h = −2 + h, which tends to −2. Taking the limit as 2 comes from forgetting the sign of the −1.

**A814.** A. It gives 0/0, which is undefined, so the expression must first be simplified
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-14 -->

Explanation: With h = 0 both numerator and denominator are zero, giving the undefined form 0/0. After the factor h is cancelled, the limit as h → 0 can be evaluated.

**A815.** B. 12
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-15 -->

Explanation: [2(3 + h)² − 18]/h = (12h + 2h²)/h = 12 + 2h, which tends to 12. The value 18 is f(3), not the gradient.

**A816.** D. the gradient of the tangent to y = x² at x = 3, which is 6
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-16 -->

Explanation: The quotient is the secant gradient between x = 3 and x = 3 + h on y = x², and (6h + h²)/h = 6 + h → 6. It is the gradient at x = 3, not at x = 6.

**A817.** A. x = 2
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-17 -->

Explanation: A horizontal tangent has gradient 0, so 2x − 4 = 0 and x = 2. The value x = 4 is where the function is zero, not where its gradient is zero.

**A818.** C. It is not differentiable at x = a
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-18 -->

Explanation: A function must be continuous at a point to be differentiable there. At a jump the secant gradients become unbounded, so the limit does not exist.

**A819.** D. 4
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-19 -->

Explanation: [4(x + h) − 7 − 4x + 7]/h = 4h/h = 4. The constant −7 disappears because it cancels in f(x + h) − f(x).

**A820.** A. 12
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-20 -->

Explanation: (2 + h)³ − 8 = 12h + 6h² + h³, so the quotient is 12 + 6h + h², which tends to 12. The value 8 is f(2), not the gradient.

**A821.** B. 2ax
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-21 -->

Explanation: [a(x + h)² − ax²]/h = (2axh + ah²)/h = 2ax + ah, which tends to 2ax as h → 0. The constant a stays as a factor.
