**A822.** C. 2 m/s
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-22 -->

Explanation: [(1 + h)² − 1]/h = 2 + h, which tends to 2 m/s. The expression 2 + h is the average velocity over a time interval of length h, not the instantaneous value.

**A823.** [f(x + h) − f(x)]/h = [4(x + h)² + 5 − 4x² − 5]/h [1] = (8xh + 4h²)/h = 8x + 4h [1]; as h → 0, f′(x) = 8x [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-23 -->

Explanation:

**A824.** [f(x + h) − f(x)]/h = [1/(x + h) − 1/x]/h [1]; combine over a common denominator: [x − (x + h)]/[x(x + h)] = −h/[x(x + h)] [1]; dividing by h gives −1/[x(x + h)] [1]; as h → 0, f′(x) = −1/x² [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-24 -->

Explanation:

**A825.** f(−2) = 4 + 10 = 14 and f(−2 + h) = 4 − 4h + h² + 10 − 5h = 14 − 9h + h² [1]; [f(−2 + h) − f(−2)]/h = (−9h + h²)/h = −9 + h [1]; as h → 0, f′(−2) = −9 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-25 -->

Explanation:

**A826.** [2(x + h)³ − 2x³]/h = 2(3x²h + 3xh² + h³)/h [1] = 6x² + 6xh + 2h² [1]; as h → 0, f′(x) = 6x² [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-26 -->

Explanation:

**A827.** The quotient at x = 0 is [f(h) − f(0)]/h = |h|/h [1]; for h > 0 this equals 1, so the limit from the right is 1 [1]; for h < 0 it equals −1, so the limit from the left is −1; the two limits differ, so f′(0) does not exist [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-27 -->

Explanation:

**A828.** f(2) = 2 and f(2 + h) = 4 + 4h + h² − 2 − h = 2 + 3h + h² [1]; [f(2 + h) − f(2)]/h = 3 + h [1]; as h → 0 the gradient is 3 [1]; tangent: y − 2 = 3(x − 2), so y = 3x − 4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-28 -->

Explanation:

**A829.** [(x + h)² − x²]/h = 2x + h [1]; as h → 0, f′(x) = 2x [1]; 2x = 6 gives x = 3, so the point is (3, 9) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-29 -->

Explanation:

**A830.** 2x + h is the gradient of the secant for a particular value of h; the derivative requires taking the limit as h → 0 [1]; this gives the correct derivative f′(x) = 2x [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-30 -->

Explanation:

**A831.** B. 2x + h + 1
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-31 -->

Explanation: f(x + h) − f(x) = 2xh + h² + h, and dividing by h gives 2x + h + 1. The expression 2x + 1 is the limit as h → 0, not the quotient.

**A832.** C. 3
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-32 -->

Explanation: (−1 + h)³ + 1 = 3h − 3h² + h³, so the quotient is 3 − 3h + h², which tends to 3. A negative answer comes from sign errors in the expansion.

**A833.** D. −1/(x(x + h))
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-33 -->

Explanation: [1/(x + h) − 1/x]/h = [(x − (x + h))/(x(x + h))]/h = −1/(x(x + h)). The answer −1/x² is the limit as h → 0, not the quotient.

**A834.** B. −2x
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-34 -->

Explanation: The quotient is (−2xh − h²)/h = −2x − h, and as h → 0 the limit is −2x. Including h in the answer leaves a term that vanishes in the limit.

**A835.** f(x + h) − f(x) = 3(2xh + h²) − 2h = 6xh + 3h² − 2h. [1] [f(x + h) − f(x)]/h = 6x + 3h − 2. [1] As h → 0, f′(x) = 6x − 2. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-35 -->

Explanation:

**A836.** (x + h)⁴ = x⁴ + 4x³h + 6x²h² + 4xh³ + h⁴. [1] f(x + h) − f(x) = 4x³h + 6x²h² + 4xh³ + h⁴. [1] Dividing by h gives 4x³ + 6x²h + 4xh² + h³. [1] As h → 0, f′(x) = 4x³. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-36 -->

Explanation:

**A837.** f(−1 + h) = (−1 + h)² + 2(−1 + h) = −1 + h², and f(−1) = −1, so the quotient is h²/h = h. [1] As h → 0 the gradient is 0. [1] The tangent passes through (−1, −1), so its equation is y = −1. [1]
<!-- id: methods12a3-fp-37 -->

Explanation:

#### Derivatives of polynomials

**A838.** B. 12x² − 10x
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-1 -->

Explanation: Differentiate term by term: 12x² − 10x, and the derivative of the constant 7 is 0. Option A wrongly keeps the constant.

**A839.** B. 3
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-2 -->

Explanation: Expand: f(x) = 2x² − 5x − 3, so f′(x) = 4x − 5 and f′(2) = 8 − 5 = 3.

**A840.** C. x = ±1
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-3 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 3x² − 3 = 0 gives x² = 1, so x = ±1.

**A841.** B. x = 5
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-4 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 2x − 4 = 6 gives x = 5.

**A842.** C. 4x³ + 4x
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-5 -->

Explanation: Expand: f(x) = x⁴ + 2x² + 1, so f′(x) = 4x³ + 4x. Option A forgets the inner derivative 2x.

**A843.** f′(x) = 6x² − 18x + 12 (1 mark); = 6(x − 1)(x − 2) (1 mark); so f′(x) = 0 when x = 1 or x = 2 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A844.** dy/dx = 2x + b (1 mark); at x = 1: 2 + b = 2, so b = 0 (1 mark); y(0) = c = 3 (1 mark). The curve is y = x² + 3.
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A845.** (a) h′(t) = 20 − 10t (1 mark); (b) 20 − 10t = 0 gives t = 2 s (1 mark); h(2) = 40 − 20 = 20 m, the maximum height (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A846.** dy/dx = 3x² − 8x + 1 [1]; at x = 3: 27 − 24 + 1 = 4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-9 -->

Explanation:

**A847.** dy/dx = 2x − 3, so the gradient at x = 4 is 5 [1]; y(4) = 16 − 12 + 2 = 6, so the point is (4, 6) [1]; y − 6 = 5(x − 4), so y = 5x − 14 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-10 -->

Explanation:

**A848.** dy/dx = 3x² − 12 [1]; 3x² − 12 = 0 gives x = 2 or x = −2 [1]; the points are (2, −16) and (−2, 16) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-11 -->

Explanation:

**A849.** f(x + h) = 3(x² + 2xh + h²) − x − h [1]; f(x + h) − f(x) = 6xh + 3h² − h [1]; dividing by h gives 6x + 3h − 1 [1]; as h → 0, f′(x) = 6x − 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-12 -->

Explanation:

**A850.** Average rate = (f(3) − f(1))/2 = (21 − (−1))/2 = 11 [1]; f′(x) = 3x² − 2, so f′(2) = 10 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-13 -->

Explanation:

**A851.** dy/dx = 2x + b [1]; stationary at x = 2: 4 + b = 0, so b = −4 [1]; y(2) = 4 − 8 + c = −1, so c = 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-14 -->

Explanation:

**A852.** The required gradient is 9 [1]; dy/dx = 3x² − 6x [1]; 3x² − 6x = 9 gives (x − 3)(x + 1) = 0, so x = 3 or x = −1 [1]; the points are (3, 2) and (−1, −2) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-15 -->

Explanation:

**A853.** v(t) = 3t² − 18t + 24 = 3(t − 2)(t − 4) [1]; v = 0 when t = 2 s and t = 4 s [1]; s(2) = 20 m and s(4) = 16 m [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-16 -->

Explanation:

**A854.** f′(x) = 2x − 6 [1]; f is decreasing when f′(x) < 0, that is x < 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-17 -->

Explanation:

**A855.** From the point: a + b = 5 [1]; dy/dx = 3ax² + b, so 3a + b = 7 [1]; subtracting gives 2a = 2, so a = 1 and b = 4 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-18 -->

Explanation:

**A856.** A. 15
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-19 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 20x³ − 6x² + 1, so f′(1) = 20 − 6 + 1 = 15. Option 14 drops the derivative of x, which is 1.

**A857.** B. −12
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-20 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 6x − 6, so f′(−1) = −6 − 6 = −12. Substituting x = −1 into f(x) instead of f′(x) would give 10, which is the value of the function, not the gradient.

**A858.** C. 3x² − 4
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-21 -->

Explanation: Expand first: f(x) = x³ − 4x, so f′(x) = 3x² − 4. Differentiating only the bracket gives 2x, which is wrong because x is also a factor.

**A859.** D. x = −1 or x = 4/3
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-22 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 6x² − 2x = 8 gives 3x² − x − 4 = 0, so (3x − 4)(x + 1) = 0 and x = 4/3 or x = −1. Both solutions are valid, so stating only one is incomplete.

**A860.** B. 1
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-23 -->

Explanation: Since x² − 9 = (x − 3)(x + 3), f(x) = x + 3 for x ≠ 3, so f′(x) = 1. Option x + 3 is the simplified function, not its derivative.

**A861.** C. −2/√3 < x < 2/√3
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-24 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 4 − 3x² > 0 gives x² < 4/3, so −2/√3 < x < 2/√3. The outer intervals are where f′(x) < 0, so the function is decreasing there.

**A862.** D. 2x + h
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-25 -->

Explanation: (x + h)² − x² = 2xh + h², and dividing by h gives 2x + h. The limit as h → 0 is 2x, but the expression itself still contains h.

**A863.** A. 2
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-26 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 6x + k, so f′(2) = 12 + k = 14 and k = 2. Option 12 is the value of 6x at x = 2, not k.

**A864.** C. 9 m s⁻¹
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-27 -->

Explanation: v(t) = s′(t) = 3t² − 3, so v(2) = 12 − 3 = 9 m s⁻¹. Option 2 m s⁻¹ is the position s(2) = 8 − 6 = 2 m, not the velocity.

**A865.** D. 6x − 3x²
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-28 -->

Explanation: Expand: f(x) = 3x² − x³, so f′(x) = 6x − 3x². Differentiating each factor separately and multiplying is not valid.

**A866.** A. 2x³ − 4x
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-29 -->

Explanation: Multiply by the power and reduce it by one: ½ × 4x³ = 2x³ and 2 × 2x = 4x, giving 2x³ − 4x. Forgetting the coefficient ½ gives 4x³ − 4x.

**A867.** B. (2, −3)
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-30 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 2x − 6 = −2 gives x = 2, and y = 4 − 12 + 5 = −3. The point (2, −2) has the correct x-value but a wrongly calculated y-value.

**A868.** f′(x) = 4x³ − 8x [1]; = 4x(x² − 2) [1]; so f′(x) = 0 when x = 0, x = √2 or x = −√2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-31 -->

Explanation:

**A869.** dy/dx = 2ax − 3 [1]; at x = 2: 4a − 3 = 9, so a = 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-32 -->

Explanation:

**A870.** f(x) = x³ − x² − 6x [1]; f′(x) = 3x² − 2x − 6 [1]; f′(1) = 3 − 2 − 6 = −5 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-33 -->

Explanation:

**A871.** V′(t) = 12 − 2t [1]; the volume stops increasing when V′(t) = 0 [1]; 12 − 2t = 0, so t = 6 minutes [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-34 -->

Explanation:

**A872.** f′(x) = 3x² − 6x − 9 = 3(x − 3)(x + 1) [1]; f is increasing when f′(x) > 0 [1]; so x < −1 or x > 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-35 -->

Explanation:

**A873.** y = x³ − 3x², so x-intercepts are x = 0 and x = 3 [1]; dy/dx = 3x² − 6x [1]; gradient is 0 at x = 0 and 27 − 18 = 9 at x = 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-36 -->

Explanation:

**A874.** Average rate = (f(4) − f(1))/3 = (0 − (−3))/3 = 1 [1]; f′(x) = 2x − 4 [1]; 2c − 4 = 1 gives c = 2.5 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-dp-37 -->

Explanation:

#### Tangents and stationary points

**A875.** A. y = x − 4
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-1 -->

Explanation: At x = 2, y = 4 − 6 = −2 and dy/dx = 2x − 3 = 1. Tangent: y + 2 = 1(x − 2), so y = x − 4.

**A876.** A. (2, −16)
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-2 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 3x² − 12 = 0 gives x = ±2. f(2) = 8 − 24 = −16 and f(−2) = 16. The gradient changes from negative to positive at x = 2, so (2, −16) is the local minimum and (−2, 16) is the local maximum.

**A877.** C. a local minimum
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-3 -->

Explanation: A change from negative to positive gradient indicates a local minimum. A stationary point of inflection has the same sign of gradient on both sides.

**A878.** A. −1/2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-4 -->

Explanation: The tangent gradient is 2x = 2. The normal gradient is the negative reciprocal, −1/2.

**A879.** D. 9
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-5 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 2x − 6 = 0 gives x = 3. For y = 0 at x = 3: 9 − 18 + k = 0, so k = 9. (The curve is y = (x − 3)².)

**A880.** y(2) = 8 − 4 + 1 = 5 (1 mark); dy/dx = 3x² − 2 = 10 at x = 2 (1 mark); tangent: y − 5 = 10(x − 2), so y = 10x − 15 (1 mark); normal gradient −1/10: y − 5 = −(1/10)(x − 2), so x + 10y = 52 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-6 -->

Explanation:

**A881.** f′(x) = 3x² − 12x + 9 = 3(x − 1)(x − 3) = 0 gives x = 1 or x = 3 (1 mark); f(1) = 4 and f(3) = 0, so points (1, 4) and (3, 0) (1 mark); sign of f′: positive for x < 1, negative for 1 < x < 3, positive for x > 3 (1 mark); so (1, 4) is a local maximum and (3, 0) is a local minimum (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-7 -->

Explanation:

**A882.** dy/dx = 2x + a; at x = 1 this equals the line's gradient 4, so 2 + a = 4 and a = 2 (1 mark); y(1) = 1 + 2 = 3, so the point is (1, 3) (1 mark); tangent: y − 3 = 4(x − 1), so y = 4x − 1 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-8 -->

Explanation:

**A883.** A. 12
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-9 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 3x², so at x = −2 the gradient is 3(4) = 12. A negative answer comes from forgetting that (−2)² is positive.

**A884.** B. 2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-10 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 2x − 4 = 0 gives x = 2. The value 4 is the coefficient of x, not the solution of f′(x) = 0.

**A885.** C. y = 2x
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-11 -->

Explanation: y(1) = 2 and dy/dx = 2x = 2 at x = 1. Tangent: y − 2 = 2(x − 1), so y = 2x.

**A886.** D. y = −½x − 3/2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-12 -->

Explanation: y(3) = −3 and dy/dx = 2x − 4 = 2, so the normal gradient is −½. Normal: y + 3 = −½(x − 3), so y = −½x − 3/2. The line y = 2x − 9 is the tangent.

**A887.** A. 0
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-13 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 3x² + 3 = 3(x² + 1) is always positive, so it is never zero and there are no stationary points. Setting 3x² + 3 = 0 does not give x = ±1 because x² = −1 has no real solutions.

**A888.** C. 9
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-14 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = −2x + 6 = 0 gives x = 3, and f(3) = −9 + 18 = 9. The value 3 is where the maximum occurs, not the maximum value.

**A889.** D. 4
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-15 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 2x − 2 must equal 6, so 2x = 8 and x = 4. Using x = 3 would come from solving 2x = 6 and forgetting the −2.

**A890.** A. 3/2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-16 -->

Explanation: The line has gradient −1/3, so the tangent gradient is 3. Then 2x = 3 gives x = 3/2. Using x = 3 forgets that dy/dx = 2x.

**A891.** B. 3/2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-17 -->

Explanation: y(3) = 9 and the gradient is 6, so the tangent is y = 6x − 9. Setting y = 0 gives x = 3/2. The curve touches the axis only at x = 0, not at the tangent's intercept.

**A892.** C. x = 1 and x = 2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-18 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 6x² − 18x + 12 = 6(x − 1)(x − 2) = 0, so x = 1 or x = 2. The signs are wrong in the options with negative values.

**A893.** D. (0, 0)
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-19 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 3x² − 6x = 3x(x − 2) = 0 gives x = 0 or x = 2. y(0) = 0 and y(2) = −4, and the gradient changes from positive to negative at x = 0, so (0, 0) is the local maximum. (2, −4) is the local minimum.

**A894.** A. a stationary point of inflection
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-20 -->

Explanation: The gradient is zero but does not change sign, so the point is a stationary point of inflection. A local maximum or minimum needs the gradient to change sign.

**A895.** B. y = −x/4 + 1
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-21 -->

Explanation: y(2) = 1/2 and dy/dx = −1/x² = −1/4. Tangent: y − 1/2 = −¼(x − 2), so y = −x/4 + 1. The intercept 1/2 is just the y-value of the point, not the y-intercept.

**A896.** C. 2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-22 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 2ax + 2, so at x = 2: 4a + 2 = 10 and a = 2. Using a = 4 comes from forgetting to subtract the 2 first.

**A897.** D. b = −4, c = 3
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-23 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 2x + b = 0 at x = 2 gives b = −4. Then 4 − 8 + c = −1 gives c = 3. The value c = −1 confuses the y-coordinate with c.

**A898.** C. −16
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-24 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 4x³ − 16x = 4x(x − 2)(x + 2) = 0 gives x = 0, ±2. f(±2) = 16 − 32 = −16 is the minimum, while x = 0 gives a local maximum of 0.

**A899.** B. x = 2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-25 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 2x − 4 = 0 gives x = 2 and y = −3, so the tangent is the horizontal line y = −3. The normal is perpendicular, hence the vertical line x = 2.

**A900.** A. −2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-26 -->

Explanation: The tangent is y = 3x − 2. Solving x³ = 3x − 2 gives x³ − 3x + 2 = (x − 1)²(x + 2) = 0, so the other intersection is x = −2. The double root at x = 1 is the point of tangency.

**A901.** y(−1) = 1 − 3 = −2 [1]; dy/dx = 2x + 3 = 1 at x = −1 [1]; tangent: y + 2 = 1(x + 1), so y = x − 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-27 -->

Explanation:

**A902.** dy/dx = 2x − 8 = 0 gives x = 4 [1]; y(4) = 16 − 32 + 5 = −11, so the point is (4, −11) [1]; the gradient changes from negative to positive (or the parabola opens upwards), so it is a local minimum [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-28 -->

Explanation:

**A903.** f′(x) = 3x² + 6x − 9 = 3(x + 3)(x − 1) = 0 gives x = −3 or x = 1 [1]; f(−3) = −27 + 27 + 27 = 27 and f(1) = 1 + 3 − 9 = −5 [1]; f′ is positive for x < −3, negative for −3 < x < 1 and positive for x > 1 [1]; so (−3, 27) is a local maximum and (1, −5) is a local minimum [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-29 -->

Explanation:

**A904.** dy/dx = 2x − 4 = 2 gives x = 3 [1]; y(3) = 9 − 12 + 3 = 0, so the point is (3, 0) [1]; tangent: y − 0 = 2(x − 3), so y = 2x − 6 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-30 -->

Explanation:

**A905.** y(2) = 3 and dy/dx = 2x − 2 = 2 at x = 2 [1]; tangent: y − 3 = 2(x − 2), so y = 2x − 1 [1]; it meets the y-axis at A(0, −1) and the x-axis at B(1/2, 0) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-31 -->

Explanation:

**A906.** dy/dx = 3x² + 1 [1]; since 3x² ≥ 0, dy/dx ≥ 1 > 0 for all x, so dy/dx is never zero and there are no stationary points [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-32 -->

Explanation:

**A907.** y(−1) = 1 + 1 = 2 [1]; dy/dx = 2x − 1 = −3 at x = −1 [1]; normal gradient = 1/3 [1]; y − 2 = (1/3)(x + 1), so x − 3y + 7 = 0 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-33 -->

Explanation:

**A908.** dy/dx = 3x² − 12 = 0 gives x = 2 or x = −2 [1]; the gradient changes from negative to positive at x = 2, so the local minimum is at x = 2 [1]; y(2) = 8 − 24 + c = 0, so c = 16 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-34 -->

Explanation:

**A909.** Point (1, 3): a + b = 3 [1]; dy/dx = 2ax + b [1]; gradient 5 at x = 1: 2a + b = 5 [1]; subtracting gives a = 2 and b = 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-35 -->

Explanation:

**A910.** dy/dx = 3x² − 3 = 0 [1]; x = 1 or x = −1, giving y(1) = −2 and y(−1) = 2 so the points are (1, −2) and (−1, 2) [1]; the tangents are y = −2 and y = 2 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-36 -->

Explanation:

**A911.** dy/dx = 3x² = 0 at x = 0, so (0, 0) is stationary [1]; dy/dx > 0 for x < 0 and dy/dx > 0 for x > 0 [1]; the gradient does not change sign, so it is a stationary point of inflection, not a local maximum or minimum [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-37 -->

Explanation:

**A912.** D. (−1, 6)
<!-- id: methods12a3-ts-38 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 3(x − 3)(x + 1) = 0 gives x = −1 or x = 3. The gradient changes from positive to negative at x = −1, where y = −1 − 3 + 9 + 1 = 6, while (3, −26) is the local minimum.

#### Applications of differentiation

**A913.** D. 100 m²
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-1 -->

Explanation: With width x, length is 20 − x, so A = x(20 − x) and A′ = 20 − 2x = 0 gives x = 10. The rectangle is a 10 m square with area 100 m².

**A914.** D. t = 1 and t = 3
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-2 -->

Explanation: v(t) = x′(t) = 3t² − 12t + 9 = 3(t − 1)(t − 3), which is zero at t = 1 and t = 3.

**A915.** C. 2 cm
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-3 -->

Explanation: V = 4x³ − 48x² + 144x, so V′ = 12x² − 96x + 144 = 12(x − 2)(x − 6). Only x = 2 is feasible (x < 6) and it gives the maximum, V = 2 × 8² = 128 cm³. At x = 6 the volume is zero.

**A916.** D. $9 per item
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-4 -->

Explanation: C′(q) = 0.04q + 5, so C′(100) = 4 + 5 = $9 per item.

**A917.** C. 5
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-5 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 2x − 4 = 0 at x = 2, where f(2) = −4 (the minimum). Endpoints: f(0) = 0 and f(5) = 25 − 20 = 5. The greatest value is 5, at the endpoint x = 5.

**A918.** Let the two sides perpendicular to the wall be x m, so the side parallel to the wall is 100 − 2x (1 mark); A = x(100 − 2x) = 100x − 2x² (1 mark); A′ = 100 − 4x = 0 gives x = 25 (1 mark); dimensions 25 m by 50 m, maximum area 1250 m² (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-6 -->

Explanation:

**A919.** P′(q) = −4q + 80 (1 mark); P′(q) = 0 gives q = 20, a maximum since the parabola opens downward (1 mark); P(20) = −800 + 1600 − 200 = $600 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-7 -->

Explanation:

**A920.** (a) v(t) = 3t² − 18t + 24 = 3(t − 2)(t − 4) (1 mark); (b) at rest at t = 2 s and t = 4 s (1 mark); x(2) = 8 − 36 + 48 = 20 cm and x(4) = 64 − 144 + 96 = 16 cm (1 mark); (c) v > 0 for 0 < t < 2 (moving in the positive direction), v < 0 for 2 < t < 4 (moving in the negative direction), v > 0 for t > 4 (positive direction again) (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-8 -->

Explanation:

**A921.** A. x = −1 and x = 1
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-9 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 3x² − 3 = 3(x − 1)(x + 1) = 0 gives x = ±1. Solving x³ − 3x = 0 instead finds the x-intercepts (x = 0, ±√3), not the stationary points.

**A922.** B. 7
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-10 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 2x + 3, so the gradient at x = 2 is 7. The value 10 is the y-coordinate at x = 2, not the gradient.

**A923.** C. y = 6x − 9
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-11 -->

Explanation: The gradient is 2x = 6 and the point is (3, 9), so y − 9 = 6(x − 3) giving y = 6x − 9. Using y = 6x + 9 ignores that the line must pass through (3, 9).

**A924.** A. 10π
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-12 -->

Explanation: dA/dr = 2πr, which is 10π at r = 5. The value 25π is the area itself, not its rate of change.

**A925.** B. −6 m s⁻²
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-13 -->

Explanation: v = 3t² − 12t + 9 and a = v′ = 6t − 12, so a(1) = −6 m s⁻². The velocity at t = 1 is 0, which is a different quantity, and x(1) = 4 m is the position.

**A926.** C. −4
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-14 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 2x − 6 = 0 gives x = 3, and f(3) = 9 − 18 + 5 = −4. The value 3 is where the minimum occurs, not the minimum value.

**A927.** A. f(x) = x³
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-15 -->

Explanation: For f(x) = x³, f′(0) = 0 and f′ is positive on both sides of 0, so the gradient does not change sign. The functions x², −x² and x⁴ have f′(0) = 0 but have a minimum or maximum there.

**A928.** B. −2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-16 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = 3x² − 3 = 0 gives x = 1 in the interval, with f(1) = −2. The endpoints give f(0) = 0 and f(3) = 18. The value 2 is f(−1), but x = −1 is outside the interval.

**A929.** C. 50
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-17 -->

Explanation: S = x² + (10 − x)², so S′ = 2x − 2(10 − x) = 4x − 20 = 0 gives x = 5, and S = 25 + 25 = 50. The value 25 is only the square of one of the numbers.

**A930.** D. 4
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-18 -->

Explanation: The average rate of change is (f(3) − f(1))/(3 − 1) = (9 − 1)/2 = 4. The value 8 forgets to divide by the interval width, and 6 is the instantaneous rate at x = 3.

**A931.** A. 16π
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-19 -->

Explanation: dV/dr = 4πr², which is 16π at r = 2. The value 32π/3 is the volume itself rather than its rate of change.

**A932.** B. 2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-20 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 2x − 4 = 0 gives x = 2. The value −3 is the y-coordinate of the point, not its x-coordinate.

**A933.** C. 2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-21 -->

Explanation: f′(x) = −3x² + 3 = 0 gives x = ±1. The gradient changes from negative to positive at x = −1 (a local minimum, −2) and from positive to negative at x = 1 (a local maximum), where f(1) = 2.

**A934.** D. (2, 4)
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-22 -->

Explanation: Parallel means gradient 4, so 2x = 4 gives x = 2 and y = 4. The point (4, 16) comes from wrongly setting x² = 4x.

**A935.** A. 3
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-23 -->

Explanation: v = 2t − 6 changes sign at t = 3. At t = 6 the particle is merely back at its starting position x = 0, still moving in the positive direction.

**A936.** B. $30
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-24 -->

Explanation: C′(q) = 20 + 0.2q, so C′(50) = 20 + 10 = $30 per item. The value $1750 is the total cost C(50), not the rate of change.

**A937.** C. −1/2
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-25 -->

Explanation: The tangent gradient is 2x = 2, so the normal gradient is the negative reciprocal, −1/2. The value 2 is the tangent gradient and 1/2 omits the negative sign.

**A938.** dy/dx = 3x² − 2, so the gradient at x = 2 is 10 [1]. When x = 2, y = 8 − 4 = 4, so the point is (2, 4) [1]. y − 4 = 10(x − 2), so y = 10x − 16 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-26 -->

Explanation:

**A939.** f′(x) = 3x² − 12x + 9 = 3(x − 1)(x − 3) [1]. Stationary at x = 1 and x = 3, with f(1) = 4 and f(3) = 0 [1]. f′ > 0 for x < 1, f′ < 0 for 1 < x < 3 and f′ > 0 for x > 3 [1]. So (1, 4) is a local maximum and (3, 0) is a local minimum [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-27 -->

Explanation:

**A940.** f′(x) = 6x² − 6x − 12 = 6(x − 2)(x + 1), so x = −1 and x = 2 are stationary points in the interval [1]. f(−1) = 7 and f(2) = −20 [1]. Endpoints: f(−2) = −4 and f(3) = −9 [1]. Greatest value is 7 (at x = −1) and least value is −20 (at x = 2) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-28 -->

Explanation:

**A941.** Height h = 32/x², so S = x² + 4xh = x² + 128/x [1]. S′ = 2x − 128/x² = 0 gives x³ = 64, so x = 4 [1]. S′ is negative for x < 4 and positive for x > 4, so this is a minimum [1]. S(4) = 16 + 32 = 48 cm² [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-29 -->

Explanation:

**A942.** When x = 1, y = 1 − 3 = −2, so the point is (1, −2) [1]. dy/dx = 2x − 3 = −1 at x = 1, so the normal gradient is 1 [1]. y + 2 = 1(x − 1), so y = x − 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-30 -->

Explanation:

**A943.** (a) v(t) = 6t² − 18t + 12 [1] and a(t) = 12t − 18 [1]. (b) v = 6(t − 1)(t − 2) = 0, so at rest when t = 1 s and t = 2 s [1]. a(1) = −6 m s⁻² and a(2) = 6 m s⁻² [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-31 -->

Explanation:

**A944.** Revenue R = q(50 − 2q), so profit P = 50q − 2q² − 10q − 100 = 40q − 2q² − 100 [1]. P′ = 40 − 4q = 0 gives q = 10, a maximum as the parabola opens downward [1]. P(10) = 400 − 200 − 100 = $100 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-32 -->

Explanation:

**A945.** f′(x) = 3x² + 3 ≥ 3 > 0 for all x, so f′(x) = 0 has no solution [1]. Hence the function is strictly increasing for all x [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-33 -->

Explanation:

**A946.** f′(x) = 3x² − 6x + 3 = 3(x − 1)², which is zero only at x = 1 [1]. f′(x) ≥ 0 on both sides of x = 1, so the gradient does not change sign and the point is a stationary point of inflection, not a maximum or minimum [1]. f(1) = 1 − 3 + 3 = 1, so the point is (1, 1) [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-34 -->

Explanation:

**A947.** A. 45 m
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-35 -->

Explanation: h′(t) = 30 − 10t = 0 gives t = 3, and h(3) = 90 − 45 = 45 m. The time 3 s must be substituted back into h(t) to obtain the height.

**A948.** B. 27 cm³ per cm
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-36 -->

Explanation: dV/ds = 3s², so at s = 3 the rate is 3 × 9 = 27 cm³ per cm. The value 9 is s², which omits the factor 3 from the power rule.

**A949.** P = 2(x + 36/x) = 2x + 72/x [1]; P′ = 2 − 72/x² = 0 gives x² = 36, so x = 6 m [1]; minimum perimeter P = 12 + 12 = 24 m [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-37 -->

Explanation:

**A950.** T′(t) = −2t + 8 = 0 gives t = 4 [1]; T(4) = −16 + 32 + 5 = 21 [1]; endpoints T(0) = T(8) = 5, so the maximum is 21 °C at 10 am [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-ad-38 -->

Explanation:

#### Antidifferentiation

**A951.** B. 2x³ − 2x² + x + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-1 -->

Explanation: Increase each power by 1 and divide by the new power: 6x³/3 − 4x²/2 + x = 2x³ − 2x² + x, plus a constant c. Option C omits the constant.

**A952.** A. 8
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-2 -->

Explanation: f(x) = 2x² − 3x + c. f(1) = 2 − 3 + c = 5 gives c = 6. Then f(2) = 8 − 6 + 6 = 8.

**A953.** C. x⁶/6 + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-3 -->

Explanation: Add 1 to the power and divide by the new power: x⁶/6 + c. 5x⁴ is the derivative, not the antiderivative.

**A954.** C. 5
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-4 -->

Explanation: y = x³ − 2x + c. At (1, 4): 1 − 2 + c = 4, so c = 5. The y-intercept is y(0) = 5.

**A955.** D. 2x²
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-5 -->

Explanation: Antiderivatives of 2x have the form x² + c. The derivative of 2x² is 4x, so 2x² is not an antiderivative.

**A956.** f(x) = x³ − 2x² + 2x + c (1 mark); f(2) = 8 − 8 + 4 + c = 9 (1 mark); c = 5, so f(x) = x³ − 2x² + 2x + 5 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-6 -->

Explanation:

**A957.** x(t) = 3t² − 12t + c (1 mark); x(0) = 5 gives c = 5, so x(t) = 3t² − 12t + 5 (1 mark); x(3) = 27 − 36 + 5 = −4 m (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-7 -->

Explanation:

**A958.** Stationary at x = 2: 4 + a = 0, so a = −4 (1 mark); dy/dx = 2x − 4, so y = x² − 4x + c (1 mark); at (2, −1): 4 − 8 + c = −1, so c = 3 (1 mark); y = x² − 4x + 3 (1 mark).
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-8 -->

Explanation:

**A959.** A. 7x + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-9 -->

Explanation: The antiderivative of a constant k is kx, so ∫ 7 dx = 7x + c. Option 7 + c is the mistake of leaving the constant unchanged, but its derivative is 0, not 7.

**A960.** B. −1/x + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-10 -->

Explanation: Add 1 to the power to get x⁻¹ and divide by −1, giving −1/x + c. The expression −2/x³ is the derivative of x⁻², not its antiderivative.

**A961.** C. (2/3)x^(3/2) + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-11 -->

Explanation: Write √x = x^(1/2), add 1 to the power to get 3/2 and divide by 3/2, which gives (2/3)x^(3/2) + c. Dividing by the old power instead of the new power gives the wrong coefficient.

**A962.** A. x²/2 − 1/x + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-12 -->

Explanation: Write 1/x² as x⁻², so the antiderivative is x²/2 + x⁻¹/(−1) = x²/2 − 1/x + c. The sign of the second term is negative because of the division by −1.

**A963.** B. 3x² − 5
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-13 -->

Explanation: f is the derivative of F, so f(x) = 3x² − 5. Integrating F instead of differentiating it gives the first option, which goes the wrong way.

**A964.** C. x³/3 + x² − 3x + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-14 -->

Explanation: Expand first: (x + 3)(x − 1) = x² + 2x − 3, then integrate term by term to get x³/3 + x² − 3x + c. You cannot integrate each factor separately and multiply.

**A965.** D. The derivative of a constant is zero, so many functions share the same derivative
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-15 -->

Explanation: Functions that differ only by a constant have identical derivatives, so the antiderivative is a family of functions, written with + c. The value of c is found only if extra information, such as a point on the curve, is given.

**A966.** A. 15
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-16 -->

Explanation: f(x) = 2x³ − 2x + c and f(0) = 3 gives c = 3. Then f(2) = 16 − 4 + 3 = 15. Leaving out the constant gives 12.

**A967.** B. −3.5
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-17 -->

Explanation: y = 2x² − 6x + 1, which has its minimum where dy/dx = 0, at x = 1.5. Then y = 4.5 − 9 + 1 = −3.5; the value 1.5 is the x-coordinate of the minimum, not the minimum value.

**A968.** C. 2 m
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-18 -->

Explanation: x(t) = t³ − 4t + c and x(0) = 2 gives c = 2. So x(2) = 8 − 8 + 2 = 2 m. Forgetting the initial position gives 0 m rather than 2 m.

**A969.** A. y = x³ + 2
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-19 -->

Explanation: y = x³ + c and 10 = 8 + c gives c = 2. The line y = 6x − 2 also passes through (2, 10), but it comes from differentiating 3x² rather than integrating it.

**A970.** B. −2/x² + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-20 -->

Explanation: Add 1 to the power to get x⁻² and divide by −2: 4x⁻²/(−2) = −2x⁻² = −2/x². The expression −12/x⁴ is the derivative, and the positive 2/x² has the wrong sign.

**A971.** C. x⁴/4 − x² + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-21 -->

Explanation: Divide each term by x first: (x⁴ − 2x²)/x = x³ − 2x. Then integrate to get x⁴/4 − x² + c. Integrating the numerator and ignoring the division gives the first option.

**A972.** D. 11
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-22 -->

Explanation: y = 6x − x² + 2, and dy/dx = 0 at x = 3. Then y = 18 − 9 + 2 = 11; the value 9 comes from forgetting the constant 2.

**A973.** B. 14 m s⁻¹
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-23 -->

Explanation: v(t) = 6t + c and v(0) = −4 gives v(t) = 6t − 4, so v(3) = 18 − 4 = 14 m s⁻¹. Ignoring the initial velocity gives 18 m s⁻¹.

**A974.** Integrate term by term: x⁴ − 3x² + 5x [1]; add the constant of integration, so the answer is x⁴ − 3x² + 5x + c [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-24 -->

Explanation:

**A975.** f(x) = x³/3 − 3x² + 5x + c [1]; f(0) = 2 gives c = 2, so f(x) = x³/3 − 3x² + 5x + 2 [1]; f(3) = 9 − 27 + 15 + 2 = −1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-25 -->

Explanation:

**A976.** y = x³ − 12x + c [1]; at (2, −10): 8 − 24 + c = −10, so c = 6 and y = x³ − 12x + 6 [1]; the y-intercept is 6 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-26 -->

Explanation:

**A977.** h(t) = 10t − 5t² + c [1]; h(0) = 2 gives h(t) = 10t − 5t² + 2 [1]; greatest height when v = 0, so t = 1 s [1]; h(1) = 10 − 5 + 2 = 7 m [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-27 -->

Explanation:

**A978.** For example 2x² and 2x² + 5 (any two functions of the form 2x² + c) [1]; the derivative of a constant is zero, so any constant can be added to an antiderivative without changing its derivative [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-28 -->

Explanation:

**A979.** Write 2/x² as 2x⁻², so y = −2x⁻¹ + 3x + c [1]; at (1, 4): −2 + 3 + c = 4, so c = 3 [1]; y = 3x − 2/x + 3 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-29 -->

Explanation:

**A980.** Factorise the numerator: x² − 4 = (x − 2)(x + 2) [1]; cancel to get x − 2 [1]; integrate to get x²/2 − 2x + c [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-30 -->

Explanation:

**A981.** y = kx²/2 + c [1]; the point (0, 1) gives c = 1 [1]; the point (2, 9) gives 2k + 1 = 9, so k = 4 [1]; y = 2x² + 1 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-31 -->

Explanation:

**A982.** v(t) = 3t² − 2t + c and v(0) = 1 gives v(t) = 3t² − 2t + 1 [1]; x(t) = t³ − t² + t + c with x(0) = 0 giving x(t) = t³ − t² + t [1]; x(2) = 8 − 4 + 2 = 6 m [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-32 -->

Explanation:

**A983.** Differentiate F: F′(x) = 2x³ − 6x + 7 [1]; since F′(x) = f(x), F is an antiderivative of f [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-33 -->

Explanation:

**A984.** C. x⁴/2 − 3x²/2 + c
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-34 -->

Explanation: Add 1 to each power and divide by the new power: 2x⁴/4 − 3x²/2 + c = x⁴/2 − 3x²/2 + c. Dividing the second term by 2 is needed, so 3x² alone is wrong.

**A985.** A. 7
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-35 -->

Explanation: y = 2x⁴ + c, and y(0) = 5 gives c = 5. Then y(1) = 2 + 5 = 7, whereas 2 ignores the constant.

**A986.** D. 15 m
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-36 -->

Explanation: x(t) = 2t² − t, so x(3) = 18 − 3 = 15 m. Evaluating v(3) = 11 gives a velocity, not a position.

**A987.** f(x) = 3x³ − 2x² + x + c [1]; f(−1) = −3 − 2 − 1 + c = 2 [1]; c = 8, so f(x) = 3x³ − 2x² + x + 8 [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-37 -->

Explanation:

**A988.** V(t) = 3t² − 0.1t³ + c [1]; V(0) = 50 gives c = 50, so V(t) = 3t² − 0.1t³ + 50 [1]; V(10) = 300 − 100 + 50 = 250 litres [1].
<!-- id: methods12a3-an-38 -->

Explanation:

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**A989.** A parabola, a line and a tangent
<!-- id: vce-methods12-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** \(f(x) = (x - 2)^2 - 1\); turning point \((2, -1)\) *(2 marks)*

1. Complete the square: \(x^2 - 4x + 3 = (x - 2)^2 - 4 + 3 = (x - 2)^2 - 1\). *(1 mark)*
2. In the form \(a(x - h)^2 + k\) the turning point is \((h, k) = (2, -1)\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Reading the turning point as \((-2, -1)\). In \((x - h)^2\) the \(x\)-coordinate is \(+h\), so \((x - 2)^2\) gives \(x = 2\).

Topic: Quadratic functions (vce-methods12-quadratic-functions)

**b.** \(x = 1\) and \(x = 3\) *(1 mark)*

1. \(x^2 - 4x + 3 = (x - 1)(x - 3) = 0\), so \(x = 1\) or \(x = 3\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Getting the signs wrong when factorising: the factors must multiply to \(+3\) and add to \(-4\), so they are \(-1\) and \(-3\).

Topic: Quadratic functions (vce-methods12-quadratic-functions)

**c.** \(y = 4x - 13\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(f(4) = 16 - 16 + 3 = 3\), and \(f'(x) = 2x - 4\), so the gradient is \(f'(4) = 4\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(y - 3 = 4(x - 4)\), so \(y = 4x - 13\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using \(f(4)\) as the gradient. The gradient of the tangent is \(f'(4)\).

Topic: Tangents and stationary points (vce-methods12-tangents-and-stationary-points)

**d.** \((1, 0)\) and \((4, 3)\) *(2 marks)*

1. Solve \(x^2 - 4x + 3 = x - 1\): \(x^2 - 5x + 4 = 0\), so \((x - 1)(x - 4) = 0\) and \(x = 1\) or \(x = 4\). *(1 mark)*
2. Substitute into \(y = x - 1\): the points are \((1, 0)\) and \((4, 3)\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving only the \(x\)-values. The question asks for coordinates, so find \(y\) as well.

Topic: Linear functions and coordinate geometry (vce-methods12-linear-functions-and-coordinate-geometry)

**e.** \(y = x^2\) *(2 marks)*

1. A translation 2 units left replaces \(x\) with \(x + 2\), and 1 unit up adds 1: \(y = f(x + 2) + 1\). *(1 mark)*
2. Using turning point form, \(y = (x + 2 - 2)^2 - 1 + 1 = x^2\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Replacing \(x\) with \(x - 2\) for a move to the left. Moving left by 2 means replacing \(x\) with \(x + 2\).

Topic: Transformations of graphs (vce-methods12-transformations-of-graphs)

**f.** \(0\) *(1 mark)*

1. \(\dfrac{f(4) - f(0)}{4 - 0} = \dfrac{3 - 3}{4} = 0\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Finding the instantaneous rate of change at one point, such as \(f'(4)\). The average rate of change uses the two end points.

Topic: Rates of change (vce-methods12-rates-of-change)

**A990.** Growing bacteria and contaminated dishes
<!-- id: vce-methods12-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 12 marks*

**a.** 500 *(1 mark)*

1. \(N(0) = 500 \times 2^0 = 500\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Taking \(2^0\) to be 0. Any non-zero number to the power 0 is 1.

Topic: Exponential equations and modelling (vce-methods12-exponential-equations-and-modelling)

**b.** 2000 *(1 mark)*

1. \(N(6) = 500 \times 2^{6/3} = 500 \times 2^2 = 2000\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Multiplying 500 by \(\frac{6}{3} = 2\) instead of using it as a power. \(2^{6/3} = 2^2 = 4\), so the number has doubled twice.

Topic: Exponential equations and modelling (vce-methods12-exponential-equations-and-modelling)

**c.** 12 hours *(2 marks)*

1. \(500 \times 2^{t/3} = 8000\), so \(2^{t/3} = 16 = 2^4\). *(1 mark)*
2. Equate the indices: \(\frac{t}{3} = 4\), so \(t = 12\) hours. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Dividing 8000 by 500 and then by 2, as if the growth were linear. The number doubles every 3 hours, so write 16 as a power of 2.

Topic: Exponential equations and modelling (vce-methods12-exponential-equations-and-modelling)

**d.** \(k = \dfrac{\log_e 2}{3}\) *(2 marks)*

1. Write the base 2 as a power of \(e\): \(2 = e^{\log_e 2}\), so \(2^{t/3} = e^{\frac{t}{3}\log_e 2}\). *(1 mark)*
2. Comparing with \(e^{kt}\), \(k = \dfrac{\log_e 2}{3}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Writing \(k = \log_e \frac{2}{3}\). The 3 divides the whole logarithm: \(k = \frac{1}{3}\log_e 2\).

Topic: Logarithms and log laws (vce-methods12-logarithms-and-log-laws)

**e.** \(\frac{1}{15}\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(\Pr(\text{first contaminated}) = \frac{3}{10}\); then 2 of the remaining 9 are contaminated, so the second has probability \(\frac{2}{9}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(\frac{3}{10} \times \frac{2}{9} = \frac{6}{90} = \frac{1}{15}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using \(\frac{3}{10} \times \frac{3}{10}\). Without replacement, the second probability changes to \(\frac{2}{9}\).

Topic: Probability rules and Venn diagrams (vce-methods12-probability-rules-and-venn-diagrams)

**f.** \(\frac{7}{15}\) *(2 marks)*

1. Exactly one can happen two ways: contaminated then normal, \(\frac{3}{10} \times \frac{7}{9}\), or normal then contaminated, \(\frac{7}{10} \times \frac{3}{9}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(\frac{21}{90} + \frac{21}{90} = \frac{42}{90} = \frac{7}{15}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Counting only one order. 'Exactly one' includes both orders, so add the two branches.

Topic: Probability rules and Venn diagrams (vce-methods12-probability-rules-and-venn-diagrams)

**g.** \(\frac{1}{8}\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(\Pr(\text{at least one}) = \frac{1}{15} + \frac{7}{15} = \frac{8}{15}\) (or \(1 - \frac{7}{10} \times \frac{6}{9} = \frac{8}{15}\)). *(1 mark)*
2. \(\Pr(\text{both} \mid \text{at least one}) = \dfrac{1/15}{8/15} = \frac{1}{8}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Dividing by the probability of exactly one (\(\frac{7}{15}\)). The given event is 'at least one', which also includes 'both'.

Topic: Conditional probability and independence (vce-methods12-conditional-probability-and-independence)

**A991.** A Ferris wheel
<!-- id: vce-methods12-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 9 marks*

**a.** Minimum 2 m, maximum 38 m, period 30 minutes *(3 marks)*

1. Minimum when \(\cos = 1\): \(20 - 18 = 2\) m. *(1 mark)*
2. Maximum when \(\cos = -1\): \(20 + 18 = 38\) m. *(1 mark)*
3. Period \(= \dfrac{2\pi}{\pi/15} = 30\) minutes. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving the period as \(\frac{\pi}{15}\). Period = \(2\pi\) divided by the coefficient of \(t\).

Topic: Graphs of sine and cosine (vce-methods12-graphs-of-sine-and-cosine)

**b.** 11 m *(1 mark)*

1. \(h(5) = 20 - 18\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = 20 - 18 \times \frac{1}{2} = 11\) m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using \(\cos\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\). That is \(\cos\frac{\pi}{6}\).

Topic: Graphs of sine and cosine (vce-methods12-graphs-of-sine-and-cosine)

**c.** 10 minutes *(2 marks)*

1. \(20 - 18\cos\left(\frac{\pi t}{15}\right) = 29\), so \(\cos\left(\frac{\pi t}{15}\right) = -\frac{1}{2}\). *(1 mark)*
2. The first solution is \(\frac{\pi t}{15} = \frac{2\pi}{3}\), so \(t = 10\) minutes. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Taking \(\frac{\pi t}{15} = \frac{\pi}{3}\), which solves \(\cos = +\frac{1}{2}\).

Topic: Solving trigonometric equations (vce-methods12-solving-trigonometric-equations)

**d.** 1.8 m per minute *(1 mark)*

1. \(\dfrac{h(5) - h(0)}{5 - 0} = \dfrac{11 - 2}{5} = 1.8\) m per minute. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Dividing 11 by 5. The seat starts at 2 m, not 0 m.

Topic: Rates of change (vce-methods12-rates-of-change)

**e.** 10 minutes *(2 marks)*

1. \(h > 29\) when \(\cos\left(\frac{\pi t}{15}\right) < -\frac{1}{2}\), that is \(\frac{2\pi}{3} < \frac{\pi t}{15} < \frac{4\pi}{3}\). *(1 mark)*
2. So \(10 < t < 20\): the seat is above 29 m for 10 minutes of each rotation. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Finding only the first time (10 minutes). The question asks for a duration, so find when it drops below 29 m again.

Topic: Solving trigonometric equations (vce-methods12-solving-trigonometric-equations)

**A992.** A cubic and a bag of balls
<!-- id: vce-methods12-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 9 marks*

**a.** \(p(3) = 27 - 27 - 27 + 27 = 0\), so \(x - 3\) is a factor. *(1 mark)*

1. \(p(3) = 27 - 27 - 27 + 27 = 0\); by the factor theorem, \(x - 3\) is a factor. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Substituting \(x = -3\). For the factor \(x - 3\), substitute \(x = 3\).

Topic: Factor and remainder theorems (vce-methods12-factor-and-remainder-theorems)

**b.** \(p(x) = (x - 3)^2(x + 3)\); \(x = 3\) or \(x = -3\) *(2 marks)*

1. Group: \(x^2(x - 3) - 9(x - 3) = (x - 3)(x^2 - 9) = (x - 3)(x - 3)(x + 3)\). *(1 mark)*
2. So \(p(x) = (x - 3)^2(x + 3)\), with solutions \(x = 3\) (repeated) and \(x = -3\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Stopping at \((x - 3)(x^2 - 9)\). \(x^2 - 9\) is a difference of two squares.

Topic: Solving polynomial equations (vce-methods12-solving-polynomial-equations)

**c.** \((-1, 32)\), a local maximum; \((3, 0)\), a local minimum *(3 marks)*

1. \(p'(x) = 3x^2 - 6x - 9 = 3(x - 3)(x + 1)\), so \(x = -1\) or \(x = 3\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(p(-1) = -1 - 3 + 9 + 27 = 32\) and \(p(3) = 0\). *(1 mark)*
3. For a positive cubic, the first stationary point is a local maximum and the second a local minimum: \((-1, 32)\) maximum, \((3, 0)\) minimum. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Forgetting to substitute back into \(p(x)\) for the \(y\)-coordinates.

Topic: Tangents and stationary points (vce-methods12-tangents-and-stationary-points)

**d.** \(\dfrac{x^4}{4} - x^3 - \dfrac{9x^2}{2} + 27x + c\) *(1 mark)*

1. Increase each power by 1 and divide by the new power: \(\frac{x^4}{4} - x^3 - \frac{9x^2}{2} + 27x + c\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Differentiating instead of antidifferentiating.

Topic: Antidifferentiation (vce-methods12-antidifferentiation)

**e.** \(\frac{2}{5}\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(\Pr(\text{both red}) = \frac{3}{5} \times \frac{2}{4} = \frac{6}{20}\); \(\Pr(\text{both blue}) = \frac{2}{5} \times \frac{1}{4} = \frac{2}{20}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(\Pr(\text{same colour}) = \frac{6}{20} + \frac{2}{20} = \frac{8}{20} = \frac{2}{5}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using \(\frac{3}{5} \times \frac{3}{5}\). Without replacement, the second probability changes.

Topic: Probability rules and Venn diagrams (vce-methods12-probability-rules-and-venn-diagrams)

## VCE Units 3 & 4

### Polynomials & transformations

#### Polynomial graphs and key features

**A993.** A. The graph touches the x-axis and turns
<!-- id: methods-1-1-1 -->

Explanation: The factor (x − 2) has even multiplicity 2, so the graph touches the x-axis at x = 2 and turns there.

**A994.** B. (−1, −8)
<!-- id: methods-1-1-2 -->

Explanation: y = a(x − h)³ + k has its stationary point of inflection at (h, k); here h = −1 and k = −8.

**A995.** C. 6
<!-- id: methods-1-1-3 -->

Explanation: Substituting x = 0 gives (−1)(2)(−3) = 6.

**A996.** x-intercepts: (−2, 0), (0, 0) and (3, 0); y-intercept: (0, 0).
<!-- id: methods-1-1-4 -->

Explanation: Factorise: x(x² − x − 6) = x(x − 3)(x + 2). Award marks for correct factorisation and all intercepts.

**A997.** D. y → −∞ as x → ±∞
<!-- id: methods-1-1-5 -->

Explanation: The polynomial has degree 4 (even) with leading coefficient −2 (negative), so both ends go down.

**A998.** A. A maximum turning point at (3, 2)
<!-- id: methods-1-1-6 -->

Explanation: The graph of y = x⁴ is reflected in the x-axis (so the turning point is a maximum) and translated 3 right and 2 up.

**A999.** B. 3
<!-- id: methods-1-1-7 -->

Explanation: x³ − 4x = x(x − 2)(x + 2), giving x-intercepts at x = −2, 0 and 2.

**A1000.** Local maximum at (1, 5) and local minimum at (3, 1).
<!-- id: methods-1-1-8 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 3x² − 12x + 9 = 3(x − 1)(x − 3) = 0 gives x = 1, 3; y(1) = 5, y(3) = 1. For a positive cubic, the first stationary point is the maximum.

**A1001.** C. y = (x + 2)²(x − 1)(x − 4)
<!-- id: methods-1-1-9 -->

Explanation: y = a(x + 2)²(x − 1)(x − 4); at x = 0, a(4)(−1)(−4) = 16a = 16, so a = 1.

**A1002.** D. (−1, 2)
<!-- id: methods-1-1-10 -->

Explanation: dy/dx = 3x² − 3 = 0 gives x = ±1; y(−1) = −1 + 3 = 2, and the graph rises then falls there, so (−1, 2) is the local maximum.

**A1003.** A. [−5, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-1-1-11 -->

Explanation: The minimum turning point is (1, −5) and the parabola opens upward, so y ≥ −5 (inclusive).

**A1004.** y = 2(x + 1)(x − 2)(x − 4)
<!-- id: methods-1-1-12 -->

Explanation: Let y = a(x + 1)(x − 2)(x − 4). At x = 0: a(1)(−2)(−4) = 8a = 16, so a = 2.

**A1005.** B. {0} ∪ [4, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-1-1-13 -->

Explanation: x²(x − 4) ≥ 0. Since x² ≥ 0, we need x − 4 ≥ 0 or x² = 0, so x = 0 or x ≥ 4.

**A1006.** C. y = 2(x − 2)³ − 1
<!-- id: methods-1-1-14 -->

Explanation: h = 2, k = −1, and 1 = a(1)³ − 1 gives a = 2.

**A1007.** D. 4
<!-- id: methods-1-1-15 -->

Explanation: (x² − 1)(x² − 4) = 0 gives x = ±1 and x = ±2, four distinct real solutions.

**A1008.** x = −3, −2, 2, 3
<!-- id: methods-1-1-16 -->

Explanation: Treat as a quadratic in x²: (x² − 4)(x² − 9) = 0, so x² = 4 or x² = 9.

**A1009.** A. 13
<!-- id: methods-1-1-17 -->

Explanation: (f(3) − f(1))/(3 − 1) = (27 − 1)/2 = 13. (12 is the instantaneous rate at x = 2.)

**A1010.** B. 9
<!-- id: methods-1-1-18 -->

Explanation: A single touching point requires the discriminant 36 − 4k = 0, so k = 9.

**A1011.** C. (−2, −3)
<!-- id: methods-1-1-19 -->

Explanation: Completing the square: y = (x + 2)² − 3, so the turning point is (−2, −3).

**A1012.** −2 < k < 2
<!-- id: methods-1-1-20 -->

Explanation: x³ − 3x has a local maximum of 2 at x = −1 and a local minimum of −2 at x = 1. The horizontal line y = −k meets the curve three times when −2 < −k < 2, i.e. −2 < k < 2.

**A1013.** D. y < 0 for all x > 0 except x = 2
<!-- id: methods-1-1-21 -->

Explanation: For x > 0, −x is negative and (x − 2)² ≥ 0, so y ≤ 0 with equality only at x = 2, where the graph touches the axis.

**A1014.** A. (−∞, −2) ∪ (1, 5)
<!-- id: methods-1-1-22 -->

Explanation: The positive cubic has roots −2, 1, 5; it is below the x-axis to the left of −2 and between 1 and 5.

**A1015.** B. 4
<!-- id: methods-1-1-23 -->

Explanation: A polynomial of degree n has at most n − 1 turning points, so a quintic has at most 4.

**A1016.** At x = 1 it crosses the x-axis with a stationary point of inflection (odd multiplicity 3). At x = −2 it touches the x-axis and turns (even multiplicity 2).
<!-- id: methods-1-1-24 -->

Explanation: Link the behaviour to the multiplicity of each factor.

**A1017.** V = x(30 − 2x)(20 − 2x) cm³, with domain 0 < x < 10.
<!-- id: methods-1-1-25 -->

Explanation: Length 30 − 2x, width 20 − 2x, height x. All dimensions must be positive, so x > 0 and 20 − 2x > 0, giving x < 10.

#### Factor and remainder theorems

**A1018.** C. 1
<!-- id: methods-1-2-1 -->

Explanation: By the remainder theorem, the remainder is P(2) = 8 − 8 + 6 − 5 = 1.

**A1019.** D. x + 3
<!-- id: methods-1-2-2 -->

Explanation: P(−3) = −27 + 21 + 6 = 0, so x + 3 is a factor; the others give P(−1) = P(3) = P(−2) = 12.

**A1020.** A. −5
<!-- id: methods-1-2-3 -->

Explanation: P(−1) = −2 + k + 5 + 2 = k + 5 = 0, so k = −5.

**A1021.** P(2) = 8 − 12 − 12 + 16 = 0, so x − 2 is a factor. P(x) = (x − 2)(x² − x − 8), with roots x = 2 and x = (1 ± √33)/2.
<!-- id: methods-1-2-4 -->

Explanation: Use division or equating coefficients for the quadratic factor, then the quadratic formula: x = (1 ± √(1 + 32))/2.

**A1022.** B. 3
<!-- id: methods-1-2-5 -->

Explanation: The remainder is P(1/2) = 4(1/8) + 1/2 + 2 = 3.

**A1023.** C. (x + 1)(x − 2)(x + 3)
<!-- id: methods-1-2-6 -->

Explanation: P(−1) = −1 + 2 + 5 − 6 = 0, so (x + 1) is a factor; dividing gives x² + x − 6 = (x + 3)(x − 2).

**A1024.** D. a = −4, b = 3
<!-- id: methods-1-2-7 -->

Explanation: P(1) = 1 + a + b = 0 and P(−1) = −1 − a + b = 6. Adding gives 2b = 6, so b = 3 and a = −4.

**A1025.** x = −2, −1/2, 3
<!-- id: methods-1-2-8 -->

Explanation: P(3) = 54 − 9 − 39 − 6 = 0, so (x − 3) is a factor. Dividing gives 2x² + 5x + 2 = (2x + 1)(x + 2).

**A1026.** A. x² + 2x + 4
<!-- id: methods-1-2-9 -->

Explanation: Difference of cubes: x³ − 8 = (x − 2)(x² + 2x + 4).

**A1027.** B. 2x³ + 3x² − x + 5 = (x + 1)(2x² + x − 2) + 7
<!-- id: methods-1-2-10 -->

Explanation: Synthetic division by −1 gives coefficients 2, 1, −2 and remainder 7; check P(−1) = −2 + 3 + 1 + 5 = 7.

**A1028.** C. 1
<!-- id: methods-1-2-11 -->

Explanation: Grouping: x²(x − 1) + (x − 1) = (x − 1)(x² + 1); x² + 1 = 0 has no real solutions, so only x = 1.

**A1029.** a = 6, b = −13
<!-- id: methods-1-2-12 -->

Explanation: P(1) = 7 + a + b = 0 gives a + b = −7; P(2) = 14 + 4a + 2b = 12 gives 2a + b = −1. Subtracting, a = 6, b = −13.

**A1030.** D. 1/3
<!-- id: methods-1-2-13 -->

Explanation: Rational roots p/q must have p dividing 6 and q dividing 2; a denominator of 3 is impossible.

**A1031.** A. (x − a) is a factor of P(x)
<!-- id: methods-1-2-14 -->

Explanation: This is the factor theorem: P(a) = 0 if and only if (x − a) is a factor.

**A1032.** B. 3
<!-- id: methods-1-2-15 -->

Explanation: P(2) = 8 − 4a + 4 = 0, so 4a = 12 and a = 3.

**A1033.** 55
<!-- id: methods-1-2-16 -->

Explanation: Remainder = P(−2) = 3(16) − 2(−8) + (−2) − 7 = 48 + 16 − 2 − 7 = 55.

**A1034.** C. {−1, 2, 3}
<!-- id: methods-1-2-17 -->

Explanation: Dividing gives (x − 3)(x² − x − 2) = (x − 3)(x − 2)(x + 1).

**A1035.** D. 0
<!-- id: methods-1-2-18 -->

Explanation: P(−1) = (−1)¹⁰⁰ − 1 = 1 − 1 = 0.

**A1036.** A. 5
<!-- id: methods-1-2-19 -->

Explanation: By the remainder theorem, the remainder on division by x − 3 equals P(3).

**A1037.** Quotient x² + 5x + 2, remainder 3, i.e. x³ + 4x² − 3x + 1 = (x − 1)(x² + 5x + 2) + 3.
<!-- id: methods-1-2-20 -->

Explanation: Check the remainder with the remainder theorem: P(1) = 1 + 4 − 3 + 1 = 3.

**A1038.** B. (x + 3)(x² − 3x + 9)
<!-- id: methods-1-2-21 -->

Explanation: Sum of cubes: a³ + b³ = (a + b)(a² − ab + b²) with a = x, b = 3.

**A1039.** C. (x − 3)(x − 2)(x + 2)
<!-- id: methods-1-2-22 -->

Explanation: Grouping: x²(x − 3) − 4(x − 3) = (x − 3)(x² − 4) = (x − 3)(x − 2)(x + 2).

**A1040.** D. x + 1
<!-- id: methods-1-2-23 -->

Explanation: P(−1) = −1 + k − k + 1 = 0 for all k, so x + 1 is always a factor.

**A1041.** m = −2, n = −1
<!-- id: methods-1-2-24 -->

Explanation: Both x − 1 and x + 1 must be factors: P(1) = 3 + m + n = 0 and P(−1) = 1 + m − n = 0, so m = −2, n = −1. Check: x³ − 2x² − x + 2 = (x − 2)(x² − 1).

**A1042.** The only possible rational roots are ±1 (rational root theorem). P(1) = 3 and P(−1) = −1, neither is zero, so there are no rational roots.
<!-- id: methods-1-2-25 -->

Explanation: Students must identify the candidate roots and test each one.

**A1043.** Finding unknown coefficients
<!-- id: methods-1-2-m1 -->
*Multi-part question · 5 marks*

**a.** The two conditions give \(a + b = -3\) and \(4a + b = 0\), and solving these simultaneously gives \(a = 1\) and \(b = -4\). *(2 marks)*

1. Form one equation from each condition. Remainder theorem: \(p(1) = 1 + a - 4 + b = -6\), so \(a + b = -3\). Factor theorem: \(p(-2) = -8 + 4a + 8 + b = 0\), so \(4a + b = 0\). *(1 mark)*
2. Subtract the first equation from the second: \(3a = 3\), so \(a = 1\). Then \(b = -3 - a = -4\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Substituting the wrong value: \(p(-1)\) for the divisor \(x - 1\), or \(p(2)\) for the factor \(x + 2\). Substitute the value of \(x\) that makes the divisor zero.

Topic: Factor and remainder theorems (methods-1-2)

**b.** \(p(x) = (x + 2)(x + 1)(x - 2)\) *(2 marks)*

1. With \(a = 1\) and \(b = -4\), \(p(x) = x^3 + x^2 - 4x - 4\). Since \(x + 2\) is a factor, divide (or equate coefficients) to get \(p(x) = (x + 2)(x^2 - x - 2)\). *(1 mark)*
2. Factorise the quadratic: \(x^2 - x - 2 = (x - 2)(x + 1)\), so \(p(x) = (x + 2)(x + 1)(x - 2)\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Stopping at \((x + 2)(x^2 - x - 2)\). The question asks for linear factors, so the quadratic must be factorised too.

Topic: Factor and remainder theorems (methods-1-2)

**c.** \(x \in (-\infty, -2] \cup [-1, 2]\) *(1 mark)*

1. The zeros of \(p\) are \(x = -2, -1, 2\). The leading coefficient is positive, so \(p(x) < 0\) for \(x < -2\), \(p(x) > 0\) on \((-2, -1)\), \(p(x) < 0\) on \((-1, 2)\) and \(p(x) > 0\) for \(x > 2\).
2. The inequality includes \(p(x) = 0\), so the zeros are included: \(x \in (-\infty, -2] \cup [-1, 2]\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using round brackets at \(-2\), \(-1\) and \(2\). Those values make \(p(x) = 0\), so they satisfy \(p(x) \le 0\) and belong in the answer.

Topic: Polynomial graphs and key features (methods-1-1)

#### Dilations, reflections and translations

**A1044.** A. y = (x − 3)² + 2
<!-- id: methods-1-3-1 -->

Explanation: A translation of h units right replaces x with x − h; a translation of k units up adds k.

**A1045.** B. y = f(x/2)
<!-- id: methods-1-3-2 -->

Explanation: A dilation by factor a from the y-axis stretches horizontally, replacing x with x/a.

**A1046.** C. y = 3√x
<!-- id: methods-1-3-3 -->

Explanation: A dilation by factor 3 from the x-axis multiplies every y-value by 3.

**A1047.** Reflection in the y-axis, then a translation 2 units right, then a dilation by a factor of 3 from the x-axis.
<!-- id: methods-1-3-4 -->

Explanation: Check: √x → √(−x) → √(−(x − 2)) = √(2 − x) → 3√(2 − x). Other correct orders are acceptable if the rule is reproduced.

**A1048.** D. y = e^(−x)
<!-- id: methods-1-3-5 -->

Explanation: Reflecting in the y-axis replaces x with −x.

**A1049.** A. y = −1/(x + 2)
<!-- id: methods-1-3-6 -->

Explanation: Reflecting gives y = −1/x; translating 2 left replaces x with x + 2.

**A1050.** B. y = 8x³
<!-- id: methods-1-3-7 -->

Explanation: Replace x with x/(½) = 2x: y = (2x)³ = 8x³.

**A1051.** y = ((x + 1)/2)³ − 4, i.e. y = (x + 1)³/8 − 4
<!-- id: methods-1-3-8 -->

Explanation: Dilation: y = (x/2)³. Translation: replace x with x + 1 and subtract 4.

**A1052.** C. y = 2x² + 2
<!-- id: methods-1-3-9 -->

Explanation: After translating, y = x² + 1; the dilation then doubles all y-values: y = 2(x² + 1) = 2x² + 2.

**A1053.** D. A dilation by factor 2 from the x-axis, a reflection in the x-axis, then a translation 1 unit left and 3 units up
<!-- id: methods-1-3-10 -->

Explanation: The coefficient −2 gives the dilation from the x-axis and the reflection in the x-axis; (x + 1) means 1 unit left; + 3 means 3 units up.

**A1054.** A. (3, 2)
<!-- id: methods-1-3-11 -->

Explanation: y = f(x − 1) − 3 is a translation 1 unit right and 3 units down, so (2, 5) → (3, 2).

**A1055.** y = 6 − 2x²
<!-- id: methods-1-3-12 -->

Explanation: y = −x² → y = −x² + 3 → y = 2(3 − x²) = 6 − 2x². The dilation applies to the whole expression after the translation.

**A1056.** B. (2, 0)
<!-- id: methods-1-3-13 -->

Explanation: y = f(2x) is a dilation by factor ½ from the y-axis, so x-coordinates are halved.

**A1057.** C. (−∞, −1]
<!-- id: methods-1-3-14 -->

Explanation: f(−x) = √(−x − 1) requires −x − 1 ≥ 0, i.e. x ≤ −1. Reflecting in the y-axis maps the domain [1, ∞) to (−∞, −1].

**A1058.** D. [−11, 1]
<!-- id: methods-1-3-15 -->

Explanation: −3f(x) takes values in [−12, 0]; adding 1 gives [−11, 1].

**A1059.** (−4, 7) and (−3, 5)
<!-- id: methods-1-3-16 -->

Explanation: Each point (x, y) maps to (x − 3, 2y − 1): (−1, 4) → (−4, 7) and (0, 3) → (−3, 5).

**A1060.** A. x = 3 and y = 1
<!-- id: methods-1-3-17 -->

Explanation: y = 1/x has asymptotes x = 0 and y = 0; translating 3 right and 1 up moves them to x = 3 and y = 1. The dilation does not change them.

**A1061.** B. x = −2
<!-- id: methods-1-3-18 -->

Explanation: y = log_e(x) has asymptote x = 0; translating 2 units left moves it to x = −2.

**A1062.** C. A dilation by a factor of ½ from the y-axis
<!-- id: methods-1-3-19 -->

Explanation: Replacing x with 2x = x/(½) is a dilation by factor ½ from the y-axis (the period halves to π).

**A1063.** Since (2x)² = 4x², it is either a dilation by factor ½ from the y-axis or a dilation by factor 4 from the x-axis.
<!-- id: methods-1-3-20 -->

Explanation: For power functions, a horizontal dilation can be rewritten as an equivalent vertical one.

**A1064.** D. 1/9
<!-- id: methods-1-3-21 -->

Explanation: The horizontal dilation gives y = (x/3)² = (1/9)x², which is a dilation by factor 1/9 from the x-axis.

**A1065.** A. (−2, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-1-3-22 -->

Explanation: 1/(x − 1)² takes all values in (0, ∞); subtracting 2 gives (−2, ∞). The asymptote y = −2 is never reached.

**A1066.** B. (−∞, 4)
<!-- id: methods-1-3-23 -->

Explanation: The image is y = 4 − e^x. Since e^x > 0, y < 4.

**A1067.** Dilation by a factor of 3 from the x-axis, then a translation 2 units right and 1 unit up. Asymptotes: x = 2 and y = 1.
<!-- id: methods-1-3-24 -->

Explanation: The dilation does not move the asymptotes; the translation moves x = 0 to x = 2 and y = 0 to y = 1.

**A1068.** y = −log_e(x/3), with domain (0, ∞).
<!-- id: methods-1-3-25 -->

Explanation: Dilation: y = log_e(x/3); reflection in the x-axis multiplies by −1. Equivalently y = log_e(3/x). The domain is unchanged because neither transformation affects x-values' sign.

#### Transformation mapping notation

**A1069.** C. y = 3(x − 2)²
<!-- id: methods-1-4-1 -->

Explanation: Let x' = x + 2 and y' = 3y, so x = x' − 2 and y = y'/3. Substituting: y'/3 = (x' − 2)², so y = 3(x − 2)².

**A1070.** D. (6, 3)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-2 -->

Explanation: Apply the rule directly: (2 × 3, 4 − 1) = (6, 3).

**A1071.** A. (x, y) → (x, −y)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-3 -->

Explanation: Reflecting in the x-axis keeps x the same and negates y.

**A1072.** y = 12(x + 1)² + 2
<!-- id: methods-1-4-4 -->

Explanation: x' = x/2 − 1 gives x = 2(x' + 1); y' = 3y + 2 gives y = (y' − 2)/3. Then (y' − 2)/3 = 4(x' + 1)², so y = 12(x + 1)² + 2.

**A1073.** B. A dilation by a factor of ½ from the y-axis
<!-- id: methods-1-4-5 -->

Explanation: Multiplying x-coordinates by ½ halves distances from the y-axis.

**A1074.** C. y = 2√(−x) + 1
<!-- id: methods-1-4-6 -->

Explanation: x = −x' and y = (y' − 1)/2, so (y' − 1)/2 = √(−x'), giving y = 2√(−x) + 1.

**A1075.** D. T(x, y) = (x + 1, y + 5)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-7 -->

Explanation: The image is the parabola translated 1 unit right and 5 units up, so add 1 to x and 5 to y.

**A1076.** T(x, y) = (x − 3, −y + 1)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-8 -->

Explanation: Reflect in the x-axis, then translate 3 left and 1 up. Check: x = x' + 3 and y = 1 − y', so 1 − y' = √(x' + 3), giving y' = 1 − √(x' + 3).

**A1077.** A. (2, 6)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-9 -->

Explanation: Solve 3x − 1 = 5, giving x = 2, and −y + 2 = −4, giving y = 6. (14, 6) is the image of (5, −4), not its pre-image.

**A1078.** B. T(x, y) = (2x, −y + 3)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-10 -->

Explanation: Multiplying out gives x' = 2x and y' = −y, then adding the translation vector gives y' = −y + 3.

**A1079.** C. y = 2e^(x + 3)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-11 -->

Explanation: x = x' + 3 and y = y'/2, so y'/2 = e^(x' + 3), giving y = 2e^(x + 3).

**A1080.** T(x, y) = (x + 1, (y + 3)/2)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-12 -->

Explanation: The forward mapping from y = x² is (x, y) → (x − 1, 2y − 3); its inverse is (x, y) → (x + 1, (y + 3)/2). Check: y' = (y + 3)/2 = (x + 1)² = x'².

**A1081.** D. T(x, y) = (x/2, y)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-13 -->

Explanation: With x' = x/2, x = 2x', so y' = (2x')³ = 8x'³.

**A1082.** A. y = 6x + 3
<!-- id: methods-1-4-14 -->

Explanation: y = y'/3 and x = x', so y'/3 = 2x' + 1, giving y = 6x + 3.

**A1083.** B. (−∞, 0)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-15 -->

Explanation: The original domain x > 0 maps to x' = −x < 0. The vertical translation does not affect the domain.

**A1084.** T(x, y) = (2x − 3, −y); matrix form [x'; y'] = [[2, 0], [0, −1]][x; y] + [−3; 0].
<!-- id: methods-1-4-16 -->

Explanation: The diagonal entries give the dilation and reflection; the translation vector gives the shift.

**A1085.** C. (−1, −1)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-17 -->

Explanation: Apply the mapping: (2(−1) + 1, 2 − 3) = (−1, −1).

**A1086.** D. T⁻¹(x, y) = ((x − 4)/2, y + 1)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-18 -->

Explanation: Undo each step in reverse order: subtract 4 then halve for x, and add 1 for y.

**A1087.** A. y = −2/x
<!-- id: methods-1-4-19 -->

Explanation: y = −y'/2 and x = x', so −y'/2 = 1/x', giving y = −2/x.

**A1088.** y = e^(x/2) + 1, horizontal asymptote y = 1.
<!-- id: methods-1-4-20 -->

Explanation: x = x'/2 and y = y' − 1, so y' − 1 = e^(x'/2). The asymptote y = 0 moves up 1 unit.

**A1089.** B. (x, y) → (x − 5, y − 2)
<!-- id: methods-1-4-21 -->

Explanation: y = f(x + 5) − 2 is a translation 5 units left and 2 units down.

**A1090.** C. 6π
<!-- id: methods-1-4-22 -->

Explanation: The image is y = sin(x/3), a horizontal stretch by factor 3, so the period becomes 3 × 2π = 6π.

**A1091.** D. x = 1 and y = −3
<!-- id: methods-1-4-23 -->

Explanation: The image is y = 2/(x − 1) − 3. The asymptotes x = 0 and y = 0 map to x = 1 and y = 2(0) − 3 = −3.

**A1092.** T₁ gives y = f((x − 2)/2); T₂ gives y = f((x − 4)/2). T₁ is a dilation by factor 2 from the y-axis then a translation 2 right; T₂ is a translation 2 right then the dilation (total shift 4 right), so the images differ by 2 units horizontally.
<!-- id: methods-1-4-24 -->

Explanation: The order of dilation and translation changes the result.

**A1093.** One possible answer: a = ½, b = 2, c = −1, d = 1.
<!-- id: methods-1-4-25 -->

Explanation: With x' = x/2 + 2 and y' = −y + 1: x = 2(x' − 2) and y = 1 − y', so 1 − y' = 8(x' − 2)³, giving y' = 1 − (2x' − 4)³. Other answers exist, e.g. a = 1, b = 2, c = −8, d = 1.

### Exponential, log & circular functions

#### Exponential and logarithmic graphs

**A1094.** B. (−5, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-2-1-1 -->

Explanation: e^(3x) > 0 for all x, so 2e^(3x) > 0 and f(x) > −5; the asymptote y = −5 is never reached.

**A1095.** A. y = 3
<!-- id: methods-2-1-2 -->

Explanation: As x → ∞, e^(−x) → 0, so y → 3.

**A1096.** A. (3, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-2-1-3 -->

Explanation: The argument must be positive: 2x − 6 > 0 gives x > 3.

**A1097.** f⁻¹(x) = 2log_e((x + 1)/3), with domain (−1, ∞).
<!-- id: methods-2-1-4 -->

Explanation: Swap x and y: (x + 1)/3 = e^(y/2), so y = 2log_e((x + 1)/3). The domain of f⁻¹ is the range of f, which is (−1, ∞).

**A1098.** B. x = −4
<!-- id: methods-2-1-5 -->

Explanation: The argument x + 4 approaches 0 as x → −4, so the asymptote is x = −4.

**A1099.** B. f⁻¹(x) = ½log_e(x − 1), x > 1
<!-- id: methods-2-1-6 -->

Explanation: Swap x and y: x = e^(2y) + 1, so e^(2y) = x − 1 and y = ½log_e(x − 1). The domain of f⁻¹ is the range of f, (1, ∞).

**A1100.** Asymptote y = 2; y-intercept (0, 1); x-intercept (log_e(2), 0); range (−∞, 2).
<!-- id: methods-2-1-7 -->

Explanation: At x = 0, y = 2 − 1 = 1. Setting y = 0 gives e^x = 2. Since e^x > 0, y < 2.

**A1101.** D. f⁻¹(x) = e^(x − 3) + 2
<!-- id: methods-2-1-8 -->

Explanation: Swap x and y: x − 3 = log_e(y − 2), so y = e^(x − 3) + 2, with domain R.

**A1102.** A. (0, −2)
<!-- id: methods-2-1-9 -->

Explanation: At x = 0, y = 3e⁰ − 5 = 3 − 5 = −2.

**A1103.** C. x = e^(−2)
<!-- id: methods-2-1-10 -->

Explanation: log_e(x) = −2 gives x = e^(−2).

**A1104.** x-intercept x = 1 + e^(−1); vertical asymptote x = 1; there is no y-intercept because x = 0 is not in the domain.
<!-- id: methods-2-1-11 -->

Explanation: log_e(x − 1) = −1 gives x − 1 = e^(−1). The domain requires x > 1.

**A1105.** C. x = log_e(3)
<!-- id: methods-2-1-12 -->

Explanation: 2e^x = 6 gives e^x = 3, so x = log_e(3).

**A1106.** D. y = e^(−x) + 2
<!-- id: methods-2-1-13 -->

Explanation: Reflection in the y-axis replaces x with −x; translating up adds 2.

**A1107.** t = log_e(2)/0.15 ≈ 4.62 years.
<!-- id: methods-2-1-14 -->

Explanation: e^(−0.15t) = ½ gives 0.15t = log_e(2) ≈ 0.6931, so t ≈ 4.62. The answer does not depend on the initial value.

**A1108.** B. y = log_e(x/3)
<!-- id: methods-2-1-15 -->

Explanation: A dilation by factor 3 from the y-axis replaces x with x/3.

**A1109.** D. [0, e² − 1]
<!-- id: methods-2-1-16 -->

Explanation: f is increasing, f(0) = 0 and f(2) = e² − 1, so the range is [0, e² − 1].

**A1110.** C. 5log_e(4) hours
<!-- id: methods-2-1-17 -->

Explanation: e^(0.2t) = 4, so 0.2t = log_e(4) and t = 5log_e(4) ≈ 6.93 hours.

**A1111.** A dilation by a factor of 2 from the x-axis, then a translation of 1 unit in the positive x-direction and 3 units in the positive y-direction.
<!-- id: methods-2-1-18 -->

Explanation: Replacing x by x − 1 is a shift right by 1; multiplying by 2 dilates from the x-axis; adding 3 shifts up. The dilation must happen before the vertical translation.

**A1112.** C. 20°C
<!-- id: methods-2-1-19 -->

Explanation: As t → ∞, e^(−0.05t) → 0, so T → 20°C (room temperature).

**A1113.** B. log_e(2)/10
<!-- id: methods-2-1-20 -->

Explanation: e^(−10k) = ½ gives −10k = −log_e(2), so k = log_e(2)/10.

**A1114.** a = 6, b = 4.
<!-- id: methods-2-1-21 -->

Explanation: At x = 1, log_e(1) = 0 so b = 4. At x = e, a + 4 = 10 so a = 6.

**A1115.** A. a = 3, b = 2
<!-- id: methods-2-1-22 -->

Explanation: At x = 0, y = a = 3. Then 3e^b = 3e² gives b = 2.

**A1116.** A. y = x
<!-- id: methods-2-1-23 -->

Explanation: The functions are inverses of each other, and inverse graphs are reflections in y = x.

**A1117.** D. (−2, 2)
<!-- id: methods-2-1-24 -->

Explanation: We need 4 − x² > 0, i.e. x² < 4, so −2 < x < 2.

**A1118.** [−1, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-2-1-25 -->

Explanation: h is decreasing. h(e²) = 1 − 2 = −1, and h(x) → ∞ as x → 0⁺.

#### Log laws and exponential equations

**A1119.** D. 5
<!-- id: methods-2-2-1 -->

Explanation: 2⁵ = 32, so log_2(32) = 5.

**A1120.** A. log_e(4)
<!-- id: methods-2-2-2 -->

Explanation: Using log_e(a) − log_e(b) = log_e(a/b), we get log_e(8/2) = log_e(4).

**A1121.** A. 2
<!-- id: methods-2-2-3 -->

Explanation: 2log_3(6) = log_3(36), and log_3(36/4) = log_3(9) = 2.

**A1122.** x = (1/3)log_e(7)
<!-- id: methods-2-2-4 -->

Explanation: 2e^(3x) = 14 gives e^(3x) = 7, so 3x = log_e(7).

**A1123.** B. x = ½log_e(7)
<!-- id: methods-2-2-5 -->

Explanation: Take natural logs: 2x = log_e(7), so x = ½log_e(7).

**A1124.** C. x = log_e(10)/log_e(3)
<!-- id: methods-2-2-6 -->

Explanation: Taking natural logs: x log_e(3) = log_e(10), so x = log_e(10)/log_e(3) ≈ 2.096.

**A1125.** x = 2
<!-- id: methods-2-2-7 -->

Explanation: log_2(x(x + 2)) = 3 gives x² + 2x = 8, so (x + 4)(x − 2) = 0. Reject x = −4 because it is outside the domain.

**A1126.** B. x = 13
<!-- id: methods-2-2-8 -->

Explanation: x + 3 = 2⁴ = 16, so x = 13.

**A1127.** B. x = 5 only
<!-- id: methods-2-2-9 -->

Explanation: x(x − 3) = 10 gives x² − 3x − 10 = 0, so (x − 5)(x + 2) = 0. x = −2 is rejected because log_e(−2) is undefined.

**A1128.** D. x = log_e(2) or x = log_e(3)
<!-- id: methods-2-2-10 -->

Explanation: Let u = e^x: u² − 5u + 6 = 0 gives u = 2 or u = 3, so x = log_e(2) or x = log_e(3).

**A1129.** x = log_e(3)
<!-- id: methods-2-2-11 -->

Explanation: Multiply by e^x: e^(2x) − e^x − 6 = 0, so (e^x − 3)(e^x + 2) = 0. Since e^x > 0, e^x = 3.

**A1130.** B. x = log_e(4) only
<!-- id: methods-2-2-12 -->

Explanation: (e^x − 4)(e^x + 3) = 0. Since e^x > 0, only e^x = 4 is valid, so x = log_e(4).

**A1131.** C. x³
<!-- id: methods-2-2-13 -->

Explanation: 3log_e(x) = log_e(x³), and e^(log_e(x³)) = x³.

**A1132.** 5
<!-- id: methods-2-2-14 -->

Explanation: 2 + 0 − 3(−1) = 5.

**A1133.** C. ½
<!-- id: methods-2-2-15 -->

Explanation: √e = e^(1/2), so log_e(e^(1/2)) = ½.

**A1134.** D. 2p + q
<!-- id: methods-2-2-16 -->

Explanation: 12 = 2² × 3, so log_a(12) = 2log_a(2) + log_a(3) = 2p + q.

**A1135.** A. log_e(xz/y²)
<!-- id: methods-2-2-17 -->

Explanation: 2log_e(y) = log_e(y²). Adding logs multiplies and subtracting divides, giving log_e(xz/y²).

**A1136.** x = log_e(3)/(2log_e(5) − log_e(3)) = log_e(3)/log_e(25/3) ≈ 0.518
<!-- id: methods-2-2-18 -->

Explanation: 2x log_e(5) = (x + 1)log_e(3), so x(2log_e(5) − log_e(3)) = log_e(3). Equivalent exact forms are acceptable.

**A1137.** A. x = log_e(2)/log_e(2.5)
<!-- id: methods-2-2-19 -->

Explanation: (x + 1)log_e(2) = x log_e(5), so x(log_e(5) − log_e(2)) = log_e(2) and x = log_e(2)/log_e(2.5) ≈ 0.756.

**A1138.** A. x = (e³ + 1)/2
<!-- id: methods-2-2-20 -->

Explanation: 2x − 1 = e³, so x = (e³ + 1)/2.

**A1139.** x = ½
<!-- id: methods-2-2-21 -->

Explanation: log_e((x + 1)/x) = log_e(3) gives x + 1 = 3x, so x = ½. This is valid because x > 0.

**A1140.** D. x = 2
<!-- id: methods-2-2-22 -->

Explanation: Let u = 2^x: u² − 3u − 4 = 0 gives (u − 4)(u + 1) = 0. Since 2^x > 0, 2^x = 4 and x = 2.

**A1141.** B. x = 1/9
<!-- id: methods-2-2-23 -->

Explanation: x = 3^(−2) = 1/9.

**A1142.** C. −2log_e(x)
<!-- id: methods-2-2-24 -->

Explanation: log_e(x^(−2)) = −2log_e(x).

**A1143.** t = (100/3)log_e(4/3) ≈ 9.59 years
<!-- id: methods-2-2-25 -->

Explanation: 1200/900 = e^(0.03t), so 0.03t = log_e(4/3) ≈ 0.2877 and t ≈ 9.59.

#### Circular function graphs and transformations

**A1144.** A. π
<!-- id: methods-2-3-1 -->

Explanation: Period = 2π/n = 2π/2 = π.

**A1145.** D. 4
<!-- id: methods-2-3-2 -->

Explanation: Amplitude is |a| = |−4| = 4. The negative sign is a reflection, not part of the amplitude.

**A1146.** C. π/3
<!-- id: methods-2-3-3 -->

Explanation: The period of tan(nx) is π/n = π/3.

**A1147.** Amplitude 2, period 2π/3, range [−1, 3].
<!-- id: methods-2-3-4 -->

Explanation: Amplitude = |−2| = 2 and period = 2π/3. The range is 1 ± 2, giving [−1, 3].

**A1148.** B. [−5, −1]
<!-- id: methods-2-3-5 -->

Explanation: 2sin(x) ranges over [−2, 2]; subtracting 3 gives [−5, −1].

**A1149.** C. [2, 8]
<!-- id: methods-2-3-6 -->

Explanation: −3cos(2x) ranges over [−3, 3]; adding 5 gives [2, 8].

**A1150.** a = 4, n = π/2, c = 6, so y = 4sin(πx/2) + 6.
<!-- id: methods-2-3-7 -->

Explanation: a = (10 − 2)/2 = 4, c = (10 + 2)/2 = 6, and 2π/n = 4 gives n = π/2.

**A1151.** A. 8
<!-- id: methods-2-3-8 -->

Explanation: Period = 2π/(π/4) = 8.

**A1152.** B. x = π/4 + kπ/2, k ∈ Z
<!-- id: methods-2-3-9 -->

Explanation: Asymptotes occur where 2x = π/2 + kπ, so x = π/4 + kπ/2.

**A1153.** B. x = 3π/4
<!-- id: methods-2-3-10 -->

Explanation: The maximum occurs when x − π/4 = π/2, so x = 3π/4.

**A1154.** x = 7π/12 and x = 11π/12
<!-- id: methods-2-3-11 -->

Explanation: sin(2x) = −½ with 2x ∈ [0, 2π] gives 2x = 7π/6 or 11π/6.

**A1155.** C. 12 hours
<!-- id: methods-2-3-12 -->

Explanation: Period = 2π/(π/6) = 12 hours.

**A1156.** A. 2.5 m
<!-- id: methods-2-3-13 -->

Explanation: The minimum is when cos = −1: 4 − 1.5 = 2.5 m.

**A1157.** x = −π and x = π
<!-- id: methods-2-3-14 -->

Explanation: x/2 = π/2 + kπ gives x = π + 2kπ. Only k = −1 and k = 0 give values in [−2π, 2π].

**A1158.** B. y = 2sin(2x)
<!-- id: methods-2-3-15 -->

Explanation: y = 2sin(2x) has amplitude 2 and period 2π/2 = π.

**A1159.** D. y = sin(x − π/3)
<!-- id: methods-2-3-16 -->

Explanation: A translation of π/3 to the right replaces x with x − π/3.

**A1160.** A. 4
<!-- id: methods-2-3-17 -->

Explanation: The period is π, so [0, 2π] contains two full cycles, with 2 solutions in each: 4 solutions.

**A1161.** A dilation by factor 3 from the x-axis, a dilation by factor ½ from the y-axis, then a translation of π/4 units in the negative x-direction and 1 unit in the negative y-direction.
<!-- id: methods-2-3-18 -->

Explanation: Do the dilations before the translations. Note that the horizontal translation is π/4, not π/2, because the 2 has been factored out.

**A1162.** C. a = 3, c = 4
<!-- id: methods-2-3-19 -->

Explanation: a = (7 − 1)/2 = 3 and c = (7 + 1)/2 = 4.

**A1163.** D. y = sin(x)
<!-- id: methods-2-3-20 -->

Explanation: cos(x − π/2) = sin(x): shifting cosine π/2 to the right gives the sine curve.

**A1164.** The maximum is 24°C, at t = 12 (noon).
<!-- id: methods-2-3-21 -->

Explanation: The maximum occurs when cos(πt/12) = −1, i.e. πt/12 = π, so t = 12. Then T = 18 + 6 = 24.

**A1165.** D. 2π
<!-- id: methods-2-3-22 -->

Explanation: The period of tan(nx) is π/n = π/(1/2) = 2π. The 3 is a dilation and does not change the period.

**A1166.** A. 38 m
<!-- id: methods-2-3-23 -->

Explanation: The maximum is when cos = −1: 20 + 18 = 38 m.

**A1167.** B. 20 minutes
<!-- id: methods-2-3-24 -->

Explanation: Period = 2π/(π/10) = 20 minutes.

**A1168.** t = 20/3 ≈ 6.67 minutes
<!-- id: methods-2-3-25 -->

Explanation: −18cos(πt/10) = 9 gives cos(πt/10) = −½. The first solution is πt/10 = 2π/3, so t = 20/3.

#### Exact values and general solutions

**A1169.** B. √3/2
<!-- id: methods-2-4-1 -->

Explanation: From the 30-60-90 triangle, sin(60°) = √3/2.

**A1170.** A. −√3/2
<!-- id: methods-2-4-2 -->

Explanation: 5π/6 is in the second quadrant with reference angle π/6, and cosine is negative there.

**A1171.** C. −1
<!-- id: methods-2-4-3 -->

Explanation: 3π/4 is in the second quadrant with reference angle π/4; tangent is negative there.

**A1172.** x = π/12, 5π/12, 13π/12, 17π/12
<!-- id: methods-2-4-4 -->

Explanation: sin(2x) = ½ with 2x ∈ [0, 4π] gives 2x = π/6, 5π/6, 13π/6, 17π/6. Remember to double the domain.

**A1173.** B. −1/2
<!-- id: methods-2-4-5 -->

Explanation: 7π/6 is in the third quadrant with reference angle π/6; sine is negative there.

**A1174.** A. √2/2
<!-- id: methods-2-4-6 -->

Explanation: Cosine is even, so cos(−π/4) = cos(π/4) = √2/2.

**A1175.** x = 2kπ/3 ± 2π/9, k ∈ Z
<!-- id: methods-2-4-7 -->

Explanation: 3x = 2kπ ± 2π/3. Divide by 3.

**A1176.** A. −√3
<!-- id: methods-2-4-8 -->

Explanation: Tangent is odd, so tan(−π/3) = −tan(π/3) = −√3.

**A1177.** C. −0.6
<!-- id: methods-2-4-9 -->

Explanation: sin(π + θ) = −sin(θ) = −0.6.

**A1178.** A. 0.3
<!-- id: methods-2-4-10 -->

Explanation: cos(2π − θ) = cos(−θ) = cos(θ) = 0.3.

**A1179.** x = π/3, 2π/3, 4π/3, 5π/3
<!-- id: methods-2-4-11 -->

Explanation: sin(x) = ±√3/2, which gives a reference angle of π/3 in all four quadrants.

**A1180.** D. −3/5
<!-- id: methods-2-4-12 -->

Explanation: cos²(x) = 1 − 16/25 = 9/25, and cosine is negative in the second quadrant.

**A1181.** C. x = π/6, 5π/6
<!-- id: methods-2-4-13 -->

Explanation: Sine is positive in quadrants 1 and 2: x = π/6 and π − π/6 = 5π/6.

**A1182.** (1 − √2)/2
<!-- id: methods-2-4-14 -->

Explanation: sin(5π/4) = −√2/2 (third quadrant) and cos(5π/3) = ½ (fourth quadrant).

**A1183.** B. x = 2π/3, 4π/3
<!-- id: methods-2-4-15 -->

Explanation: The reference angle is π/3, and cosine is negative in quadrants 2 and 3: π − π/3 and π + π/3.

**A1184.** D. x = π/3, 4π/3
<!-- id: methods-2-4-16 -->

Explanation: Tangent is positive in quadrants 1 and 3: π/3 and π + π/3 = 4π/3.

**A1185.** C. x = kπ, k ∈ Z
<!-- id: methods-2-4-17 -->

Explanation: sin(x) = 0 at every integer multiple of π.

**A1186.** x = 3π/8, 7π/8
<!-- id: methods-2-4-18 -->

Explanation: With 2x ∈ [0, 2π], tan(2x) = −1 gives 2x = 3π/4 or 7π/4.

**A1187.** A. x = 2kπ ± π/6, k ∈ Z
<!-- id: methods-2-4-19 -->

Explanation: The general solution of cos(x) = a is x = 2kπ ± cos⁻¹(a), and cos⁻¹(√3/2) = π/6.

**A1188.** B. x = π/4 + kπ, k ∈ Z
<!-- id: methods-2-4-20 -->

Explanation: Tangent has period π, so x = tan⁻¹(1) + kπ = π/4 + kπ.

**A1189.** sin(θ) = −12/13, tan(θ) = 12/5
<!-- id: methods-2-4-21 -->

Explanation: sin²(θ) = 1 − 25/169 = 144/169. In the third quadrant sine is negative and tangent is positive.

**A1190.** B. x = π/6 + kπ or x = π/3 + kπ, k ∈ Z
<!-- id: methods-2-4-22 -->

Explanation: 2x = π/3 + 2kπ or 2π/3 + 2kπ. Divide by 2: x = π/6 + kπ or π/3 + kπ.

**A1191.** D. x = 3π/4, 5π/4
<!-- id: methods-2-4-23 -->

Explanation: cos(x) = −√2/2. The reference angle is π/4, in quadrants 2 and 3.

**A1192.** D. x = π/4, 5π/4
<!-- id: methods-2-4-24 -->

Explanation: Divide by cos(x) to get tan(x) = 1, so x = π/4 or π + π/4 = 5π/4.

**A1193.** x = π/6 + kπ, k ∈ Z
<!-- id: methods-2-4-25 -->

Explanation: Divide by √3 cos(x) to get tan(x) = 1/√3, so x = π/6 + kπ.

### Algebra of functions

#### Domain, range and composite functions

**A1194.** A. x ≤ 2
<!-- id: methods-3-1-1 -->

Explanation: Require 4 − 2x ≥ 0, so x ≤ 2 (x = 2 is included since √0 = 0 is defined).

**A1195.** B. (3, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-3-1-2 -->

Explanation: The argument of log_e must be strictly positive: x − 3 > 0 gives x > 3.

**A1196.** C. [2, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-3-1-3 -->

Explanation: Completing the square gives f(x) = (x − 3)² + 2, so the minimum value is 2, attained at x = 3.

**A1197.** Domain [−3, 3]; range [0, 3].
<!-- id: methods-3-1-4 -->

Explanation: Require 9 − x² ≥ 0 so −3 ≤ x ≤ 3. The expression under the root ranges from 0 to 9, so f ranges from 0 to 3.

**A1198.** D. (1, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-3-1-5 -->

Explanation: 2e^(−x) takes all values in (0, ∞), so adding 1 gives (1, ∞); the asymptote y = 1 is never reached.

**A1199.** A. R \ {−3, 3}
<!-- id: methods-3-1-6 -->

Explanation: The denominator is zero when x² = 9, i.e. x = ±3, so both values must be excluded.

**A1200.** B. [0, 4]
<!-- id: methods-3-1-7 -->

Explanation: The domain contains 0, where the minimum value 0 occurs; the maximum is f(2) = 4.

**A1201.** f(g(x)) = x² − 1 and g(f(x)) = (x − 1)² = x² − 2x + 1. They are not equal (e.g. at x = 1 they give 0 and 0, but at x = 2 they give 3 and 1).
<!-- id: methods-3-1-8 -->

Explanation: Composition is not commutative in general; award marks for each correct rule and a valid comparison.

**A1202.** C. 4x² − 12x + 10
<!-- id: methods-3-1-9 -->

Explanation: f(g(x)) = (2x − 3)² + 1 = 4x² − 12x + 9 + 1 = 4x² − 12x + 10.

**A1203.** D. 7
<!-- id: methods-3-1-10 -->

Explanation: f(2) = 5, then g(5) = 2(5) − 3 = 7. (The value 2 is f(g(2)), the composition in the wrong order.)

**A1204.** A. [4, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-3-1-11 -->

Explanation: h(x) = √(x − 4) requires x − 4 ≥ 0, so x ≥ 4.

**A1205.** ran f = [0, ∞) ⊆ R = dom g, so g∘f is defined. g(f(x)) = √x + 2, domain [0, ∞), range [2, ∞).
<!-- id: methods-3-1-12 -->

Explanation: The domain of g∘f is the domain of f; the range follows since √x ≥ 0.

**A1206.** B. R \ {0}
<!-- id: methods-3-1-13 -->

Explanation: g∘f requires ran f ⊆ (0, ∞); x² > 0 only when x ≠ 0, so 0 must be excluded from D.

**A1207.** C. e^(2x)
<!-- id: methods-3-1-14 -->

Explanation: g(f(x)) = (e^x)² = e^(2x). The option e^(x²) is f(g(x)).

**A1208.** D. [1, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-3-1-15 -->

Explanation: g(x) = x² takes values in [0, ∞), and e^t for t ≥ 0 gives values in [1, ∞), with e^0 = 1 attained.

**A1209.** [−2, 5)
<!-- id: methods-3-1-16 -->

Explanation: Need 5 − x > 0 (x < 5) and x + 2 ≥ 0 (x ≥ −2); the domain is the intersection [−2, 5).

**A1210.** A. (−∞, 0) ∪ (4, ∞)
<!-- id: methods-3-1-17 -->

Explanation: Require x² − 4x = x(x − 4) > 0, which holds when x < 0 or x > 4.

**A1211.** B. (−∞, 3]
<!-- id: methods-3-1-18 -->

Explanation: √(x + 1) takes all values in [0, ∞), so 3 − √(x + 1) takes all values ≤ 3.

**A1212.** C. R \ {3}
