<!-- id: methods-8-1-18 -->

Explanation: E(X²) = ∫₀¹ 3x⁴ dx = 3/5, so Var(X) = 3/5 − (3/4)² = 48/80 − 45/80 = 3/80.

**A1708.** ∫₁^(e²) k/x dx = k[log_e(x)]₁^(e²) = 2k = 1, so k = 1/2.
<!-- id: methods-8-1-19 -->

Explanation: Set the total area under the pdf equal to 1.

**A1709.** P(X > 1) = (1/4)[x²/2 + x]₁² = (1/4)(4 − 1.5) = 5/8 = 0.625.
<!-- id: methods-8-1-20 -->

Explanation: Integrate the pdf from 1 to 2. Check: the total area is (1/4)(2 + 2) = 1.

**A1710.** E(X) = (1/2)∫₀^π x sin(x) dx = (1/2)[−x cos(x) + sin(x)]₀^π = (1/2)(π) = π/2.
<!-- id: methods-8-1-21 -->

Explanation: Use integration by parts, or note that the pdf is symmetric about x = π/2.

**A1711.** Solve 1 − e^(−m/2) = 1/2, so e^(−m/2) = 1/2 and m = 2 log_e(2) ≈ 1.39 years.
<!-- id: methods-8-1-22 -->

Explanation: Set ∫₀^m f(x) dx = 0.5 and solve for m.

**A1712.** f(x) ≥ 0 for all x, and ∫₋∞^∞ f(x) dx = 1, so the total area under the graph is 1.
<!-- id: methods-8-1-23 -->

Explanation: Both conditions are needed to get the mark.

**A1713.** Var(X) = E(X²) − [E(X)]², so E(X²) = 2 + 9 = 11.
<!-- id: methods-8-1-24 -->

Explanation: Rearrange the variance formula.

**A1714.** P = [x³] from 0.2 to 0.6 = 0.216 − 0.008 = 0.208.
<!-- id: methods-8-1-25 -->

Explanation: Integrate the pdf over the interval.

#### Normal distribution and standardisation

**A1715.** C. 0.0228
<!-- id: methods-8-2-1 -->

Explanation: σ = √25 = 5, so z = (80 − 70)/5 = 2 and P(Z > 2) ≈ 0.0228.

**A1716.** D. 156 cm and 188 cm
<!-- id: methods-8-2-2 -->

Explanation: About 95% of a normal distribution lies within 2 standard deviations of the mean: 172 ± 16.

**A1717.** A. 1.3
<!-- id: methods-8-2-3 -->

Explanation: z = (78 − 65)/10 = 1.3.

**A1718.** B. She did better in English (z = 2 compared with z = 1.5)
<!-- id: methods-8-2-4 -->

Explanation: Methods z = (72 − 60)/8 = 1.5 and English z = (80 − 70)/5 = 2. The higher z-score shows the better relative result.

**A1719.** C. 0.0668
<!-- id: methods-8-2-5 -->

Explanation: By symmetry P(Z < −1.5) = 1 − P(Z < 1.5) ≈ 1 − 0.9332 = 0.0668.

**A1720.** D. 0.3
<!-- id: methods-8-2-6 -->

Explanation: The points a and 100 − a are symmetric about the mean 50, so the tail areas are equal.

**A1721.** A. 7.80
<!-- id: methods-8-2-7 -->

Explanation: P(Z > 1.2816) = 0.1, so 10/σ = 1.2816 and σ ≈ 7.80.

**A1722.** B. 0.6247
<!-- id: methods-8-2-8 -->

Explanation: P(Z < 1.5) − P(Z < −0.5) ≈ 0.9332 − 0.3085 = 0.6247.

**A1723.** C. 0.8186
<!-- id: methods-8-2-9 -->

Explanation: z-values are −1 and 2, and P(−1 < Z < 2) ≈ 0.9772 − 0.1587 = 0.8186.

**A1724.** D. 266.4 g
<!-- id: methods-8-2-10 -->

Explanation: P(Z > 1.6449) = 0.05, so the mass is 250 + 1.6449 × 10 ≈ 266.4 g. 269.6 g wrongly uses 1.96.

**A1725.** A. 0 and 1
<!-- id: methods-8-2-11 -->

Explanation: Standardising shifts the mean to 0 and scales the standard deviation to 1.

**A1726.** B. 17.48
<!-- id: methods-8-2-12 -->

Explanation: z for 0.8 is 0.8416, so μ = 20 − 0.8416 × 3 ≈ 17.48.

**A1727.** C. 9.42
<!-- id: methods-8-2-13 -->

Explanation: 40 = μ − 0.8416σ and 60 = μ + 1.2816σ. Subtracting gives 20 = 2.1232σ, so σ ≈ 9.42.

**A1728.** D. mean 65 and standard deviation 12
<!-- id: methods-8-2-14 -->

Explanation: E(Y) = 3(20) + 5 = 65 and sd(Y) = |3| × 4 = 12. The added 5 does not change the spread.

**A1729.** A. 0.8664
<!-- id: methods-8-2-15 -->

Explanation: P(−1.5 < Z < 1.5) = 2(0.9332) − 1 ≈ 0.8664.

**A1730.** B. 1.96
<!-- id: methods-8-2-16 -->

Explanation: Each tail has area 0.025, and P(Z < 1.96) = 0.975.

**A1731.** C. 0.1434
<!-- id: methods-8-2-17 -->

Explanation: P(X > 130)/P(X > 115) = P(Z > 2)/P(Z > 1) ≈ 0.02275/0.15866 ≈ 0.1434.

**A1732.** D. 0.1056
<!-- id: methods-8-2-18 -->

Explanation: z = (450 − 500)/40 = −1.25, and P(Z < −1.25) ≈ 0.1056.

**A1733.** z-values are −1 and 2, so P = P(−1 < Z < 2) ≈ 0.9772 − 0.1587 = 0.8186.
<!-- id: methods-8-2-19 -->

Explanation: Standardise both endpoints first.

**A1734.** X is continuous, so P(X = a) = 0. Including or excluding the endpoint therefore does not change the probability.
<!-- id: methods-8-2-20 -->

Explanation: Refer to the probability of a single value being zero.

**A1735.** P(Z > 1.0364) = 0.15, so k = 30 + 1.0364 × 5 ≈ 35.18.
<!-- id: methods-8-2-21 -->

Explanation: Use the inverse normal with an area of 0.85 to the left.

**A1736.** 42 is 2σ below the mean and 54 is 1σ above. The estimate is 47.5% + 34% = 81.5%.
<!-- id: methods-8-2-22 -->

Explanation: Split the interval at the mean and use half of 95% and half of 68%.

**A1737.** z = (28 − 32)/5 = −0.8, so P(X ≥ 28) = P(Z ≥ −0.8) ≈ 0.7881.
<!-- id: methods-8-2-23 -->

Explanation: The answer must be greater than 0.5 because 28 is below the mean.

**A1738.** Each is legal with probability ≈ 0.7881, so P(all three) ≈ 0.7881³ ≈ 0.490.
<!-- id: methods-8-2-24 -->

Explanation: Multiply the probabilities of the independent events.

**A1739.** z for 0.05 is −1.6449, so 15 = μ − 1.6449 × 2, which gives μ ≈ 18.29.
<!-- id: methods-8-2-25 -->

Explanation: The mean must be above 15 because 15 is in the lower tail.

#### Distribution of sample proportions

**A1740.** A. 0.4
<!-- id: methods-8-3-1 -->

Explanation: The sample proportion is an unbiased estimator: E(P̂) = p = 0.4.

**A1741.** B. 0.049
<!-- id: methods-8-3-2 -->

Explanation: sd(P̂) = √(p(1 − p)/n) = √(0.24/100) ≈ 0.049.

**A1742.** C. halved
<!-- id: methods-8-3-3 -->

Explanation: sd(P̂) = √(p(1 − p)/n), so multiplying n by 4 divides the sd by √4 = 2.

**A1743.** D. 0.0206
<!-- id: methods-8-3-4 -->

Explanation: sd = √(0.24/100) ≈ 0.04899, so z = 0.1/0.04899 ≈ 2.04 and P(Z > 2.04) ≈ 0.0206.

**A1744.** A. 0.33
<!-- id: methods-8-3-5 -->

Explanation: P̂ = X/20 can only take multiples of 0.05. 0.33 is not one of these.

**A1745.** B. 0.2335
<!-- id: methods-8-3-6 -->

Explanation: P̂ = 0.2 means X = 2 where X ~ Bi(10, 0.3): C(10,2)(0.3)²(0.7)⁸ ≈ 0.2335.

**A1746.** C. a sample statistic
<!-- id: methods-8-3-7 -->

Explanation: It is calculated from the sample, so it is a statistic (p̂) that estimates the unknown parameter p.

**A1747.** D. p = 0.5, n = 400
<!-- id: methods-8-3-8 -->

Explanation: The sds are 0.025, 0.05, 0.03 and about 0.042 respectively.

**A1748.** A. 0.5
<!-- id: methods-8-3-9 -->

Explanation: p(1 − p) is a downward parabola with its maximum at p = 0.5.

**A1749.** B. 625
<!-- id: methods-8-3-10 -->

Explanation: √(0.25/n) = 0.02 gives 0.25/n = 0.0004, so n = 625.

**A1750.** C. 0.0228
<!-- id: methods-8-3-11 -->

Explanation: sd = √(0.16/64) = 0.05, so z = (0.1 − 0.2)/0.05 = −2 and P(Z < −2) ≈ 0.0228.

**A1751.** D. 0.3125
<!-- id: methods-8-3-12 -->

Explanation: P(X ≥ 3) for X ~ Bi(4, 0.5) = (4 + 1)/16 = 5/16 = 0.3125.

**A1752.** A. 0.0042
<!-- id: methods-8-3-13 -->

Explanation: Var(P̂) = p(1 − p)/n = 0.21/50 = 0.0042. Note that 0.0648 is the standard deviation.

**A1753.** B. X/n
<!-- id: methods-8-3-14 -->

Explanation: P̂ = X/n is the proportion of the sample with the attribute.

**A1754.** C. The spread of the p̂ values would decrease if each sample had size 200
<!-- id: methods-8-3-15 -->

Explanation: The spread depends on the sample size n, not on how many samples are simulated. P̂ is discrete, so it is only approximately normal.

**A1755.** D. Using a random number generator to select students from the complete enrolment list
<!-- id: methods-8-3-16 -->

Explanation: Only the random selection from the full population list gives every student an equal chance of being chosen.

**A1756.** A. 0.7887
<!-- id: methods-8-3-17 -->

Explanation: sd = √(0.24/150) = 0.04, so the z-values are ±1.25 and P(−1.25 < Z < 1.25) ≈ 0.7887.

**A1757.** B. 0.1
<!-- id: methods-8-3-18 -->

Explanation: p(1 − p)/100 = 0.0009 gives p(1 − p) = 0.09, so p = 0.1 or p = 0.9.

**A1758.** P̂ = X/n, where X is the number in the sample with the attribute. E(P̂) = p and sd(P̂) = √(p(1 − p)/n).
<!-- id: methods-8-3-19 -->

Explanation: All three parts are needed.

**A1759.** sd = √(0.15 × 0.85/200) = √0.0006375 ≈ 0.0252.
<!-- id: methods-8-3-20 -->

Explanation: Substitute into √(p(1 − p)/n).

**A1760.** P̂ = 0.4 means X = 2 with X ~ Bi(5, 0.4): C(5,2)(0.4)²(0.6)³ = 10 × 0.16 × 0.216 = 0.3456.
<!-- id: methods-8-3-21 -->

Explanation: Convert the sample proportion to a count, then use the binomial distribution.

**A1761.** np = 1, so the expected number of successes is very small. The distribution of P̂ is strongly right-skewed and discrete, not approximately normal.
<!-- id: methods-8-3-22 -->

Explanation: Mention that np (and n(1 − p)) should be reasonably large, for example at least 10.

**A1762.** sd = √(0.35 × 0.65/400) ≈ 0.02385, so z = 0.03/0.02385 ≈ 1.258 and P ≈ 0.104.
<!-- id: methods-8-3-23 -->

Explanation: Find the sd of P̂, then standardise.

**A1763.** n = 400. The sd is proportional to 1/√n, so halving it requires n to be multiplied by 2² = 4.
<!-- id: methods-8-3-24 -->

Explanation: Show the √n relationship.

**A1764.** P(X ≤ 2) for X ~ Bi(10, 0.5) = (1 + 10 + 45)/1024 = 56/1024 ≈ 0.0547.
<!-- id: methods-8-3-25 -->

Explanation: P̂ ≤ 0.2 is equivalent to X ≤ 2.

#### Approximate confidence intervals for p

**A1765.** C. (0.255, 0.345)
<!-- id: methods-8-4-1 -->

Explanation: M = 1.96√(0.3 × 0.7/400) ≈ 1.96 × 0.0229 ≈ 0.045, which gives 0.3 ± 0.045.

**A1766.** D. 2M
<!-- id: methods-8-4-2 -->

Explanation: The interval runs from p̂ − M to p̂ + M, so its width is 2M.

**A1767.** A. If many such intervals were constructed from random samples, about 95% of them would contain p
<!-- id: methods-8-4-3 -->

Explanation: The confidence level describes the long-run proportion of intervals, built by this method, that capture the fixed parameter p.

**A1768.** B. widen the interval
<!-- id: methods-8-4-4 -->

Explanation: A higher confidence level uses a larger z-value (2.576 instead of 1.645), so the margin of error increases.

**A1769.** C. 1.645
<!-- id: methods-8-4-5 -->

Explanation: For 90% confidence each tail has area 0.05, and P(Z < 1.645) ≈ 0.95.

**A1770.** D. p̂ = 0.50, M = 0.08
<!-- id: methods-8-4-6 -->

Explanation: p̂ is the midpoint (0.42 + 0.58)/2 = 0.50, and M is half the width, 0.16/2 = 0.08.

**A1771.** A. 150
<!-- id: methods-8-4-7 -->

Explanation: 0.08 = 1.96√(0.25/n) gives n = 0.25 × (1.96/0.08)² ≈ 150.

**A1772.** B. 1068
<!-- id: methods-8-4-8 -->

Explanation: n ≥ 0.25 × (1.96/0.03)² ≈ 1067.1. Always round up, so n = 1068.

**A1773.** C. multiplied by 4
<!-- id: methods-8-4-9 -->

Explanation: The width is proportional to 1/√n, so n must increase by a factor of 2² = 4.

**A1774.** D. The survey suggests the claim is not correct because 0.5 lies outside the interval
<!-- id: methods-8-4-10 -->

Explanation: 0.5 is not a plausible value of p at the 95% level, which casts doubt on the claim. A confidence interval never gives certainty.

**A1775.** A. (0.085, 0.215)
<!-- id: methods-8-4-11 -->

Explanation: M = 2.576√(0.15 × 0.85/200) ≈ 2.576 × 0.02525 ≈ 0.065, which gives 0.15 ± 0.065.

**A1776.** B. The size of the population
<!-- id: methods-8-4-12 -->

Explanation: The margin z√(p̂(1 − p̂)/n) depends only on z, p̂ and n.

**A1777.** C. 19
<!-- id: methods-8-4-13 -->

Explanation: 0.95 × 20 = 19.

**A1778.** D. 0.857
<!-- id: methods-8-4-14 -->

Explanation: 0.95³ ≈ 0.857.

**A1779.** A. 100
<!-- id: methods-8-4-15 -->

Explanation: p̂ = 0.3 and M = 0.09, so √(0.21/n) = 0.09/1.96 ≈ 0.0459. Solving gives n ≈ 100.

**A1780.** B. p is fixed, while the endpoints of the interval vary from sample to sample
<!-- id: methods-8-4-16 -->

Explanation: The parameter p is a fixed unknown constant. The interval is random because it is computed from a random sample.

**A1781.** C. (0.279, 0.361)
<!-- id: methods-8-4-17 -->

Explanation: p̂ = 160/500 = 0.32 and M = 1.96√(0.32 × 0.68/500) ≈ 0.041.

**A1782.** D. narrower, with the same centre
<!-- id: methods-8-4-18 -->

Explanation: Both intervals are centred at p̂. The smaller z-value (1.645 rather than 1.96) gives a narrower interval.

**A1783.** p̂ = 0.65 and M = 1.96√(0.65 × 0.35/600) ≈ 0.038, so the interval is (0.612, 0.688).
<!-- id: methods-8-4-19 -->

Explanation: Find p̂ first, then the margin of error.

**A1784.** If many random samples of the same size were taken and an interval constructed from each, about 95% of these intervals would contain the true proportion p. This interval gives plausible values of p from 0.47 to 0.55.
<!-- id: methods-8-4-20 -->

Explanation: Do not say there is a 95% probability that p lies in this particular interval.

**A1785.** M = 1.645√(0.4 × 0.6/250) ≈ 1.645 × 0.03098 ≈ 0.051.
<!-- id: methods-8-4-21 -->

Explanation: Use z ≈ 1.645 for 90%.

**A1786.** n ≥ 0.2 × 0.8 × (1.96/0.04)² = 0.16 × 2401 ≈ 384.2, so n = 385.
<!-- id: methods-8-4-22 -->

Explanation: Always round the sample size up.

**A1787.** p̂ = 0.56 and M = 0.04. Then 0.04 = 1.96√(0.56 × 0.44/n), so n ≈ 0.2464 × (1.96/0.04)² ≈ 592.
<!-- id: methods-8-4-23 -->

Explanation: p̂ is the midpoint and M is half the width.

**A1788.** 0.70 is outside the interval, so the survey gives evidence that the true proportion is less than 70% and does not support the claim.
<!-- id: methods-8-4-24 -->

Explanation: Base the conclusion on whether the claimed value lies inside the interval.

**A1789.** It uses the normal approximation to the distribution of P̂. It also uses p̂ in place of the unknown p when calculating the standard deviation.
<!-- id: methods-8-4-25 -->

Explanation: Both reasons earn full marks.

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**A1790.** A cubic curve and a probability density function
<!-- id: methods-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 14 marks*

**a.** By the product rule, \(f'(x) = (x - 3)^2 + 2x(x - 3) = (x - 3)(3x - 3) = 3(x - 1)(x - 3)\). *(1 mark)*

1. Product rule with \(u = x\) and \(v = (x - 3)^2\): \(f'(x) = (x - 3)^2 + x \times 2(x - 3)\).
2. Take out the common factor \((x - 3)\): \(f'(x) = (x - 3)(x - 3 + 2x) = (x - 3)(3x - 3) = 3(x - 1)(x - 3)\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Writing only the final line. In a 'show that' question the working is the answer, so show the product rule (or the expanded form \(x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x\)) before factorising.

Topic: Chain, product and quotient rules (methods-4-2)

**b.** \((1, 4)\), a local maximum *(2 marks)*

1. \(f'(x) = 0\) gives \(x = 1\) or \(x = 3\). Only \(x = 1\) is in \((0, 3)\), and \(f(1) = 1 \times (-2)^2 = 4\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(f'(0.5) = 3(-0.5)(-2.5) > 0\) and \(f'(2) = 3(1)(-1) < 0\), so the gradient changes from positive to negative and \((1, 4)\) is a local maximum. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Stating 'maximum' with no reason. Support it with a sign test of \(f'\) on either side of \(x = 1\), or with \(f''(1) = -6 < 0\).

Topic: Stationary points and their nature (methods-5-1)

**c.** \(y = -3x + 8\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(f(2) = 2(2 - 3)^2 = 2\) and \(f'(2) = 3(2 - 1)(2 - 3) = -3\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(y - 2 = -3(x - 2)\), so \(y = -3x + 8\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using \(f(2)\) as the gradient. The gradient is \(f'(2)\); \(f(2)\) gives the point that the tangent passes through.

Topic: Tangents, normals and rates of change (methods-4-4)

**d.** \(\frac{27}{4}\) square units *(2 marks)*

1. \(f(x) \ge 0\) on \([0, 3]\) and \(f(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x\), so the area is \(\displaystyle\int_0^3 (x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x)\,dx\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(= \left[\frac{x^4}{4} - 2x^3 + \frac{9x^2}{2}\right]_0^3 = \frac{81}{4} - 54 + \frac{81}{2} = \frac{27}{4}\) square units. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Antidifferentiating \(x(x - 3)^2\) factor by factor, as \(\frac{x^2}{2} \times \frac{(x - 3)^3}{3}\). There is no product rule for antiderivatives, so expand first.

Topic: Area under and between curves (methods-6-3)

**e.i.** The total area under a probability density function is 1. Using part d, \(\frac{27}{4}k = 1\), so \(k = \frac{4}{27}\). *(1 mark)*

1. \(\displaystyle\int_0^3 k\,x(x - 3)^2\,dx = 1\). By part d, \(\displaystyle\int_0^3 x(x - 3)^2\,dx = \frac{27}{4}\), so \(\frac{27}{4}k = 1\) and \(k = \frac{4}{27}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Setting the maximum value of \(g\), which is \(4k\), equal to 1. It is the area under the graph, not its height, that must equal 1.

Topic: Probability density functions (methods-8-1)

**e.ii.** \(\frac{11}{27}\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(\Pr(X < 1) = \displaystyle\frac{4}{27}\int_0^1 (x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x)\,dx = \frac{4}{27}\left[\frac{x^4}{4} - 2x^3 + \frac{9x^2}{2}\right]_0^1\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(= \frac{4}{27}\left(\frac{1}{4} - 2 + \frac{9}{2}\right) = \frac{4}{27} \times \frac{11}{4} = \frac{11}{27}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Leaving out \(k\) and giving \(\frac{11}{4}\). A probability can never be greater than 1.

Topic: Probability density functions (methods-8-1)

**f.** \(\frac{6}{5}\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(\text{E}(X) = \displaystyle\int_0^3 x\,g(x)\,dx = \frac{4}{27}\int_0^3 (x^4 - 6x^3 + 9x^2)\,dx\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(= \frac{4}{27}\left[\frac{x^5}{5} - \frac{3x^4}{2} + 3x^3\right]_0^3 = \frac{4}{27}\left(\frac{243}{5} - \frac{243}{2} + 81\right) = \frac{4}{27} \times \frac{81}{10} = \frac{6}{5}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Integrating \(g(x)\) instead of \(x\,g(x)\). That integral is always 1, the total probability.

Topic: Probability density functions (methods-8-1)

**g.** \(1.157\) *(2 marks)*

1. The median \(m\) satisfies \(\displaystyle\int_0^m g(x)\,dx = \frac{1}{2}\), that is, \(\frac{4}{27}\left(\frac{m^4}{4} - 2m^3 + \frac{9m^2}{2}\right) = \frac{1}{2}\) with \(0 \le m \le 3\). *(1 mark)*
2. Solve with CAS. The quartic has two real solutions, \(m \approx -0.742\) and \(m \approx 1.157\). Only the second lies in \([0, 3]\), so the median is \(1.157\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Reporting a CAS solution that lies outside \([0, 3]\). The median must be in the interval where the density is defined.

Topic: Probability density functions (methods-8-1)

**A1791.** Seedlings in a nursery
<!-- id: methods-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 12 marks*

**a.** 2 cm *(1 mark)*

1. \(h(0) = 20 - 18e^{0} = 20 - 18 = 2\), so the height is 2 cm. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Taking \(e^0\) to be 0. Any non-zero number to the power 0 is 1.

Topic: Exponential and logarithmic graphs (methods-2-1)

**b.** \(1.66\) cm per week *(2 marks)*

1. \(h'(t) = -18 \times (-0.25)e^{-0.25t} = 4.5e^{-0.25t}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(h'(4) = 4.5e^{-1} \approx 1.66\) cm per week. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving the height \(h(4) \approx 13.38\) cm. A rate of change needs the derivative \(h'(t)\).

Topic: Exponential, logarithmic and circular derivatives (methods-4-3)

**c.** \(5.1\) weeks *(2 marks)*

1. Solve \(20 - 18e^{-0.25t} = 15\): \(18e^{-0.25t} = 5\), so \(e^{-0.25t} = \frac{5}{18}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(-0.25t = \log_e\left(\frac{5}{18}\right)\), so \(t = 4\log_e\left(\frac{18}{5}\right) \approx 5.1\) weeks. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Taking logs before the exponential term is on its own, for example writing \(\log_e(20 - 18e^{-0.25t})\) as \(\log_e 20 - \log_e(18e^{-0.25t})\). There is no log law for a difference inside the log.

Topic: Log laws and exponential equations (methods-2-2)

**d.** 20 cm *(1 mark)*

1. As \(t \to \infty\), \(e^{-0.25t} \to 0\), so \(h(t) \to 20\). The model predicts heights approach 20 cm. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Answering 18 cm, the coefficient of the exponential term. That term shrinks to 0, which leaves 20.

Topic: Exponential and logarithmic graphs (methods-2-1)

**e.i.** \(0.3487\) *(1 mark)*

1. \(Y \sim \text{Bi}(10, 0.9)\), so \(\Pr(Y = 10) = 0.9^{10} \approx 0.3487\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calculating \(1 - 0.9^{10}\), which is the probability that at least one seed fails to germinate.

Topic: Binomial distribution (methods-7-3)

**e.ii.** \(0.9298\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(\Pr(Y \ge 8) = \Pr(Y = 8) + \Pr(Y = 9) + \Pr(Y = 10)\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(= \binom{10}{8}(0.9)^8(0.1)^2 + \binom{10}{9}(0.9)^9(0.1) + 0.9^{10} \approx 0.19371 + 0.38742 + 0.34868 \approx 0.9298\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Leaving out \(\Pr(Y = 8)\), which gives \(\Pr(Y > 8)\). 'At least 8' includes 8.

Topic: Binomial distribution (methods-7-3)

**f.** \(0.0304\) *(1 mark)*

1. Let \(H \sim \text{N}(18.5, 0.8^2)\). With CAS, \(\Pr(H > 20) = \Pr(Z > 1.875) \approx 0.0304\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Entering the variance, \(0.64\), where the calculator asks for the standard deviation, \(0.8\).

Topic: Normal distribution and standardisation (methods-8-2)

**g.** \((0.823, 0.917)\) *(2 marks)*

1. The sample proportion is \(\hat{p} = \frac{174}{200} = 0.87\).
2. \(\hat{p} \pm 1.96\sqrt{\dfrac{\hat{p}(1 - \hat{p})}{n}} = 0.87 \pm 1.96\sqrt{\dfrac{0.87 \times 0.13}{200}} \approx 0.87 \pm 0.0466\). *(1 mark)*
3. So the interval is approximately \((0.823, 0.917)\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using \(n = 174\) in the standard error. Here \(n\) is the sample size, 200.

Topic: Approximate confidence intervals for p (methods-8-4)

**A1792.** A sine curve and a probability
<!-- id: methods-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** \(x = \frac{\pi}{12}\) or \(x = \frac{5\pi}{12}\) *(2 marks)*

1. Let \(\theta = 2x\), so \(\theta \in [0, 2\pi]\) and \(\sin\theta = \frac{1}{2}\). Then \(\theta = \frac{\pi}{6}\) or \(\theta = \pi - \frac{\pi}{6} = \frac{5\pi}{6}\). *(1 mark)*
2. Halve each solution: \(x = \frac{\pi}{12}\) or \(x = \frac{5\pi}{12}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Forgetting to halve, or giving only the first-quadrant solution. Sine is also positive in the second quadrant, so \(\theta = \pi - \frac{\pi}{6}\) is a solution too.

Topic: Exact values and general solutions (methods-2-4)

**b.** \(1\) *(1 mark)*

1. \(f'(x) = 2\cos(2x)\), so \(f'\left(\frac{\pi}{6}\right) = 2\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = 2 \times \frac{1}{2} = 1\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Writing \(f'(x) = \cos(2x)\). By the chain rule, the derivative of \(\sin(2x)\) is \(2\cos(2x)\).

Topic: Exponential, logarithmic and circular derivatives (methods-4-3)

**c.** 1 square unit *(2 marks)*

1. \(f(x) \ge 0\) on \(\left[0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]\), so the area is \(\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi/2} \sin(2x)\,dx = \left[-\frac{1}{2}\cos(2x)\right]_0^{\pi/2}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(= -\frac{1}{2}\cos(\pi) + \frac{1}{2}\cos(0) = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2} = 1\) square unit. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Antidifferentiating \(\sin(2x)\) as \(-2\cos(2x)\). Divide by the 2 instead of multiplying: the antiderivative is \(-\frac{1}{2}\cos(2x)\).

Topic: Area under and between curves (methods-6-3)

**d.i.** The total area under a probability density function is 1. By part c, \(\int_0^{\pi/2} \sin(2x)\,dx = 1\), so \(k \times 1 = 1\) and \(k = 1\). *(1 mark)*

1. \(\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi/2} k\sin(2x)\,dx = 1\). By part c the integral of \(\sin(2x)\) over this interval is 1, so \(k = 1\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Setting the maximum value of \(g\) equal to 1. That happens to give \(k = 1\) here as well, but the reason is that the area under \(g\) must be 1.

Topic: Probability density functions (methods-8-1)

**d.ii.** \(\frac{1}{4}\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(\Pr\left(X < \frac{\pi}{6}\right) = \displaystyle\int_0^{\pi/6} \sin(2x)\,dx = \left[-\frac{1}{2}\cos(2x)\right]_0^{\pi/6}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(= -\frac{1}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) + \frac{1}{2}\cos(0) = -\frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{4}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Evaluating \(\cos\) at \(\frac{\pi}{6}\) instead of at \(2 \times \frac{\pi}{6} = \frac{\pi}{3}\).

Topic: Probability density functions (methods-8-1)

**d.iii.** \(\frac{1}{2}\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(\Pr\left(X < \frac{\pi}{4}\right) = \left[-\frac{1}{2}\cos(2x)\right]_0^{\pi/4} = -\frac{1}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{2}\). *(1 mark)*
2. The event \(X < \frac{\pi}{6}\) lies inside \(X < \frac{\pi}{4}\), so the probability is \(\dfrac{\Pr\left(X < \frac{\pi}{6}\right)}{\Pr\left(X < \frac{\pi}{4}\right)} = \dfrac{1/4}{1/2} = \frac{1}{2}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Multiplying the two probabilities as if the events were independent. For a conditional probability, divide the probability of both events by the probability of the given event.

Topic: Conditional probability and independence (methods-7-4)

**A1793.** A cooling cup of coffee
<!-- id: methods-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 12 marks*

**a.** 90 °C *(1 mark)*

1. \(T(0) = 20 + 70e^{0} = 90\) °C. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Writing 70 °C. The coffee starts at \(20 + 70\), not 70.

Topic: Exponential and logarithmic graphs (methods-2-1)

**b.** \(-4.25\) °C per minute *(2 marks)*

1. \(T'(t) = 70 \times (-0.1)e^{-0.1t} = -7e^{-0.1t}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(T'(5) = -7e^{-0.5} \approx -4.25\) °C per minute (the coffee is cooling). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Dropping the negative sign. A falling temperature has a negative rate of change.

Topic: Exponential, logarithmic and circular derivatives (methods-4-3)

**c.** 5.60 minutes *(2 marks)*

1. \(20 + 70e^{-0.1t} = 60\), so \(e^{-0.1t} = \frac{4}{7}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(t = -10\log_e\left(\frac{4}{7}\right) = 10\log_e\left(\frac{7}{4}\right) \approx 5.60\) minutes. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Solving \(70e^{-0.1t} = 60\) and forgetting the room temperature of 20.

Topic: Log laws and exponential equations (methods-2-2)

**d.** 64.25 °C *(2 marks)*

1. Average value \(= \dfrac{1}{10}\displaystyle\int_0^{10} \left(20 + 70e^{-0.1t}\right)dt = \dfrac{1}{10}\Big[20t - 700e^{-0.1t}\Big]_0^{10}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(= \dfrac{1}{10}\left(200 - 700e^{-1} + 700\right) = 90 - 70e^{-1} \approx 64.25\) °C. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Averaging the starting and finishing temperatures. That is only correct for linear change; use the average value of the function.

Topic: Trapezium approximation and average value (methods-6-4)

**e.i.** \(0.1056\) *(1 mark)*

1. \(\Pr(T < 70) = \Pr(Z < -1.25) \approx 0.1056\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using the variance (16) instead of the standard deviation (4) in the calculator.

Topic: Normal distribution and standardisation (methods-8-2)

**e.ii.** \(c \approx 69.87\) *(2 marks)*

1. \(\Pr(T > c) = 0.9\) means \(\Pr(T < c) = 0.1\). *(1 mark)*
2. With inverse normal, \(c = 75 + 4 \times (-1.2816) \approx 69.87\) °C. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using 0.9 in the inverse normal, which gives the temperature that 90% are below.

Topic: Normal distribution and standardisation (methods-8-2)

**f.** \(0.0798\) *(2 marks)*

1. Let \(X\) be the number below 70 °C; \(X \sim \text{Bi}(5, 0.10565)\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(\Pr(X = 2) = \binom{5}{2}(0.10565)^2(0.89435)^3 \approx 0.0798\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Rounding the probability from part e.i to 0.1 before using it, which changes the fourth decimal place.

Topic: Binomial distribution (methods-7-3)

**A1794.** A logarithm and its inverse
<!-- id: methods-x5 -->
*Multi-part question · 9 marks*

**a.** \(f^{-1}(x) = e^{x/2} - 1\), with domain \(\mathbb{R}\) *(2 marks)*

1. Swap \(x\) and \(y\): \(x = 2\log_e(y + 1)\), so \(y + 1 = e^{x/2}\) and \(f^{-1}(x) = e^{x/2} - 1\). *(1 mark)*
2. The domain of \(f^{-1}\) is the range of \(f\), which is \(\mathbb{R}\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving the domain of \(f\), \((-1, \infty)\). The inverse's domain is the original function's range.

Topic: Inverse functions (methods-3-2)

**b.** \(1\) *(1 mark)*

1. \(f'(x) = \dfrac{2}{x + 1}\), so \(f'(1) = \dfrac{2}{2} = 1\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Writing \(f'(x) = \dfrac{1}{x + 1}\) and forgetting the coefficient 2.

Topic: Exponential, logarithmic and circular derivatives (methods-4-3)

**c.** \(y = 2x\) *(1 mark)*

1. \(f(0) = 0\) and \(f'(0) = 2\), so the tangent is \(y = 2x\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using \(f'(1)\) from part b. The tangent at the origin needs the gradient at \(x = 0\).

Topic: Tangents, normals and rates of change (methods-4-4)

**d.** \(x = e - 1\) *(1 mark)*

1. \(2\log_e(x + 1) = 2\), so \(\log_e(x + 1) = 1\), \(x + 1 = e\) and \(x = e - 1\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Writing \(x + 1 = 1\). If \(\log_e(x + 1) = 1\), then \(x + 1 = e^1\).

Topic: Log laws and exponential equations (methods-2-2)

**e.** \(2e - 4\) square units *(2 marks)*

1. \(f^{-1}(0) = 0\) and \(f^{-1}(x) \ge 0\) for \(x \ge 0\), so the area is \(\displaystyle\int_0^2 \left(e^{x/2} - 1\right)dx = \Big[2e^{x/2} - x\Big]_0^2\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(= (2e - 2) - (2 - 0) = 2e - 4\) square units. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Antidifferentiating \(e^{x/2}\) as \(\frac{1}{2}e^{x/2}\). Dividing by the coefficient \(\frac{1}{2}\) gives \(2e^{x/2}\).

Topic: Area under and between curves (methods-6-3)

**f.** \(y = 2\log_e\left(\dfrac{x + 1}{2}\right)\) *(2 marks)*

1. Dilation by factor 2 from the \(y\)-axis replaces \(x\) with \(\frac{x}{2}\): \(y = 2\log_e\left(\frac{x}{2} + 1\right)\). *(1 mark)*
2. Translation 1 unit right replaces \(x\) with \(x - 1\): \(y = 2\log_e\left(\frac{x - 1}{2} + 1\right) = 2\log_e\left(\frac{x + 1}{2}\right)\). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Replacing \(x\) with \(2x\) for a dilation by factor 2 from the \(y\)-axis. Dilating by factor \(k\) replaces \(x\) with \(\frac{x}{k}\).

Topic: Transformation mapping notation (methods-1-4)
